Restrictions of Maass forms on SL ⁡ ( 2 , ℂ ) \mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C}) to hyperbolic surfaces and geodesic tubes
Jiaqi Hou

Abstract
. Let ψ \psi be an L 2 L^{2} -normalized Hecke-Maass form with a large spectral parameter λ > 0 \lambda>0 on a compact arithmetic congruence hyperbolic 3-manifold X = Γ \ SL ⁡ ( 2 , ℂ ) / SU ⁡ ( 2 ) X=\Gamma\backslash\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})/\mathrm{SU}(2) , and let Y Y be a totally geodesic surface in X X with bounded diameter. The local L 2 L^{2} -bound for the restriction of ψ \psi to Y Y is ‖ ψ | Y ‖ L 2 ​ ( Y ) ≪ λ 1 / 4 \|\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\ll\lambda^{1/4} by Burq, Gérard, and Tzvetkov. We apply the method of arithmetic amplification developed by Iwaniec and Sarnak to obtain a power saving over the local bound. The new feature in the proof is that we establish two different estimates for the integrals of ψ | Y \psi|_{Y} against geodesic beams over Y Y via two amplification arguments. Combining these estimates, we can improve the local bound for generalized Fourier coefficients of ψ | Y \psi|_{Y} against eigenfunctions on Y Y with spectral parameters near λ \lambda . We also apply the amplification method to obtain a power saving over the trivial bound O ⁡ ( 1 ) O(1) for L 2 L^{2} -norms of ψ \psi restricted to λ − 1 / 2 \lambda^{-1/2} -neighborhoods of unit-length geodesic segments. Consequently, by applying a result of Blair and Sogge, we obtain power savings over the local L p L^{p} -bounds of ψ \psi by Sogge for 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 from our improved bound for the Kakeya-Nikodym norm.



1.
Introduction

Let
M
be a compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
, and let
\Delta
be the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M
.
If
\psi
is a Laplace eigenfunction on
M

satisfying
\Delta\psi+\lambda^{2}\psi=0
with
\lambda>0
.
It is an interesting problem to study the concentration of
\psi
as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
.
We assume that
\|\psi\|_{2}=1
.
A classical result on
\|\psi\|_{p}
is due to Sogge
[
32
]
(see also Avakumović
[
2
]
and Levitan
[
27
]
when
p=\infty
),
and is

(1.1)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{p}\ll\lambda^{\delta(p,n)},

where
\delta(p,n)
is given by

\delta(p,n)=\begin{cases}\frac{n-1}{4}-\frac{n-1}{2p}\;&\text{ for }\;2\leq p\leq\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1},\\
\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{n}{p}\;\;\;\;&\text{ for }\;\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}\leq p\leq\infty.\end{cases}

The notation
A\ll B
means that there is a positive constant
C
such that
|A|\leq CB
. (See Section
2
for the notation in this paper.)
When
M
is the round sphere, the estimates (
1.1
) are saturated by the zonal spherical harmonics for
p\geq\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
and by the highest weight spherical harmonics for
2<p\leq\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
.

Nevertheless, it is expected that (
1.1
) can be improved under extra geometric assumptions on
M
.
For instance, there are log improvements if
M
has nonpositive sectional curvature.
We assume the manifold
M
satisfies the nonpositive curvature condition in this paragraph.
Bérard
[
3
]
obtained a
\log\lambda
improvement for the error term in the local Weyl law, which implies that

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{\infty}\ll\frac{\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{\sqrt{\log\lambda}}.

Hassell and Tacy
[
19
]
obtained further improvements by showing that

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{p}\ll\frac{\lambda^{\delta(p,n)}}{\sqrt{\log\lambda}}=\frac{\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{n}{p}}}{\sqrt{\log\lambda}},\quad\text{ if }p>\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1},

where the implied constant blows up as
p
goes to
\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
.
For
2<p<\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
, improvement involving powers of
\log\lambda
were proved by Blair and Sogge
[
6
]
, where the powers depend on
n
and
p
. Blair and Sogge obtained the results by proving improved bounds for Kakeya-Nikodym norms which measure the
L^{2}
-concentration of eigenfunctions on
\lambda^{-1/2}
-tubes of unit length geodesics. We will discuss the Kakeya-Nikodym norms and their improved bounds in more detail in Section
1.2
. In
[
7
]
, Blair and Sogge established a gain of an inverse power of
\log\lambda
at the critical exponent
p=\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
.

The
L^{\infty}
-norm problems can also be studied using arithmetic techniques when the manifold
M
and the eigenfunction
\psi
are equipped with additional arithmetic structures.
We let
M
be a compact congruence arithmetic hyperbolic surface arising from an indefinite quaternion division algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\psi
be a Hecke-Maass form which is a joint eigenfunction of the Laplace-Beltrami operator and the unramified Hecke operators on
M
. Iwaniec and Sarnak
[
25
]
showed that the bound
\|\psi\|_{\infty}\ll\lambda^{1/2}
given by (
1.1
) can be strengthened by a power to
\|\psi\|_{\infty}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{5/12+\epsilon}.

Their proof uses the technique known as arithmetic amplification. This approach has since been used by many authors to bound
L^{\infty}
-norms of Hecke-Maass forms on other groups. For instance, see
[
9
,
10
]
. The main results in this paper also rely on the amplification method.

We are also interested in studying the concentration of
\psi
along submanifolds.
The standard
L^{p}
-restriction estimates were established by Burq, Gérard and Tzvetkov
[
13
]
and Hu
[
23
]
.
We assume that
\Sigma\subset M
is a closed submanifold and
\dim\Sigma=k
.
If
k=n-1
, they showed that

(1.2)

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}\ll\lambda^{\rho_{n-1}(p,n)},

where
\rho_{n-1}(p,n)
is given by

\rho_{n-1}(p,n)=\begin{cases}\frac{n-1}{4}-\frac{n-2}{2p}\quad&\text{ if }2\leq p\leq\frac{2n}{n-1},\\
\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{n-1}{p}\quad&\text{ if }\frac{2n}{n-1}\leq p\leq\infty.\end{cases}

If
n\geq 3
and
k=n-2
, then we have

(1.3)

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\ll\lambda^{1/2}\sqrt{\log\lambda},

and

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}\ll\lambda^{\rho_{n-2}(p,n)},\quad\text{ where }\rho_{n-2}(p,n)=\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{n-2}{p},\quad 2<p\leq\infty.

If
n\geq 4
and
k\leq n-3
, then

(1.5)

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}\ll\lambda^{\rho_{k}(p,n)},\quad\text{ where }\rho_{k}(p,n)=\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{k}{p},\quad 2\leq p\leq\infty.

By Chen and Sogge
[
14
, Theorem 1.1]
and Wang and Zhang
[
35
, Theorem 2]
, the
\sqrt{\log\lambda}
loss in the endpoint estimates (
1.3
) can be removed provided that
\Sigma
is a totally geodesic submanifold of codimension 2.
In
[
13
, §6.2]
, Burq, Gérard and Tzvetkov gave examples to show that the estimates (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) are sharp, except for the
\sqrt{\log\lambda}
loss in (
1.3
).
If
M
is the round sphere and
\Sigma
is any submanifold containing a pole of the sphere, the estimates (
1.3
)–(
1.5
), and (
1.2
) for
\frac{2n}{n-1}\leq p\leq\infty
are saturated by the zonal spherical harmonics.
If
M
is the round sphere and
\Sigma
contains a geodesic, then (
1.2
), with
2\leq p\leq\frac{2n}{n-1}
, are saturated by the highest weight spherical harmonics.
The estimates (
1.2
) can be improved if
\Sigma
is a hypersurface and has curvature. Indeed
[
23
, Theorem 1.4]
showed that if
k=n-1
and the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
is (positive or negative) definite, then, for
2\leq p\leq\frac{2n}{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\Sigma}\|_{L^{p}(\Sigma)}\ll\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{3}-\frac{2n-3}{3p}}.

Like the
L^{p}
-norm problems, we expect that the estimates (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) may be strengthened under curvature assumptions on
M
. If
M
has nonpositive curvature, Chen
[
15
]
proved a
(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}
gain for
p>\frac{2n}{n-1}
if
k=n-1
and for
p>2
if
k\leq n-2
.
We let
\Pi
denote the space of all unit-length geodesic segments on
M
.
There has been considerable work towards improving (
1.2
) for geodesic restrictions on surfaces with nonpositive curvature.
Assume that
M
is a surface with nonpositive curvature.
Xi and Zhang
[
36
]
and Blair
[
8
]
dealt with the endpoint
p=4
and proved

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in\Pi}\|\psi|_{\gamma}\|_{L^{4}(\gamma)}\ll\lambda^{1/4}(\log\lambda)^{-1/4}.

Blair and Sogge
[
6
]
obtained a
(\log\lambda)^{-1/4}
gain for
p=2
.
For geodesic restrictions on hyperbolic 3-manifolds, Zhang
[
37
]
improved the endpoint estimate (
1.3
). If we let
M
be a hyperbolic 3-manifold, Zhang obtained a log improvement:

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in\Pi}\|\psi|_{\gamma}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\ll\lambda^{1/2}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}.

Now we let
M
be a compact congruence arithmetic hyperbolic surface and let
\psi
be an
L^{2}
-normalized Hecke-Maass form. By extending the technique of arithmetic amplification developed by Iwaniec and Sarnak
[
25
]
, Marshall
[
29
, Theorem 1.1]
obtained a power saving over the bound (
1.2
) for geodesic restrictions and
p=2
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in\Pi}\|\psi|_{\gamma}\|_{L^{2}(\gamma)}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{3/14+\epsilon}.

Combining the above estimate with the main theorem of
[
4
]
, Marshall
[
29
, Corollary 1.2]
also obtained an improvement over the local bound
\|\psi\|_{4}\ll\lambda^{1/8}
by Sogge (
1.1
):

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{4}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{1/8-1/56+\epsilon}.

See also Humphries and Khan
[
24
]
and Ki
[
26
]
on
L^{4}
-norms of Hecke-Maass cusp forms on the modular surface
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
via different methods.
Using Waldspurger’s formula and
L
-functions, Ali
[
1
]
proved a stronger
L^{2}
-bound for a Hecke-Maass cusp form on the modular surface
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})\backslash\mathbb{H}^{2}
restricted to closed geodesics associated to a fundamental discriminant
D>0
.

Marshall extended Sogge’s bound (
1.1
) and the estimates (
1.2
)–(
1.5
) by Burq, Gérard and Tzvetkov to higher rank cases in
[
30
]
. He proved almost sharp local bounds for the
L^{p}
-norms for eigenfunctions of the full ring of invariant differential operators on a compact locally symmetric space, as well as their restrictions to maximal flat subspaces.
Marshall
[
28
]
also applied the amplification method to obtain a power saving for the
L^{2}
-norms of the maximal flat restrictions for Maass forms on
\operatorname{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.

We want to extend Marshall’s results
[
29
]
about geodesic restrictions on hyperbolic surfaces to restriction problems of eigenfunctions on higher dimensional manifolds. In this paper, we consider two restriction problems for eigenfunctions on hyperbolic 3-manifolds.

1.1.
Hyperbolic surface restrictions

One generalization is to estimate the
L^{2}
-norms for hypersurface restrictions.
We now let
X=\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{H}^{3}
,
where
\mathbb{H}^{3}=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})/\operatorname{SU}(2)
is the hyperbolic 3-space,
and
\Gamma\subset\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is a cocompact arithmetic congruence lattice arising from a quaternion division algebra over a number field with one complex place.
Let
\psi
be a Hecke–Maass form on
X
, that is, an eigenfunction of the Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta
and the unramified Hecke operators.
We assume that
\|\psi\|_{2}=1
.
In this context, it is more natural to let
\lambda
be the spectral parameter of
\psi
, which satisfies
\Delta\psi+(\lambda^{2}+1)\psi=0
.
As we are considering large eigenvalue asymptotics, we will assume that
\lambda>0
.
The submanifold we will consider in this paper is a totally geodesic surface
Y
in
X
.

We put the following boundedness assumption on the submanifold
Y
to state a uniform result.
We suppose that
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow X
is the covering map,
and let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
be an open ball of radius
1
under the hyperbolic metric.
Let
\iota:\mathbb{H}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{3}
be any embedding of the hyperbolic plane.
We assume that

(1.6)

\displaystyle Y=\pi\circ\iota(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}).

Recall that the estimate (
1.2
) for the
L^{2}
-norm of
\psi|_{Y}
gives
\|\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\ll\lambda^{1/4}
. We improve it as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\psi
be an
L^{2}
-normalized Hecke–Maass form on
X
with spectral parameter
\lambda>0
, and let
Y
be a totally geodesic surface in
X
satisfying (
1.6
).
For any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\ll\lambda^{1/4-1/1220+\epsilon},

where the implied constant depends only on
X
and
\epsilon
.

Theorem
1.1
is one of the main results in this paper. The proof occupies Sections
4
–
9
and we will give the outline of the proof in Section
1.3
.

1.2.
Kakeya–Nikodym norms

As before, we let
M
be a compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
, and let
\psi
be an
L^{2}
-normalized Laplace eigenfunction on
M
with frequency
\lambda>0
.
As we mentioned before, the highest weight spherical harmonics on the round spheres saturate Sogge’s
L^{p}
-norm estimates (
1.1
) for
2<p\leq\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
. They have significant
L^{2}
-mass on tubes of width
\lambda^{-1/2}
about unit geodesics on the equator.

Let
\Pi
denote the space of unit length geodesic segments in
M
.
If
\gamma\in\Pi
, we denote the
\lambda^{-1/2}
-neighborhood of
\gamma
in
M
by
\mathscr{T}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}(\gamma)
.
Following
[
5
]
, we define the Kakeya-Nikodym norm without averages of
\psi
to be

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}=\sup_{\gamma\in\Pi}\|\psi|_{\mathscr{T}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}(\gamma)}\|_{2}=\left(\sup_{\gamma\in\Pi}\int_{\mathscr{T}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}(\gamma)}|\psi(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}.

These norms were introduced by Sogge
[
33
]
. Since we are assuming the eigenfunctions to be
L^{2}
-normalized, we always have the trivial upper bound

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}\leq 1.

The highest weight spherical harmonics on the round spheres also saturate the trivial bound
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}\ll 1
. We mention that when
n=2
or 3, it may be seen that (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) (after removing the log loss) imply the trivial bound
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}\ll 1
.
Nevertheless, in higher dimensions
n>3
, the geodesic restriction estimates given by (
1.5
) are too singular to detect concentrations of eigenfunctions near geodesics. In fact, in these dimensions, estimates (
1.5
) are saturated by the zonal spherical harmonics rather than the highest weight spherical harmonics on the round spheres.

Blair and Sogge
[
5
]
showed that improvements on the trivial bound for
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}
will give improvements on the
L^{p}
-bounds (
1.1
) for small exponents
p
.
Indeed they proved that

(1.7)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{p}\ll\lambda^{\delta(p,n)}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}^{\frac{2(n+1)}{p(n-1)}-1},\quad\text{ if }\;\frac{2(n+2)}{n}<p<\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}.

See also
[
11
,
33
,
4
]
for the related results in dimension two.
By using Toponogov’s comparison theorem from Riemannian geometry, Blair and Sogge
[
6
]
obtained log improvements for the Kakeya–Nikodym norms when
M
has nonpositive curvature. Using these and (
1.7
), they were able to obtain log improvements over Sogge’s
L^{p}
-bounds (
1.1
) for such manifolds when
2<p<\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
.

Now assume that
\psi
is an
L^{2}
-normalized Hecke-Maass form, with spectral parameter
\lambda>0
, on a compact arithmetic congruence hyperbolic 3-manifold
X
as before.
We prove the following powering saving over the trivial bound.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\psi
be an
L^{2}
-normalized Hecke–Maass form on
X
with spectral parameter
\lambda>0
.
For any
\epsilon>0
and any geodesic segment
\gamma
of unit length, we have

\displaystyle\|\psi|_{\mathscr{T}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}(\gamma)}\|_{2}\ll\lambda^{-1/20+\epsilon},

where the implied constant depends only on
X
and
\epsilon
. Therefore,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\psi\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{KN}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{-1/20+\epsilon}
.

We will prove this result in Section
10
.
By combining Theorem
1.2
with (
1.7
), we obtain the following improvements over (
1.1
).

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
\psi
be an
L^{2}
-normalized Hecke–Maass form on
X
with spectral parameter
\lambda>0
. We have

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{p}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{\delta(p,3)-\frac{1}{20}(\frac{4}{p}-1)+\epsilon}

if
10/3<p<4
.

Moreover, we can interpolate the above bounds with
\|\psi\|_{2}=1
to obtain power savings over the
L^{p}
-bounds (
1.1
) for
X
with
2<p<4
. If we apply the recent work
[
18
]
by Gao, Wu and Xi, the estimates in Corollary
1.3
can be extended to
22/7\leq p<4
.

1.3.
Outline of the proof for Theorem
1.1

If we fix a real valued function
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, it suffices to estimate the
L^{2}
-norm of
(\chi\psi|_{Y})(z):=\chi(z)\psi(g_{0}z)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, uniformly for
g_{0}
in a compact subset of
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.
We shall do this by estimating the operator norm for

\displaystyle\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow\langle\chi\psi,\phi\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(z)\psi(g_{0}z)\overline{\phi(z)}dz

for
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
. The proof will use Harish-Chandra transforms and Helgason transforms on hyperbolic spaces. We give their definitions and properties in Section
3
. Given
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, we denote its Helgason transform by
\widetilde{\phi}
, which is a function on
\mathbb{R}\times B
. Here
B=\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
is the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

To prove such an estimate, we construct an
\operatorname{SU}(2)
-bi-invariant test function
k_{\lambda}
via the inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
\mathbb{H}^{3}
of an even real-variable function
h_{\lambda}
that is non-negative on the spectral parameters and concentrates near
\pm\lambda
. We sum the test function over the arithmetic lattice to form the kernel function

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}k_{\lambda}(x^{-1}\gamma y)

on the arithmetic hyperbolic
3
-manifold
X=\Gamma\backslash\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then the integral operator on
L^{2}(X)
with kernel
K(x,y)
is an approximate spectral projector onto eigenfunctions with spectral parameter
\lambda
.
By the spectral expansion for
K
, we obtain the following pretrace formula

(1.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\psi_{i}(x)\overline{\psi_{i}(y)}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}k_{\lambda}(x^{-1}\gamma y),

where
\psi_{i}
’s are Hecke-Maass forms on
X
with spectral parameters
\lambda_{i}
, which form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(X)
and
\psi
is one of them.

Let us first outline how to get Burq, Gérard and Tzvetkov’s local bound
\|\chi\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\lambda^{1/4}
by integrating the pretrace formula.
Let
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space for
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Given
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, if we integrate (
1.8
) against
\overline{\chi\phi}\times\chi\phi
along
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}\times g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we obtain

(1.9)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})|\langle\chi\psi_{i},\phi\rangle|^{2}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi\phi(x_{1})}\chi\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{1}^{-1}g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}x_{2})dz_{1}dz_{2}.

By dropping all terms on the spectral side, i.e. the left-hand side of (
1.9
), except the term
|\langle\chi\psi,\phi\rangle|^{2}
, it suffices to bound the integrals

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\phi,g)=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi\phi(x_{1})}\chi\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{1}^{-1}gx_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}\quad\text{ for }g\in\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C}).

We may assume that
\Gamma
is torsion-free and the supports of
\chi
and
k_{\lambda}
are sufficiently small so that only
I(\lambda,\phi,e)
contributes to the geometric side (the right-hand side of (
1.9
)). Then we obtain that (see Section
4.3
)

(1.10)

\displaystyle\langle\chi\psi,\phi\rangle\ll|I(\lambda,\phi,e)|^{1/2}\ll\left(\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|\right)^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Here
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)
is the Harish-Chandra transform of
k_{\lambda}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Then the local bound
\|\chi\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\lambda^{1/4}
follows from the estimate
\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|\ll\lambda^{1/2}
(See (
5.8
) in Proposition
5.4
).

To improve the local bound, the first main step in our proof is to refine the pointwise bound for
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)
.
Given any
0<\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 10^{-6}
and
\beta>0
satisfying

\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
, Proposition
5.4
shows that if
s\notin\pm[\lambda-\beta/2,\lambda+\beta/2]
then

(1.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}.

The proof of (
1.11
) occupies Section
5
.
We define
{H}_{\beta}
to be the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
consisting of functions with Helgason transforms supported in
\pm[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]\times B
.
Let
H_{\beta}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement of
H_{\beta}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
and let
\Pi_{\beta}
be the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
onto
H_{\beta}
.
Given
\phi\in H_{\beta}^{\perp}
, by a similar analysis like (
1.10
), (
1.11
) implies the following bound

\displaystyle\langle\chi\psi,\phi\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/4}\beta^{-1/4}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Therefore,

(1.12)

\displaystyle\|(1-\Pi_{\beta})(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/4}\beta^{-1/4}.

Here we obtain a gain of
\beta^{-1/4}
by removing the part of
\chi\psi|_{Y}
with frequencies near
\pm\lambda
.
Compared to the local bound, it may be seen that the main contribution to
\|\chi\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
should come from those frequencies near
\pm\lambda
.
We mention that the estimate (
1.12
) is purely local and does not use the arithmeticity of
\Gamma
or
\psi
.
However, it does require asymptotics for the spherical functions on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which are taken from
[
30
]
.

The second main step is to bound the
L^{2}
-norm of
\Pi_{\beta}(\chi\psi|_{Y})\in H_{\beta}
.
We want to get a power saving over the local bound
\|\Pi_{\beta}(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\lambda^{1/4}
, via the method of arithmetic amplification.
First, we will give an outline of the amplification method.
We let
\mathcal{T}
be a Hecke operator and apply
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{*}
to
K(x,y)
. The identity corresponding to (
1.9
) is now

(1.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{*}\Gamma}C(\gamma)I(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})&=\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi_{i},\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\geq\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2},\end{split}

where
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{*}\Gamma
is the set of isometries appearing in
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{*}
, and
C(\gamma)
is the coefficient of
\gamma\in\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{*}\Gamma
.
Here
\phi
can be any Harish-Chandra Schwartz function on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
We choose
\mathcal{T}
so that its eigenvalue on
\psi
is large, and it should have small eigenvalues on the remaining
\psi_{i}
by the orthogonality of systems of Hecke eigenvalues.
The term “amplification” comes from this way in which
\mathcal{T}
picks out
\psi
from the collection
\{\psi_{i}\}
.

To apply the inequality (
1.13
), besides the counting problem, the amplification proof in the previous relevant results
[
28
,
29
]
suggests that we should prove the following two estimates.
The first expected estimate is

(1.14)

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\phi,g)\ll\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

for
general
g\in\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
in a fixed compact set, which corresponds to the local bound. We define
T_{\lambda,g}
to be the oscillatory integral operator defined by

\displaystyle[T_{\lambda,g}\phi](y)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(x)k_{\lambda}(x^{-1}gy)\phi(x)dx,\quad y\in\mathbb{H}^{2}.

Then (
1.14
) will follow from the
L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}
operator norm
\|T_{\lambda,g}\|_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}\ll\lambda^{1/2}
, which we expect to hold.
If we make further assumptions on the group element
g
, for example, assuming that
g
is away from the stabilizer of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, then we need to get a better estimate than the estimate (
1.14
), which must involve a power saving.
However, this second estimate should fail for general
\phi
if
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}\neq\varnothing
.
In the following, we briefly explain the sharpness of (
1.14
) by assuming
g=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}e^{i\theta}&\\
&e^{-i\theta}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq\pm I
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
l
be the vertical geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
through the origin, which is the intersection
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}
. If we take
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
so that
\widetilde{\phi}(s,b)
is a bump function localized near
\lambda
of width 1 in the
s
-variable and is a bump function localized near the origin of
B
of width
\lambda^{-1/2}
in the
b
-variable, then
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\asymp\lambda^{1/4}
.
Applying the inverse Helgason transform for
\phi
and the inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
k_{\lambda}
, it may be seen that
I(\lambda,\phi,g)
can be written as an oscillatory integral

\displaystyle\asymp(\lambda^{1/2})^{2}\lambda^{2}\int_{M\backslash\mathrm{SU}(2)}\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{1}e^{i\lambda F(z_{1},z_{2},k)}dz_{1}dz_{2}dk.

Here
M
is the digonal subgroup of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
,
(\lambda^{1/2})^{2}
is from the integrals for
\widetilde{\phi}
,
\lambda^{2}
is from the Plancherel density for
\mathbb{H}^{3}
,
\chi_{1}
is a compactly supported smooth amplitude factor. The phase function
F(z_{1},z_{2},k)
is equal to

\displaystyle-A(z_{1})+A(z_{2})-A(kz_{1}^{-1}gz_{2})+O(\lambda^{-1})

where
A
is the Iwasawa projection (see Section
2
).
The set of the critical points of
F
, if ignoring the error term, is 2-dimensional

\displaystyle\{(z_{1},z_{2},e)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}\times\mathbb{H}^{2}\times M\backslash\mathrm{SU}(2)\,|\,z_{1},z_{2}\in l\}.

Therefore, by assuming nondegeneracy and by stationary phase, we expect
I(\lambda,\phi,g)\asymp\lambda\asymp\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}
to hold for such
\phi
.
It may be seen that, in general, the contribution of the integral over a neighborhood of
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}
can not be neglected.

The alternate approach we take in this paper is to decompose
\chi\phi
into geodesic beams:

(1.15)

\displaystyle\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\chi\varphi_{m,n},\quad\text{ for }\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta},

and we let
\phi_{m,n}=\chi\varphi_{m,n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.
Each
\phi_{m,n}
is approximately a Gaussian beam along some geodesic segment
l_{m,n}
and does not need to be still in
H_{\beta}
.
We construct this decomposition (
1.15
) in detail in Section
9.1

and show the square inequality in Proposition
9.4
, that is,

(1.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\|\phi\|_{2}^{2}.

for some constant
C>0
which is independent of
\phi
.
We divide the set
\{\phi_{m,n}\}
into two parts, based on their
L^{2}
-norms.
Let
0<\delta<1
be a small parameter to be chosen later.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)
the set of
\phi_{m,n}
’s so that their
L^{2}
-norms are
\geq(C\delta)^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
,
and denote by
\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)
the complement of
\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)
in
\{\phi_{m,n}\}
.
We let
\phi_{\delta}=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\phi_{m,n}
, let
\varphi_{\delta}=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\varphi_{m,n}
, and then write
\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle
as

(1.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle+\langle\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle.

We estimate the first term in (
1.17
) by bounding
\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle
for each individual
\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)
.
In Proposition
9.12
, we establish an estimate

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{1/2+\epsilon}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2})

for general
g
. This bound is weaker than the expected bound (
1.14
) but the proof is easier. Corollary
9.9
shows that
I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})
can be neglected unless the geodesic segment
\gamma g_{0}l_{m,n}
is contained in a small tubular neighborhood of
g_{0}l_{m,n}
.
The proof is based on the oscillatory integral estimates proved in Section
6
.
In Section
8
, we use a diophantine argument to show that there are few
\gamma
such that
\gamma g_{0}l_{m,n}
is contained in a small neighborhood of
g_{0}l_{m,n}
.
Combining these estimates with the amplification method, we conclude the following bound in Proposition
9.6
:

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}).

Inequality (
1.16
) implies the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)
is
\leq\delta^{-1}
. Hence, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and (
1.16
) give

(1.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\delta^{-1/2}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

We bound
\langle\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle

by applying a second arithmetic amplification argument.
We consider the Hecke returns with respect to
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
instead of a geodesic this time.
We first deduce the trivial bound

I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)\ll\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

if
g
stabilizes
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in Proposition
9.15
. To get a better estimate when
\gamma
is away from stabilizing
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we expand the integral
I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})
as the sum of integrals for geodesic beams

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(z_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(z_{2})k_{\lambda}(z_{1}^{-1}g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}z_{2})dz_{1}dz_{2}.

We show in Corollary
9.8
that the above integrals for the pair of
\phi_{m_{i},n_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, can be neglected unless
\gamma g_{0}l_{m_{2},n_{2}}
is contained in a small tubular neighborhood of
g_{0}l_{m_{1},n_{1}}
. The proof is also based on the oscillatory integral estimates proved in Section
6
.
In Section
7
and Proposition
7.8
, we show that there are few pairs of
(l_{m_{1},n_{1}},l_{m_{2},n_{2}})
such that
\gamma g_{0}l_{m_{2},n_{2}}
is contained in a small tubular neighborhood of
g_{0}l_{m_{1},n_{1}}
if
\gamma
is away from stabilizing
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
For the rest of the pairs of geodesics that are close, we use the general bound

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(z_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(z_{2})k_{\lambda}(z_{1}^{-1}g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}z_{2})dz_{1}dz_{2}

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}

\displaystyle\lambda^{1/2+\epsilon}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\|\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2})

proved in Proposition
9.13
with the property
\|\phi_{m_{i},n_{i}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\delta^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
.
Proposition
8.3
gives another Hecke return estimate which controls the number of
\gamma
such that
\gamma
almost stabilizes
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Combining these, in Proposition
9.7
, by the amplification method, we conclude the following estimate for the second term in (
1.17
):

(1.19)

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\delta^{1/138}\lambda^{1/4+\epsilon}\beta^{1/92}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

By combining (
1.12
), (
1.17
), (
1.18
) and (
1.19
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\chi\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}

\displaystyle\ll\|(1-\Pi_{\beta})(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+\|\Pi_{\beta}(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{1/4}\beta^{-1/4}+\delta^{-1/2}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}+\delta^{1/138}\lambda^{1/4+\epsilon}\beta^{1/92}.

By picking suitable parameters
\delta
and
\beta
, we can prove Theorem
1.1
.

1.4.

The author would like to thank his advisor Simon Marshall for suggesting this problem and providing much guidance and encouragement in the course of his work.
He would like to thank Jean-Philippe Anker and Angela Pasquale for answering questions on the Helgason transform.

2.
Notation

Throughout the paper, the notation
A\ll B
will mean that there is a positive constant
C

such that
|A|\leq CB
, and
A\asymp B
will mean that there are positive constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such
that
C_{1}B\leq A\leq C_{2}B
.
We also use
A=O(B)
to mean
A\ll B
.
The notation
A=o(B)
means
A/B
tends to 0.
Then
A\sim B
will mean
A=B+o(B)
.

2.1.
Quaternion algebras and adelic groups

Let
F
be a totally real number field with the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
. We denote the norm of an ideal
\mathfrak{n}
of
\mathcal{O}
by
\operatorname{N}(\mathfrak{n})
. If
v
is a finite place of
F
and
F_{v}
is the local field of
F
at
v
with the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{v}
,
\varpi_{v}
and
q_{v}
denote a uniformizer and the order of the residue field. Let
E
be a quadratic extension of
F
with exactly one complex place
w_{0}
, and let
v_{0}
be the place below
w_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{E}
be the ring of integers of
E
. We denote by
\operatorname{N}_{E}
the norm for ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. We let
\bar{\cdot}
denote the conjugation of
E
over
F
, and denote the trace and norm from
E
to
F
by
\operatorname{Tr}_{E}
and
\operatorname{Nm}_{E}
respectively. We denote the rings of adeles of
F
and
E
by
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}_{E}
, and the ring of finite adeles of
F
by
\mathbb{A}_{f}
. Let
|\cdot|_{v}
be the absolute value on
F_{v}
for any place
v
of
F
, and let
|\cdot|=\prod_{v}|\cdot|_{v}
be the absolute value on
\mathbb{A}
. Let
|\cdot|_{w}
be the absolute value on
E_{w}
for any place
w
of
E
, and let
|\cdot|_{E}=\prod_{w}|\cdot|_{w}
be the absolute value on
\mathbb{A}_{E}
.

Let
D
be a quaternion division algebra over
F
that is ramified at every real place except at
v_{0}
, and let
B=D\otimes_{F}E
. We assume that
B
is also a division algebra. We denote the standard anti-involution on
D
and
B
by
\iota
, and also use
\bar{\cdot}
to denote the conjugation of
B
over
D
. Let
\operatorname{trd}_{B}(x)=x+\iota(x)
and
\operatorname{nrd}_{B}(x)=x\iota(x)
denote the reduced trace and reduced norm on
B
.

We denote the groups of reduced norm
1
elements by
D^{1}
and
B^{1}
, and denote the multiplicative group by
D^{\times}
and
B^{\times}
. We let
\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{H}
be the algebraic groups over
F
such that
\mathbf{G}(F)=B^{1}
,
\mathbf{H}(F)=D^{1}
.
We denote
D\otimes_{F}F_{v}
,
B\otimes_{F}F_{v}
,
\mathbf{G}(F_{v})
and
\mathbf{H}(F_{v})
by
D_{v}
,
B_{v}
,
G_{v}
and
H_{v}
.
At
v_{0}
, we have
G_{v_{0}}\simeq\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
,
H_{v_{0}}\simeq\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
We will implicitly make these identifications later.
Let
H^{\prime}
be the normalizer of
H_{v_{0}}
in
G_{v_{0}}
which has two components with identity component
H_{v_{0}}
. In fact,

\displaystyle H^{\prime}=H_{v_{0}}\cup\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}H_{v_{0}}.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{B}\subset B
be a maximal
\mathcal{O}
order, and let
S
be a finite set of places of
F
containing all infinite places and all places where
D
ramifies and places where
E
is ramified over
F
. We choose a compact subgroup
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}
of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
as follows. We choose
K_{v_{0}}=\operatorname{SU}(2)
, and
K_{v}=B^{1}_{v}
for all other real places
v
.
The intersection
K_{v_{0}}\cap H_{v_{0}}
can be identified with
\operatorname{SO}(2)
and is a maximal compact subgroup of
H_{v_{0}}
.
For finite places in
S
we let
K_{v}
be any subgroup of
G_{v}
that
K_{v}\subset\mathcal{O}_{B,v}=\mathcal{O}_{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}_{v}
, and for other finite places we let
K_{v}=B^{1}_{v}\cap\mathcal{O}_{B,v}
.
After enlarging
S
, we may choose an isomorphism
\alpha
from
B_{v}
to the product of two
2
by
2
matrix algebras
M_{2}(F_{v})\times M_{2}(F_{v})
for all
v\notin S
that is split in
E
. The isomorphism
\alpha
satisfies:

•

\alpha(D_{v})=\{(T,T)|T\in M_{2}(F_{v})\}
,

•

\alpha(K_{v})=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{v})
,

and moreover here
\bar{\cdot}
is identified with the map switching the two factors.
Let
\mathscr{P}
be the set of primes
v\notin S
that split in
E
. We shall implicitly make the identification
\alpha
at places in
\mathscr{P}
. For
v\in\mathscr{P}
, we write
G_{v}=\operatorname{SL}(2,F_{v})\times\operatorname{SL}(2,F_{v})
, and
K_{v}=K_{v,1}\times K_{v,2}=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{v})\times\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{v})
.

Let
\mathscr{Q}
be the set of primes
v\notin S
that is inert in
E
. We still use
\alpha
to mean the isomorphism from
B_{v}
to
M_{2}(E_{v})
, the
2
-by-
2
matrix algebra over
E_{v}
, for
v\in\mathscr{Q}
. We may assume
\alpha
satisfies:

•

\alpha(D_{v})=M_{2}(F_{v})
,

•

\alpha(K_{v})=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{E_{v}})
.

For
v\in\mathscr{Q}
, we write
G_{v}=\operatorname{SL}(2,E_{v})
, and
K_{v}=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathcal{O}_{E,v})
.

For simplicity, we denote
G_{0}=G_{v_{0}}
,
H_{0}=H_{v_{0}}
, and
K_{0}=K_{v_{0}}
.

2.2.
Lie groups and algebras

Let
A
be the connected component of the diagonal subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle A=\left\{\left.\begin{pmatrix}e^{t/2}&0\\
0&e^{-t/2}\end{pmatrix}\right|t\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

Let

\displaystyle N=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&z\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\Big|z\in\mathbb{C}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad N_{0}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\Big|x\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

We denote the Lie algebras of
K_{0}
,
A
,
N
and
N_{0}
by
\mathfrak{k}
,
\mathfrak{a}
,
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
. We write the Iwasawa decompositions of
G_{0}=NAK_{0}
as

\displaystyle g=n(g)\exp\left(A(g)\right)\kappa(g)=\exp\left(N(g)\right)\exp\left(A(g)\right)\kappa(g).

We define

\displaystyle H=\begin{pmatrix}1/2&0\\
0&-1/2\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{a},\quad X=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{n}.

We identity
\mathfrak{a}\simeq\mathbb{R}
under the map
H\mapstochar\rightarrow 1
and consider
A(g)
as a function
A:G_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
under this identification, and obtain identifications
\mathfrak{n}\simeq\mathbb{C}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\simeq\mathbb{R}
similarly. We identify the dual space
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
of
\mathfrak{a}
as
\mathbb{R}
by sending the root
tH\mapstochar\rightarrow t
to
1
. Under these identifications, the pairing between
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
is the multiplication in
\mathbb{R}
.
We define the homomorhpism
a:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow A
by
a(t)=\exp(tH)
, and define
n:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow N
to be
n(z)=\exp(zX)
. The restriction of
n
to
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow N_{0}
will still be denoted by
n
. We denote by
\mathfrak{a}^{+}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the positive Weyl chamber, and by
A^{+}
the image
\exp(\mathfrak{a}^{+})=a(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
in
A
.

Let
M^{\prime}
and
M
be the normalizer and centralizer respectively of
A
in
K_{0}
. Then
W=M^{\prime}/M\simeq\mathbb{Z}/2
is the Weyl group with respect to
A
, and
M^{\prime}A
and
MA
are the normalizer and centralizer, respectively, of
A
in
G_{0}
.

We equip
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
with the norm

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|:\begin{pmatrix}Z_{1}&Z_{2}\\
Z_{3}&-Z_{1}\end{pmatrix}\mapstochar\rightarrow\left(\|Z_{1}\|^{2}+\|Z_{2}\|^{2}+\|Z_{3}\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

This norm induces a left-invariant metric on
G_{0}
, which we denote by
d
. We will denote the group identity by
e
.

We define a Haar measure
dg
on
G_{0}
through the Iwasawa decomposition
G_{0}=NAK_{0}
. Namely, if
g=n(z)a(t)k
, then
dg=e^{-2t}dzdtdk
. Here
dz,dt
are the standard measures on
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{R}
as Euclidean spaces, and
dk
is the probability Haar measure on
K_{0}
. Similarly, we define a Haar measure
dh
on
H_{0}
through the Iwasawa decomposition
H_{0}=N_{0}A\operatorname{SO}(2)
. If
h=n(x)a(t)k
, then
dh=e^{-t}dxdtdk
.

2.3.
Hecke algebras

For any continuous function
f
on
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
, we define
f^{*}(g)=\overline{f(g^{-1})}
. We define
\mathcal{H}_{f}=\bigotimes_{v<\infty}^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{v}
to be the convolution algebra of smooth functions on
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
that are compactly supported and bi-invariant under
K_{f}=\prod_{v<\infty}^{\prime}K_{v}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{v}
denote the space of smooth, compactly supported functions on
G_{v}
that are bi-invariant under
K_{v}
.
We let
\mathcal{H}^{S}=\bigotimes_{v\notin S}^{\prime}\mathcal{H}_{v}
be the unramified Hecke algebra.
We let
v\in\mathscr{P}
, and
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Define
K_{v}(a_{1},a_{2})
to be the double coset

\displaystyle K_{v}(a_{1},a_{2})=K_{v,1}(a_{1})\times K_{v,2}(a_{2}),

where
K_{v,i}(a_{i})=K_{v,i}\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}^{a_{i}}&\\
&\varpi_{v}^{-a_{i}}\end{pmatrix}K_{v,i}
.
We let

T_{v}(a)
be the characteristic function of
K_{v}(a,0)
.
If
v\in\mathscr{Q}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
K_{v}(a)
to be the double coset

\displaystyle K_{v}(a)=K_{v}\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}^{a}&\\
&\varpi_{v}^{-a}\end{pmatrix}K_{v}.

We let

T_{v}(a)
be the characteristic function of
K_{v}(a)
.
Given an ideal
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{n}
is only divisible by prime ideals not in
S
.
We define the double coset in
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})

\displaystyle K(\mathfrak{n})=\prod_{v\in\mathscr{P}}K_{v}(\operatorname{ord}_{v}(\mathfrak{n}),0)\times\prod_{v\in\mathscr{Q}}K_{v}(\operatorname{ord}_{v}(\mathfrak{n}))\times\prod_{v\in S}K_{v}.

In this paper the notation
K(\mathfrak{n})
is used non-standardly. We use this notation to denote the above Hecke double coset but not a principal congruence subgroup of
K
.

The action of
\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{f}
on an automorphic function
f
on
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
is given by the usual formula

\displaystyle[\phi f](x)=\int_{\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})}\phi(g)f(xg)dg.

Here we use the Haar measures
dg_{v}
on
G_{v}
, which are normalized so that
K_{v}
has a unit volume.

2.4.
Arithmetic manifolds and Hecke-Maass forms

Define
X=\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})/K
, which is a compact connected hyperbolic
3
-manifold. We let
\Omega=\prod_{v}\Omega_{v}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
be a compact set containing a fundamental domain for
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
.
The universal cover of
X
is the hyperbolic
3
-space
\mathbb{H}^{3}
obtained by the quotient
G_{0}/K_{0}=\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})/\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
We will denote the universal covering map by
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow X
.
We will use the upper half 3-space model of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and the upper half plane model of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{3}=\{(z,\mathbf{t})\,|\,z\in\mathbb{C},\,\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{H}^{2}=\{(x,\mathbf{t})\,|\,x\in\mathbb{R},\,\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}.

The embedding
\mathbb{H}^{2}\subset\mathbb{H}^{3}
is identified with the natural imbedding
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{SO}(2)\subset\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})/\operatorname{SU}(2)
.
We denote by
o=(0,1)\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
the origin of the symmetric space, which is the point fixed by the group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
. For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
and
(z,\mathbf{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we have the following formula:

(2.1)

\displaystyle g\cdot(z,\mathbf{t})=\left(\frac{(az+b)\overline{(cz+d)}+a\overline{c}\mathbf{t}^{2}}{|cz+d|^{2}+|c|^{2}\mathbf{t}^{2}},\frac{\mathbf{t}}{|cz+d|^{2}+|c|^{2}\mathbf{t}^{2}}\right).

And then
H^{\prime}
will be the stabilzer of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in
G_{0}
.
We will usually represent the
\mathbf{t}
-coordinate under the exponential map, that is,

\mathbf{t}=e^{t}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence,

\displaystyle(z,\mathbf{t})=n(z)a(t)\cdot o\in\mathbb{H}^{3},

where
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We let
\psi\in L^{2}(X)
be a Hecke-Maass form that is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian
\Delta
and the unramified Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}^{S}
. We let
\lambda>0
be its spectral parameter, so that

\displaystyle\Delta\psi+(1+\lambda^{2})\psi=0.

We assume that
\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(X)}=1
with respect to the hyperbolic volume on
X
. Note that because
\Delta
and
T(\mathfrak{n})
are self-adjoint, we may assume that
\psi
is real-valued.

3.
Fourier transforms on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}^{3}

We use the Harish-Chandra transforms and the Helgason transforms on both
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, so we must fix the notation we will use.
We use
\widehat{\cdot}
to mean the transforms on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
or
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
and
\widetilde{\cdot}
to mean the transforms on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
or
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
For definitions of Helgason and Harish-Chandra transforms on general noncompact symmetric spaces and their properties, we refer to
[
20
,
21
]
.

Let
\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
and
\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
be the spherical functions on
H_{0}
and
G_{0}
with spectral parameter
s
respectively. We recall the integral representations for the spherical functions. For any
x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
y\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
we have

(3.1)

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)=\int_{\operatorname{SO}(2)}e^{(is+1/2)A(kx)}dk,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(y)=\int_{K_{0}}e^{(is+1)A(ky)}dk.

Here the measures on the maximal compacts are both probability Haar measures.

Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
be a left
K_{0}
-invariant function, that is,
f(kx)=f(x)
for arbitrary
k\in K_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
. The Harish-Chandra transform on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
of
f
is defined to be

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}f(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)dx.

Here
dx
is the standard hyperbolic measure on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
so that the Haar measure
dg
on
G_{0}
is the extension of
dx
by the measure of mass
1
on
K_{0}
.

We denote by
B
the boundary of the symmetric space
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, which is the union of the real line and the point at infinity in the upper half plane model. By the linear fractional action of
\operatorname{SO}(2)
, the boundary
B
can be identified by
\operatorname{SO}(2)/\{\pm I\}
.
We use representatives in
\operatorname{SO}(2)
to mean the elements in
B
, and then we can parametrize
B
by the circle
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, that is

\displaystyle B=\left\{\left.b_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta/2&\sin\theta/2\\
-\sin\theta/2&\cos\theta/2\end{pmatrix}\right|\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

Given a function
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, the Helgason transform of
g
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
is defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(s,b)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}g(x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}dx,

where
dx
is the standard hyperbolic measure on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
,
s\in\mathbb{C}
,
b\in B
and
A(x,b)
is the distance from the origin in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
to the horocycle connecting
x
and
b
.
A(x,b)
can be calculated by the formula

\displaystyle A(x,b)=A(b^{-1}x).

Here we treat
x,b
as group elements in
H_{0}
, and the
A
on the right-hand side is the Iwasawa projection with respect to the Iwasawa decomposition
H_{0}=N_{0}A\operatorname{SO}(2)
.
If, furthermore,
g
is a left
\operatorname{SO}(2)
-invariant function, then its Helgason transform agrees with the Harish-Chandra transform on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, that is

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(s,b)=\widetilde{g}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}g(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx.

Let
f,g
be functions on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Their convolution, which we denote by
f\times g
, is defined to be the convolution of the pullbacks of
f,g
on the group
H_{0}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(f\times g)(x)=\int_{H_{0}}f(y\cdot o)g(y^{-1}\cdot x)dy.

If moreover we assume
g
is left
\operatorname{SO}(2)
-invariant, then

\displaystyle\widetilde{f\times g}(s,b)=\widetilde{f}(s,b)\widetilde{g}(s).

We will not use the Helgason transform on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
until Section
10
when dealing with the geodesic tube restrictions, so we shall postpone the definition and properties of the Helgason transform on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

We denote by
d\nu(s)

(s\in\mathbb{R})
the Plancherel measure for
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, and
d\mu(s)

(s\in\mathbb{R})
the Plancherel measure for
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Let
db
be the Haar measure on
B
so that the total measure of
B
is
1
, and we denote by
d\mu(s,b)=d\mu(s)db
where
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in B
.
We normalize the Plancherel measures so that the inverse Harish-Chandra transforms hold. More precisely, for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
and
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
that are left invariant under the maximal compact subgroups, we have

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{f}(s)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)d\nu(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{f}(s)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)d\nu(s),

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{g}(s)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)d\mu(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widetilde{g}(s)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)d\mu(s).

For any
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times B}\widetilde{g}(s,b)e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}d\mu(s,b)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B}\widetilde{g}(s,b)e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}d\mu(s,b).

From computing Harish-Chandra’s
\mathbf{c}
-functions, we know that
d\nu(s)
is some constant multiplying
s^{2}ds
, and
d\mu(s)
is some constant multiplying
s\tanh(\pi s)ds
. We will also use
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
to mean Helgason transform and its inversion on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
We list the results of Helgason transforms that we will use later: the Plancherel formula and the isomorphism on the classes of Schwartz functions.

Theorem 3.1
.

[
21
, Ch. III, Theorem 1.5, p. 202]

The Helgason transform extends from
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
to an isometry of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
onto
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
(with the measure
d\mu(s,b)
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B
).
We shall still denote the extended transform by
\widetilde{\cdot}
or
\mathcal{F}
and we have the Plancherel formula

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}f_{1}(x)\overline{f_{2}(x)}dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B}\widetilde{f_{1}}(s,b)\overline{\widetilde{f_{2}}(s,b)}d\mu(s,b),

for
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.

For any
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, we will denote the Hermitian form by

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}f_{1}(x)\overline{f_{2}(x)}dx.

Similarly, if
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
, we will write

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B}\phi_{1}(s,b)\overline{\phi_{2}(s,b)}d\mu(s,b).

The Schwartz space on a symmetric space is analogous to the Schwartz space on an Euclidean space.
We define the distance function on
H_{0}
according to the Cartan decomposition.
For
g=k_{1}a(t)k_{2}
with
k_{1},k_{2}\in\operatorname{SO}(2)
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we put
|g|=|t|
.
Let
\mathbf{D}(H_{0})
be the algebra of left-invariant differential operators on
H_{0}
, and
\overline{\mathbf{D}}(H_{0})
the algebra of right invariant differential operators on
H_{0}
. Then the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(H_{0})
is defined as the space of smooth functions
f
on
H_{0}
satisfying

(3.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in H_{0}}\left|(1+|g|)^{N}\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{0}(g)^{-1}(DEf)(g)\right|<\infty

for each
D\in\mathbf{D}(H_{0})
,
E\in\overline{\mathbf{D}}(H_{0})
and each positive integer
N
. The space
\mathcal{S}(H_{0})
is topologized by the seminorms (
3.4
).
The Harish-Chandra Schwartz space of the symmetric space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
is then defined as the space of
f\in\mathcal{S}(H_{0})
which are right invariant under the maximal compact subgroup
\operatorname{SO}(2)
. It is clear that

\displaystyle C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})

are dense embeddings in the
L^{2}
sense.

Let
\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R})
be the algebra of differential operators on
\mathbb{R}
with constant coefficients, and
\mathbf{D}(B)
the algebra of differential operators on

B
invariant under
\operatorname{SO}(2)
.
The Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)
on the frequency space
\mathbb{R}\times B
is then defined as the space of smooth functions
\phi
on
\mathbb{R}\times B
satisfying

(3.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R},b\in B}\left|(1+|s|)^{N}(DE\phi)(s,b)\right|<\infty

for each
D\in\mathbf{D}(\mathbb{R})
,
E\in\mathbf{D}(B)
, and each positive integer
N
.
The space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)
is topologized by the seminorms (
3.5
).
We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
the space of Schwartz functions
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)
satisfying the Weyl invariance functional equation

(3.6)

\displaystyle\int_{B}e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}\phi(s,b)db=\int_{B}e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}\phi(-s,b)db

for arbitrary
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
It is due to Eguchi that the Helgason transform is also continuous on the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space and has the same inversion formula.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
17
,
16
]
)
.

The Helgason transform is a continuous isomorphism of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
onto
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
, where the inverse is given by the integral (
3.3
).

3.1.
Extension of Schwartz functions with Weyl invariance

We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
the space of restrictions
\phi|_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}
for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)
.
Since every
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
is in the image of the
L^{2}
Helgason transform, it may be natural to ask whether every
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
is also the Helgason transform of some Harish-Chandra Schwartz function.
By Theorem
3.2
, the question is equivalent to whether we can extend any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
to a function in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
.

This direct generalization is false.
If we take
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
to be constant in the
B
variable, then the Weyl invariance condition (
3.6
) implies that

\displaystyle\phi(s)\int_{B}e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}db=\phi(-s)\int_{B}e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}db.

By taking
x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
to be the origin, we see that
\phi
is an even Schwartz function on
\mathbb{R}
, but it is clear that not all Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
can be extended to be even.
However, the simple modification requiring
0\notin\supp(\phi)
will give the extension property.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
satisfies
\supp(\phi)\subset[c,\infty)\times B
for some
c>0
.
There exists a unique
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
so that
\Phi|_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}=\phi
.

Following from this extension property and Eguchi’s result (Theorem
3.2
), a large class of Schwartz functions is in the image of the Helgason transform.

Corollary 3.4
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
satisfies
\supp(\phi)\subset[c,\infty)\times B
for some
c>0
. There exists a unique
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
so that
\widetilde{f}(s,b)=\phi(s,b)
for arbitrary
s>0
and
b\in B
.

To begin the proof, we recall the generalized spherical functions on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
We parametrize
\operatorname{SO}(2)
by the circle
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle\operatorname{SO}(2)=\left\{\left.k_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\right|\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

We will take the normalized Haar measure on
\operatorname{SO}(2)
, i.e.,
dk_{\theta}=d\theta/2\pi
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The character
\chi_{2n}:\operatorname{SO}(2)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is defined as

\displaystyle\chi_{2n}(k_{\theta})=e^{2in\theta}.

It may be seen that they are all characters of
\operatorname{SO}(2)
that are trivial on
\{\pm I\}
.
For
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the function

\displaystyle\varphi_{s,n}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\operatorname{SO}(2)}e^{(is+1/2)A(k^{-1}x)}\chi_{2n}(k)dk

\displaystyle=\int_{B}e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}\chi_{2n}(b)db

is called the generalized spherical function of class
\chi_{2n}
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. When
n=0
, this is the zonal spherical function.

It is proved in
[
21
, Ch. III, Theorem 2.10, p. 240]
that the generalized spherical function satisfies the functional equations

(3.7)

\displaystyle\varphi_{-s,n}(x)=\varphi_{s,n}(x)\Gamma_{n}(s),\quad\text{for any }x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}

with
\Gamma_{n}
a meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
.
In the case of rank one, an explicit formula for generalized spherical functions is calculated by Helgason
[
21
, Ch. III, Theorem 11.2, p. 328]
.
By the equation (14) in
[
21
, p. 331]
, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(s)=\frac{p_{n}(-s)q_{n}(-s)}{p_{n}(s)q_{n}(s)},

where

\displaystyle p_{n}(s)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(is+\frac{1}{2}+|n|))}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(is+\frac{1}{2}))},\quad q_{n}(s)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(is+\frac{1}{2}+1+|n|))}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(is+\frac{1}{2}+1))}.

Here
\Gamma
is the gamma function.
Applying the Legendre duplication formula, it may be seen that

\displaystyle p_{n}(s)q_{n}(s)=2^{-|n|}\frac{\Gamma(is+\frac{1}{2}+|n|)}{\Gamma(is+\frac{1}{2})}=2^{-|n|}\prod_{j=0}^{|n|-1}(is+\frac{1}{2}+j).

Hence,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}(s)=\prod_{j=0}^{|n|-1}(is+\frac{1}{2}+j)^{-1}(-is+\frac{1}{2}+j).

It may be seen that
\Gamma_{n}(s)
has no zeros or poles on
\mathbb{R}
.
We also need estimates for all derivatives of
1/\Gamma_{n}
uniformly for
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
z\in\mathbb{C}
lies in the horizontal strip

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left\{\sigma+it\,|\,\sigma\in\mathbb{R},-\min(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{1+|n|})\leq t\leq\min(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{1+|n|})\right\}.

Then
|1/\Gamma_{n}(z)|\ll 1
, where the implied constant doesn’t depend on
n
.

Proof.

If
0\leq t\leq\min(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{1+|n|})
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{n}(\sigma+it)|}=\prod_{j=0}^{|n|-1}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}+(\frac{1}{2}+j-t)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+(\frac{1}{2}+j+t)^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\prod_{j=0}^{|n|-1}1=1.

If
-\min(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{1+|n|})\leq t\leq 0
, we let
s=-t
. We may assume
n
is positive and
n\geq 3
so that
1/4\geq 1/(1+|n|)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\log\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{n}(\sigma+it)|}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{\sigma^{2}+(\frac{1}{2}+j+s)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+(\frac{1}{2}+j-s)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{\frac{1}{2}+j+s}{\frac{1}{2}+j-s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\frac{\frac{1}{2}+j+\frac{1}{1+n}}{\frac{1}{2}+j-\frac{1}{1+n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\log\left(1+\frac{1}{(n+1)(j+1/2)}\right)-\log\left(1-\frac{1}{(n+1)(j+1/2)}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n+1)(j+1/2)}

\displaystyle\ll 1.

∎

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
m\geq 0
be an integer. Then we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{m}}{ds^{m}}\frac{1}{\Gamma_{n}(s)}\ll m!(1+|n|)^{m},

for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta=\min(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{1+|n|})
.
We fix
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
The ball centered at
s
with radius
\delta
is contained in the strip (
3.9
).
Then (
3.10
) follows from Lemma
3.5
and the Cauchy integral formula.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

We show the existence by an explicit construction.
Recall that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
satisfies
\supp(\phi)\subset[c,\infty)\times B
for some
c>0
.
Using the characters
\chi_{2n}
on
B=\operatorname{SO}(2)/\{\pm I\}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the corresponding Fourier expansion

\displaystyle\phi(s,b)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}(s)\chi_{2n}(b)

with

\displaystyle\phi_{n}(s)=\int_{B}\phi(s,b)\overline{\chi_{2n}(b)}db.

Hence,
\supp(\phi_{n})\subset[c,\infty)
.
The Schwartz condition (
3.5
) implies that

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\geq c,n\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|s|)^{N_{1}}(1+|n|)^{N_{2}}\phi_{n}^{(m)}(s)<\infty

for arbitrary non-negative integers
N_{1},N_{2},m
. Here
\phi_{n}^{(m)}
means the
m
-th derivative of
\phi_{n}
.

We define

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(s)=\begin{cases}\phi_{n}(s)\quad&\text{ if }s\geq c,\\
\phi_{n}(-s)/\Gamma_{n}(-s)\quad&\text{ if }s\leq-c,\\
0&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and define

\displaystyle\Phi(s,b)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Phi_{n}(s)\chi_{2n}(b).

It is clear that the Fourier series converges and
\Phi|_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}=\phi
.
To see that
\Phi(s,b)
is a Schwartz function, we only need to show the rapid decay condition when
s<-c
.
By (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), we have, for
s<-c
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}^{(m)}(s)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom{m}{m-j}\phi_{n}^{(m-j)}(-s)\frac{d^{j}}{ds^{j}}\left(\frac{1}{\Gamma_{n}}\right)(-s)

\displaystyle\ll_{m,N_{1},N_{2}}\left((1+|s|)^{-N_{1}}(1+|n|)^{-N_{2}}\right)(1+|n|)^{m}.

This implies
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)
.

It remains to show
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
.
For
s\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
,
we apply the functional equation for generalized spherical functions (
3.7
) to obtain the following

\displaystyle\int_{B}e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}\Phi(-s,b)db

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Phi_{n}(-s)\int_{B}e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}\chi_{2n}(b)db

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\phi_{n}(s)}{\Gamma_{n}(s)}\varphi_{-s,n}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}(s)\varphi_{s,n}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}(s)\int_{B}e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}\chi_{2n}(b)db

\displaystyle=\int_{B}e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}\Phi(s,b)db,

which proves the Weyl invariance (
3.6
).

The uniqueness of the extension can be deduced from the inverse Helgason transform.
Suppose that
\Psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
also satisfies
\Psi|_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}=\phi
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\Phi)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\Psi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B}\phi(s,b)e^{(is+1/2)A(x,b)}d\mu(s,b).

By the isomorphism
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
, we conclude that
\Psi=\Phi
.
∎

4.
L^{2}
-norms of hyperbolic surface restrictions

4.1.
Main results

We first set up the hyperbolic surface restriction problem for the eigenfunction more precisely.
We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
the unit ball centered at the origin
o
.
Recall that
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{3}\rightarrow X
is the universal cover, and
Y\subset X
satisfies (
1.6
).
We let
g_{0}\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
be the isometries moving in a compact set and assume that

Y=\pi(g_{0}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}})
.
Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be a non-negative real-valued cutoff function supported in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
.
We may think of
\chi\psi|_{Y}
as a function on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\chi\psi|_{Y}(x):=\chi(x)\psi(\pi(g_{0}\cdot x)),\quad\text{ for }x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}.

If
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, we make the following convention

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle:=\langle\chi\psi|_{Y},\phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(x)\psi(\pi(g_{0}\cdot x))\overline{\phi(x)}dx.

If we let

\displaystyle\|\chi\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}=\langle\psi,\chi\cdot\chi\psi|_{Y}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\chi(x)\psi(\pi(g_{0}\cdot x))\right|^{2}dx,

to prove Theorem
1.1
, it suffices to show that
\|\chi\psi|_{Y}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{1/4-1/1220+\epsilon}
with the implied constant uniform in
g_{0}
.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
be a small constant satisfying
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 10^{-6}
.
Let
\beta
be a parameter satisfying
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
. We define
\widetilde{H}_{\beta}
to be the subspace
L^{2}([\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]\times B)
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
.
Define
H_{\beta}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{H}_{\beta})
, which is a closed subspace in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.
Let
\Pi_{\beta}
be the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
onto
H_{\beta}
.
Let
H_{\beta}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement of
H_{\beta}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
,
and let
\widetilde{H}_{\beta}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal complement of
\widetilde{H}_{\beta}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
.
It is clear that
\widetilde{H}_{\beta}^{\perp}=\mathcal{F}(H_{\beta}^{\perp})
.
To prove Theorem
1.1
, we shall bound
\Pi_{\beta}(\chi\psi|_{Y})
and
(1-\Pi_{\beta})(\chi\psi|_{Y})
separately.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
. For any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}^{\perp}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/4}\beta^{-1/4}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|(1-\Pi_{\beta})(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/4}\beta^{-1/4}.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/25}
. For any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{1/4-1/1120+\epsilon}\beta^{25/1120}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{\beta}(\chi\psi|_{Y})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{1/4-1/1120+\epsilon}\beta^{25/1120}.

The proofs of these propositions will be finished in Sections
5
and
9
. Then Theorem
1.1
follows from combining the above two propositions with
\beta=\lambda^{1/305}
.

4.2.
Amplification inequalities

We fix a real-valued function
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of Paley-Wiener type that is nonnegative and satisfies
h(0)=1
. Define
h_{\lambda}^{0}(s)=h(s-\lambda)+h(-s-\lambda)
, and let
k_{\lambda}^{0}
be the
K_{0}
-bi-invariant function on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with Harish-Chandra transform
h^{0}_{\lambda}
.
The Paley-Wiener theorem implies that
k_{\lambda}^{0}
is of compact support that may be chosen arbitrarily small. Define
k_{\lambda}=k_{\lambda}^{0}*k_{\lambda}^{0}
, which has Harish-Chandra transform
h_{\lambda}=(h_{\lambda}^{0})^{2}
. If
g\in G_{0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
, we define

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\phi,g)=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi(x_{1})\phi(x_{1})}\chi(x_{2})\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{1}^{-1}gx_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}.

We always assume that the supports of
\chi
and
k_{\lambda}
are small enough that
I(\lambda,\phi,g)=0
unless
d(g,e)\leq 1
, and denote this compact subset by
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset G_{0}
.
The main inequality for the hyperbolic surface restriction we shall use is the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{H}^{S}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
. We have

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\ll\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}\left|(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Proof.

Consider the function

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}k_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(x^{-1}\gamma y)

on
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
, where
k_{\infty}
is a compactly supported and
K_{\infty}
-bi-invariant function on
G_{\infty}
defined by
k_{\infty}(x_{\infty})=k_{\lambda}(x_{v_{0}})
. The spectral decomposition of
L^{2}(X)
is

\displaystyle L^{2}(X)=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{C}\psi_{i},

where
\psi_{i}
’s are Hecke-Maass forms on
X
with spectral parameters
\lambda_{i}
, which form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(X)
and
\psi
is one of them. Then by
[
31
]
, the integral operator acts on Hecke-Maass forms
\psi_{i}
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})}K(x,y)\psi_{i}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})}k_{\infty}\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*}(x^{-1}y)\psi_{i}(y)dy=h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i}){\mathcal{T}^{*}}\mathcal{T}\psi_{i}(x).

Hence,
K(x,y)
has a spectral expansion

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\mathcal{T}\psi_{i}(x)\overline{\mathcal{T}\psi_{i}(y)}.

If we integrate it against
\overline{\chi\phi}\times\chi\phi
on
g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}\times g_{0}\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi(x_{1})\phi(x_{1})}\chi(x_{2})\phi(x_{2})K(g_{0}x_{1},g_{0}x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(\gamma)\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi(x_{1})\phi(x_{1})}\chi(x_{2})\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{1}^{-1}g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}.

Since we have
h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
, dropping all terms but
\psi
completes the proof.
∎

4.3.
The local bound

In particular, we can choose
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{f}
to be the characteristic function of a
sufficiently small open subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
so that only the identity element
\gamma=e
will make a nonzero contribution to the sum in (
4.1
). This gives

(4.2)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\ll\left|I(\lambda,\phi,e)\right|.

Here

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\phi,e)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi(x_{1})\phi(x_{1})}\chi(x_{2})\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{1}^{-1}x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi(x_{1})\phi(x_{1})}\chi(x_{2})\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1})dx_{1}dx_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi\phi(x_{2})k_{\lambda}(x_{2}^{-1}x_{1})dx_{2}\right)\overline{\chi\phi(x_{1})}dx_{1}

\displaystyle=\langle\chi\phi\times k_{\lambda}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}},\chi\phi\rangle.

For notation simplicity, we shall still denote
k_{\lambda}|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
by
k_{\lambda}
when there is no ambiguity.
We apply the Plancherel formula to obtain

(4.3)

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\phi,e)=\langle\chi\phi\times k_{\lambda},\chi\phi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}(\chi\phi\times k_{\lambda}),\widetilde{\chi\phi}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\chi\phi}\cdot\widetilde{k_{\lambda}},\widetilde{\chi\phi}\rangle\leq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|\cdot\|\chi\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Then the estimate (
5.8
) of
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}
in next section will prove the local bound
\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\ll\lambda^{1/4}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
.

5.
Bounds away from the spectrum

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
4.1

We define the intervals

\displaystyle I^{\pm}_{\delta}=\pm[\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta],\quad\text{ and }\quad I_{\delta}=I^{+}_{\delta}\cup I^{-}_{\delta},

for
0<\delta\leq\lambda
.
Let
\tau_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be an even function satisfying

•

0\leq\tau_{1}\leq 1
;

•

\tau_{1}\equiv 1
on
I_{\beta/4}
;

•

\supp(\tau_{1})\subset I_{\beta/2}
.

For
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}^{\perp}
, we define
\chi\phi=\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}
so that

(5.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(s,b)=\tau_{1}(s)\widetilde{\chi\phi}(s,b),\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\phi_{2}}(s,b)=(1-\tau_{1}(s))\widetilde{\chi\phi}(s,b).

It is clear that
\widetilde{\phi_{1}},\widetilde{\phi_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
, and thus
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
are well-defined.
We want to show that the main term of this decomposition is
\phi_{2}
.
Roughly, multiplying a cutoff
\chi
to
\phi
will not change the support of its Helgason transform too much.
We may first calculate the gradients of the Iwasawa
A
-projection that will appear in the phase function of the Helgason transform. Recall that a boundary element
b_{\theta}
is represented by the element
\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta/2&\sin\theta/2\\
-\sin\theta/2&\cos\theta/2\end{pmatrix}
in
\operatorname{SO}(2)
.
Let
b_{\theta}\in B
and
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
.
By a direct calculation using the fractional linear action on the upper half plane model (
2.1
), we may obtain

(5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))&=t-\log\left(\cos^{2}(\theta/2)+(x^{2}+e^{2t})\sin^{2}(\theta/2)-2x\sin(\theta/2)\cos(\theta/2)\right).\end{split}

Lemma 5.1
.

Given
C>0
and
0<\delta<\pi
, there is a constant
c_{1}>0
only depending on
C
and
\delta
so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\leq 1-c_{1},

for
x,t\in(-C,C)
and
\theta\notin(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
.

Proof.

By (
5.2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2e^{2t}\sin^{2}(\theta/2)}{\cos^{2}(\theta/2)+(x^{2}+e^{2t})\sin^{2}(\theta/2)-2x\sin(\theta/2)\cos(\theta/2)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2e^{2t}\sin^{2}(\theta/2)}{e^{2t}\sin^{2}(\theta/2)+(x\sin(\theta/2)-\cos(\theta/2))^{2}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2}{1+(x-\cot(\theta/2))^{2}e^{-2t}}.

We can take
c_{1}
to be the infimum of
\frac{2}{1+(x-\cot(\theta/2))^{2}e^{-2t}}
for
x,t\in(-C,C)
and
\theta\notin(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Given a constant
C>0
, if
x,t\in(-C,C)
, then

(5.3)

\displaystyle A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=t+x\theta+\frac{x^{2}-e^{2t}+1}{4}\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}).

Moreover, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=\theta+\frac{x}{2}\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}),

(5.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=1-\frac{e^{2t}}{2}\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}).

Here the implied constants depend only on
C
.

Proof.

Since the Taylor approximations give

\displaystyle\cos^{2}(\theta/2)=1-{\theta^{2}}/{4}+O(\theta^{4}),

\displaystyle\sin^{2}(\theta/2)={\theta^{2}}/{4}+O(\theta^{4}),

\displaystyle 2\sin(\theta/2)\cos(\theta/2)=\theta+O(\theta^{3}),

we have

\displaystyle\log\left(\cos^{2}(\theta/2)+(x^{2}+e^{2t})\sin^{2}(\theta/2)-2x\sin(\theta/2)\cos(\theta/2)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\left(1-\theta^{2}/4+(x^{2}+e^{2t})\theta^{2}/4-x\theta+O(\theta^{3})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-x\theta+(x^{2}+e^{2t}-1)\theta^{2}/4\right)-\left(-x\theta+(x^{2}+e^{2t}-1)\theta^{2}/4\right)^{2}/2+O(\theta^{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-x\theta+(x^{2}+e^{2t}-1)\theta^{2}/4-x^{2}\theta^{2}/2+O(\theta^{3}).

Hence, we obtain (
5.3
) from (
5.2
). Moreover, by the analyticity of elementary functions, we have

\displaystyle A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=t+x\theta+\frac{x^{2}-e^{2t}+1}{4}\theta^{2}+\theta^{3}\xi(x,t,\theta),

where
\xi
is analytic. Then we obtain (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) by taking derivatives.

∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that
1\leq\beta\leq\lambda
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}^{\perp}
. Let
\phi_{1}
be defined as in (
5.1
). Then we have

\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll_{N}\beta^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
b\in B
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(s,b)=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(s)\widetilde{\chi\phi}(s,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(s)\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(x)\phi(x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(s)\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(x)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\widetilde{\phi}(r,d)e^{(ir+1/2)A(x,d)}d\mu(r,d)\right)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x,b)}dx

(5.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}(s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\widetilde{\phi}(r,d)\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(x)e^{(A(x,d)+A(x,b))/2}\exp(irA(d^{-1}x)-isA(b^{-1}x))dx\right)d\mu(r,d).

Because of the supports of
\tau_{1}
and
\widetilde{\phi}
, we can assume
s\in I_{\beta/2}
and
r\notin I_{\beta}
, otherwise the outer integral in (
5.6
)
will vanish.

We first assume that
0\leq r\leq\lambda-\beta
.
By the change of variable
x\mapstochar\rightarrow bx
, the integral becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(bx)e^{(A(bx,d)+A(bx,b))/2}\exp(irA(d^{-1}bx)-isA(x))dx.

We assume
d^{-1}b=b_{\theta}
with
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
,
and write the integral under the Iwasawa coordinates
(x,t)\mapstochar\rightarrow n(x)a(t)\cdot o=(x,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Then the integral can be written as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi^{\prime}(x,t)\exp(irA(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))-ist)dxdt

(5.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi^{\prime}(x,t)\exp(-is\varphi(x,t))dxdt

Here
\chi^{\prime}
is a smooth, compactly supported function obtained by combining all of the amplitude factors,
and

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t)

\displaystyle=t-\frac{r}{s}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

is the phase function. We omit the parameters
\theta
and
r/s
when we write the variables of
\chi^{\prime}
and
\varphi
for simplicity.
We choose a constant
C>0
so that
(-C,C)^{2}
contains the supports of
\chi^{\prime}
for arbitrary
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\delta>0
be chosen as in Lemma
5.2
. When
\theta\notin(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
, Lemma
5.1
gives a constant
c_{1}>0
so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\leq 1-c_{1},

for
x,t\in\supp(\chi^{\prime})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\varphi(x,t)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{r}{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{r}{s}(1-c_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\min\{1,c_{1}\}.

Since
|r/s|\leq 1
, the
n
-th higher derivatives of
\varphi
are
\ll_{n}1
. By integrating by parts,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi^{\prime}(x,t)\exp(-is\varphi(x,t))dt\ll_{N}s^{-N}.

After integrating the
x
variable, the integral (
5.7
) is also
\ll_{N}s^{-N}
.
Now we assume
\theta\in(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
. We first write the phase function as

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t)

\displaystyle=\big(t-A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\big)+\frac{s-r}{s}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}\varphi_{1}(x,t)+\alpha_{2}\varphi_{2}(x,t),

where
\alpha_{1}=\theta
,
\alpha_{2}=(s-r)/s
,
\varphi_{1}(x,t)=\big(t-A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\big)/\theta
and
\varphi_{2}(x,t)=A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))
.
Lemma
5.2
implies that
\varphi_{1}
is still analytic and

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x,t)=-x-\frac{x^{2}-e^{2t}+1}{4}\theta+O(\theta^{2}).

By calculating their derivatives or applying Lemma
5.2
, we obtain the gradients of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
:

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi_{1}=\left(\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}}{\partial x},\frac{\partial\varphi_{1}}{\partial t}\right)=\left(-1,0\right)+O(\theta),

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi_{2}=\left(\frac{\partial\varphi_{2}}{\partial x},\frac{\partial\varphi_{2}}{\partial t}\right)=\left(0,1\right)+O(\theta).

Let
\rho=(\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2})^{1/2}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla\left(\frac{\varphi}{\rho}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\rho}\nabla\varphi_{1}+\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\rho}\nabla\varphi_{2}=(-\alpha_{1}/\rho,\alpha_{2}/\rho)+O(\theta).

Hence, the square of the norm is

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\left(\frac{\varphi}{\rho}\right)\right\|^{2}=\frac{\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+O(\theta)=1+O(\theta).

Without loss of generality, we can take
\delta
to be sufficiently small so that
\left\|\nabla\left({\varphi}/{\rho}\right)\right\|\geq 1/2
when
\theta\in(-\delta,\delta)
.
It is clear that all the
n
-th higher derivatives of
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are
\ll_{n}1
. Since
|\alpha_{1}/\rho|,|\alpha_{2}/\rho|\leq 1
, all the
n
-th higher derivatives of
\varphi/\rho
are also
\ll_{n}1
.
Hence, by integrating by parts, (
5.7
) is

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi^{\prime}(x,t)\exp\left(-is\rho\frac{\varphi(x,t)}{\rho}\right)dxdt\ll_{N}(1+s\rho)^{-N}.

Since
s\rho\geq s\alpha_{2}=s-r
, the intgeral (
5.7
) is
\ll_{N}(s-r)^{-N}
. To sum up, we have shown that the inner integral in (
5.6
) is
\ll_{N}(s-r)^{-N}
when
0\leq r\leq\lambda-\beta
.

If
r\geq\lambda+\beta
, likewise, by the change of variable
x\rightarrow d\cdot x
, we write the inner integral in (
5.6
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(dx)e^{(A(dx,d)+A(dx,b))/2}\exp(irA(x)-isA(b^{-1}dx))dx.

We assume
b^{-1}d=b_{\theta}
and write the integral under the Iwasawa coordinates as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi^{\prime}(x,t)\exp(ir\varphi(x,t))dxdt

with
\chi^{\prime}
the smooth compactly supported function from all of the amplitude factors and
