\displaystyle\varphi(x,t)

\displaystyle=t-\frac{s}{r}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=\frac{s}{r}\big(t-A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\big)+\frac{r-s}{r}t.

Then the same argument shows the oscillatory integral is
\ll_{N}(r-s)^{-N}
. Applying these bounds with Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to (
5.6
) gives
|\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(s,b)|\ll_{N}\beta^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
. Then the proposition follows from the Plancherel identity

\displaystyle\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}=\int_{I^{+}_{\beta/2}\times B}|\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(s,b)|^{2}d\mu(s,b)\ll_{N}\beta^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}^{\perp}
.
By (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\left|I(\lambda,\phi,e)\right|=\langle\widetilde{\chi\phi}\cdot\widetilde{k_{\lambda}},\widetilde{\chi\phi}\rangle.

Proposition
5.3
shows that
\widetilde{\chi\phi}=\widetilde{\phi_{1}}+\widetilde{\phi_{2}}=\widetilde{\phi_{2}}+O_{N}(\beta^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})})
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\ll_{N}\left(\sup_{t\notin I_{\beta/4}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(t)|+\beta^{-N}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|\right)\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Applying the following Proposition
5.4
with the assumption
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},N}\left(\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}+\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-N}\right)\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\ll\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2},

which completes the proof.
∎

It remains to show the following point-wise estimate of
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

We have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{1/2}.

Moreover, if
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
, we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq|s|\leq\lambda/2}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|

\displaystyle\ll 1,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\notin I_{\beta/4},|s|\geq\lambda/2}|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)|

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}.

Proof.

The proofs of (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) are the main part of this section. We postpone them and prove (
5.8
) here by assuming (
5.9
) and (
5.10
).

Clearly, (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) provide stronger bounds than (
5.8
) when
s\notin I_{\beta/4}
, so we only need to show the bound in (
5.8
) provided
s\in I_{\beta/4}
. Using the integral formula for
\mathbb{H}^{2}
under the polar coordinates, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}k_{\lambda}(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{\infty}k_{\lambda}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt,

for some normalizing constant
c>0
. Because
k_{\lambda}
has a compact support near the origin, there exists a smooth compactly supported function
b_{1}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
so that
b_{1}(t)k_{\lambda}(a(t))=k_{\lambda}(a(t))
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Without loss of generality, we assume
b_{1}\equiv 1
on
[-1,1]
and vanishes outside
[-2,2]
. Hence,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)=c\int_{0}^{2}k_{\lambda}(a(t))b_{1}(t)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt.

Using
[
30
, Lemma 2.8]
, we have

\displaystyle k_{\lambda}(a(t))\ll\lambda^{2}(1+\lambda|t|)^{-1}.

By
[
30
, Theorem 1.3]
, we have

\displaystyle b_{1}(t)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\ll(1+|st|)^{-1/2}.

Therefore, for
s\in I_{\beta/4}
, the above two bounds with the integral formula (
5.11
) imply the desired bound.
∎

5.2.
Bound of
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)
for small
s

Now we begin the proof of the inequality (
5.9
) in Proposition
5.4
.
Suppose
0\leq|s|\leq\lambda/2
.
Since
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}
is even, we assume
0\leq s\leq\lambda/2
.
Let
b_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be a smooth cutoff function supported in a neighborhood of origin satisfying

•

b_{2}
is
\operatorname{SO}(2)
-bi-invariant;

•

b_{2}(x)k_{\lambda}(x)=k_{\lambda}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
.

We unfold
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(t)
by the inverse Harish-Chandra transform and then apply the integral formulas (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) to the spherical functions. Then we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}k_{\lambda}(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(x)k_{\lambda}(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(x)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}h_{\lambda}(r)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)d\nu(r)\right)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(x)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(x)dx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{\operatorname{SO}(2)}b_{2}(x)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(kx)}dkdx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\operatorname{SO}(2)}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(k^{-1}x)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(k^{-1}x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x)}dxdk\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\operatorname{SO}(2)}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(x)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x)}dxdk\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}b_{2}(x)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)e^{(-is+1/2)A(x)}dx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

(5.12)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{K_{0}}b_{2}(x)e^{(ir+1)A(kx)}e^{(-is+1/2)A(x)}dxdk\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r).

We denote the inner integral inside (
5.12
) by
J(r;\rho)
with
\rho=s/r
, so

\displaystyle J(r;\rho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{K_{0}}b_{2}(x)e^{A(kx)+A(x)/2}\exp(irA(kx)-isA(x))dxdk

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{K_{0}}b_{2}(n(x)a(t)\cdot o)e^{A(kn(x)a(t))+t/2}\exp(ir(A(kn(x)a(t))-\rho t))e^{-t}dxdtdk

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}b_{2}(n(x)a(t)\cdot o)e^{A(kn(x)a(t))+t/2}\exp(ir(A(kn(x)a(t))-\rho t))e^{-t}dxdtdk.

Recall that the factor
e^{-t}
is from the hyperbolic metric, and
M
is the centralizer of
A
in
K_{0}
.
Let
b_{3}(x,t,k)=b_{2}(n(x)a(t)\cdot o)e^{A(kn(x)a(t))+t/2}e^{-t}
. It is clear that
b_{3}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times M\backslash K_{0})
. Hence,

\displaystyle J(r;\rho)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}b_{3}(x,t,k)\exp(irF(x,t,k;\rho))dxdtdk,

where

\displaystyle F(x,t,k;\rho)=A(kn(x)a(t))-\rho t

is the phase function of the oscillatory integral.
Since
h_{\lambda}
is a Paley-Wiener function concentrating near
\pm\lambda
and
d\nu(r)=c\cdot r^{2}dr
for some constant
c\neq 0
, it may be seen that the bound (
5.9
) will follow from (
5.12
) and the following estimate of
J(r;\rho)
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Fix a constant
0<\delta<1
. We have
J(r;\rho)\ll_{\delta}r^{-2}
if
\rho\in[0,\delta]
.

We will use the method of stationary phase to bound the oscillatory integral
J(r;\rho)
, so we first need to determine the critical points of
F
.
Using the formula (
2.1
), we get the following formula about the Iwasawa projection
A
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Given
g\in G_{0}
with Iwasawa decomposition
g=nak
where
a=a(t_{0})\in A
and
k=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
, we have, for
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.13)

\displaystyle A(gn(z)a(t))=t_{0}+t-\log\left(|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\alpha\bar{\beta}z-\bar{\alpha}\beta\bar{z}\right).

By taking derivatives, for
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain

(5.14)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(gn(x))|_{x=0}=\alpha\bar{\beta}+\bar{\alpha}\beta

and

(5.15)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(ga(t))|_{t=0}=|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}.

For simplicity, we let
\Psi
and
\Theta
be the functions on
K
given by sending
k=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
to
\alpha\bar{\beta}+\bar{\alpha}\beta
and
|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}
respectively. Therefore (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) can be read as
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(gn(x))|_{x=0}=\Psi(\kappa(g))
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(ga(t))|_{t=0}=\Theta(\kappa(g))
.
Moreover, it may be seen that
\Psi
and
\Theta
are left invariant under
M
. Hence, we will also treat them as functions on
M\backslash K_{0}
.

For doing calculations, we choose the following basis for
\mathfrak{k}
:

(5.16)

\displaystyle X_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},\,X_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\,X_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix}.

Proposition 5.7
.

An element
g\in G_{0}
lies in
AK_{0}
if and only if
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX)g)|_{t=0}=0
for arbitrary
X\in\mathfrak{k}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume
g=a(t_{0})\in A
. We only have to check
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX)g)|_{t=0}=0
holds for a basis of
\mathfrak{k}
. By (
5.13
), we have
A(\exp(tX_{1})a(t_{0}))=A(\exp(tX_{2})a(t_{0}))=t_{0}-\log(\cos^{2}t+e^{2t}\sin^{2}t)
and
A(\exp(tX_{3})a(t_{0}))=t_{0}
, so
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX_{i})g)|_{t=0}=0
with
i=1,2,3
.

Conversely, we suppose
g\notin AK
but
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX)g)|_{t=0}=0
for arbitrary
X\in\mathfrak{k}
. We can assume
g=n(z_{0})a(t_{0})
with
z_{0}\neq 0
. By (
5.13
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX_{2})g)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left[t_{0}-\log\left(\cos^{2}(t)+\sin^{2}(t)(|z_{0}|^{2}+e^{2t_{0}})+\cos(t)\sin(t)(z_{0}+\bar{z}_{0})\right)\right]|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=z_{0}+\bar{z}_{0}=0,

implying
z_{0}=iy_{0}\neq 0
is purely imaginary. But

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(tX_{1})g)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left[t_{0}-\log\left(\cos^{2}(t)+\sin^{2}(t)(y_{0}^{2}+e^{2t_{0}})+2\cos(t)\sin(t)y_{0}\right)\right]|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=2y_{0}=0

gives a contradiction.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

For any fixed
0<\delta<1
and
\rho\in[0,\delta]
, the only critical point of
F(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot;\rho)
(with respect to the variables (x,t,k)) is
(0,0,k^{\prime})
with
k^{\prime}=Mk_{\rho}
. Here

k_{\rho}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\rho}&i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
i\sqrt{1-\rho}&\sqrt{1+\rho}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

We let
l
be the image of
A
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which is a vertical geodesic through the origin
o
.
Suppose that
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times M\backslash K_{0}
is a critical point of
F
. By Proposition
5.7
, taking the differentiation with respect to the
k
variable equal to zero is equivalent to

\displaystyle k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})=a^{\prime}u^{\prime}

for some
a^{\prime}\in A
and
u^{\prime}\in M\backslash K_{0}
. Note that this is well-defined because
M
centralizes
A
.
Now we take the derivatives with respect to the
x,t
variables. Applying (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}F(x^{\prime}+x,t^{\prime},k^{\prime})|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(A(k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime}+x)a(t^{\prime}))-\rho t^{\prime}\right)|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})n(e^{-t^{\prime}}x))|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(a^{\prime}u^{\prime}n(e^{-t^{\prime}}x))|_{x=0}

\displaystyle=e^{-t^{\prime}}\Psi(u^{\prime}),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}F(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}+t,k^{\prime})|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(A(k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime}+t))-\rho(t^{\prime}+t)\right)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})a(t))|_{t=0}-\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(a^{\prime}u^{\prime}a(t))|_{t=0}-\rho

\displaystyle=\Theta(u^{\prime})-\rho.

Therefore,
\Psi(u^{\prime})=0
and
\Theta(u^{\prime})=\rho
. Direct computation shows the only solution is
u^{\prime}=Mk_{\rho}
. Recall that we have
k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})=a^{\prime}u^{\prime}
. We choose a representative
k^{\prime}\in K_{0}
so that the relation
k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})=a^{\prime}k_{\rho}
holds as group elements.
We claim that

\displaystyle k^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}=k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})\mathbb{H}^{2}=a^{\prime}k_{\rho}\mathbb{H}^{2}.

is a hemisphere in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Suppose otherwise, i.e.,
k^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a vertical plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. Then
k_{\rho}\mathbb{H}^{2}
is also a vertical plane, containing
l
.
Therefore,
k_{\rho}\in M\operatorname{SO}(2)
and we may find
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
so that

\displaystyle k_{\rho}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{1}}&\\
&e^{-i\theta_{1}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta_{2})&\sin(\theta_{2})\\
-\sin(\theta_{2})&\cos(\theta_{2})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta_{1}}\cos(\theta_{2})&e^{i\theta_{1}}\sin(\theta_{2})\\
-e^{-i\theta_{1}}\sin(\theta_{2})&e^{-i\theta_{1}}\cos(\theta_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{1+\rho}}{\sqrt{2}}=e^{i\theta_{1}}\cos(\theta_{2})=e^{-i\theta_{1}}\cos(\theta_{2}),\quad\frac{i\sqrt{1-\rho}}{\sqrt{2}}=e^{i\theta_{1}}\sin(\theta_{2})=-e^{-i\theta_{1}}\sin(\theta_{2}),

which is a contradiction because no such
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
satisfy the above equations.
Hence,
k^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a hemisphere, and the intersection of
l
with
k^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a single point, which is the origin
o
.
Acting
k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})=a^{\prime}k_{\rho}
at
o
, it may be seen that the point

\displaystyle k^{\prime}(x^{\prime},e^{t^{\prime}})=k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})\cdot o=a^{\prime}k_{\rho}\cdot o=a^{\prime}\cdot o

lies on both
k^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
l
. Hence,
k^{\prime}n(x^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})\cdot o=o
, and so
x^{\prime}=t^{\prime}=0
.

Conversely, it is easy to see
(0,0,Mk_{\rho})
is a critical point of
F
.
∎

The tangent space at
M
in the quotient space
M\backslash K_{0}
can be viewed as a two-dimensional subspace of
\mathfrak{k}
that is transversal to the Lie algebra of
M
. We will choose the subspace of
\mathfrak{k}
spanned by the vectors
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
.
By the non-stationary phase, the integral in
J(s;\rho)
over the region away from the critical points decays rapidly, so we only need to estimate
J(s;\rho)
by the integrals over some small neighborhoods of the critical points so that we can represent the
k
variable in
M\backslash K_{0}
via the exponential map. To sum up, we have

\displaystyle J(r;\rho)=\widetilde{J}(r;\rho)+O_{N}(r^{-N})

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(r;\rho)=\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{b_{3}}(x,t,s_{1},s_{2})\exp(ir\widetilde{F}(x,t,s_{1},s_{2};\rho))dxdtds_{1}ds_{2}.

Here
\widetilde{b_{3}}(x,t,s_{1},s_{2})
is a smooth function supported in a small neighborhood of
(0,0,0,0)
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}(x,t,s_{1},s_{2};\rho)=A(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho}n(x)a(t))-\rho t.

is the phase functions of the oscillatory integral
\widetilde{J}(r;\rho)
, with the only critical points at
(0,0,0,0)
. It remains to calculate the Hessian of
\widetilde{F}
at
(0,0,0,0)
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G_{0}
, its Iwasawa projection to
K_{0}
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\kappa(g)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|c|^{2}+|d|^{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}{\bar{d}}&-{\bar{c}}\\
{c}&{d}\end{pmatrix},

that is,
g\in NA\kappa(g)
.

Proof.

Directly follows from computing the action of
g
on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Given
k=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&{\beta}\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
, the gradients of
\Psi
and
\Theta
at
k
are

\displaystyle\nabla_{K_{0}}\Psi(k):=\left.\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi(\exp(tX_{1})k),\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi(\exp(tX_{2})k),\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi(\exp(tX_{3})k)\right)\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(-i(\alpha^{2}-\bar{\alpha}^{2}-\beta^{2}+\bar{\beta}^{2}),-(\alpha^{2}+\bar{\alpha}^{2}-\beta^{2}-\bar{\beta}^{2}),0\right)

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{K_{0}}\Theta(k)=\left(2i(\alpha\beta-\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}),2(\alpha\beta+\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}),0\right).

In particular,

\displaystyle\nabla\Psi(k_{\rho})=\left(0,-2,0\right)

and

\displaystyle\nabla\Theta(k_{\rho})=\left(-2\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}},0,0\right).

Proof.

Since

\displaystyle\exp(tX_{1})k=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\cos t-i\bar{\beta}\sin t&{\beta}\cos t+i\bar{\alpha}\sin t\\
-\bar{\beta}\cos t+i\alpha\sin t&\bar{\alpha}\cos t+i\beta\sin t\end{pmatrix},

we have

\displaystyle\Psi(\exp(tX_{1})k)

\displaystyle=(\alpha\cos t-i\bar{\beta}\sin t)(\bar{\beta}\cos t-i\alpha\sin t)+(\bar{\alpha}\cos t+i\beta\sin t)({\beta}\cos t+i\bar{\alpha}\sin t)

\displaystyle=(\alpha\bar{\beta}+\bar{\alpha}\beta)(\cos^{2}t-\sin^{2}t)-i(\alpha^{2}-\bar{\alpha}^{2}-\beta^{2}+\bar{\beta}^{2})\sin t\cos t

and

\displaystyle\Theta(\exp(tX_{1})k)

\displaystyle=|\alpha\cos t-i\bar{\beta}\sin t|^{2}-|{\beta}\cos t+i\bar{\alpha}\sin t|^{2}

\displaystyle=(|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2})(\cos^{2}t-\sin^{2}t)+2i(\alpha\beta-\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta})\sin t\cos t.

Therefore,
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Psi(\exp(tX_{1})k)|_{t=0}=-i(\alpha^{2}-\bar{\alpha}^{2}-\beta^{2}+\bar{\beta}^{2})
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Theta(\exp(tX_{1})k)|_{t=0}=2i(\alpha\beta-\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta})

The calculations for
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
are similar.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

The Hessian of
\widetilde{F}
at
(0,0,0,0)
is

\displaystyle D_{\rho}=\begin{pmatrix}-(1-\rho)&0&0&-2\\
0&-(1-\rho^{2})&-2\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}&0\\
0&-2\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}&0&0\\
-2&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

so its determinant is

\displaystyle\det D_{\rho}=16(1-\rho^{2}).

Proof.

To calculate
\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}/\partial x^{2}
and
\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}/\partial x\partial t
, we define the map
v:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow K_{0}
by the condition that
k_{\rho}n(x)\in NAv(x)
, i.e.
v(x)=\kappa(k_{\rho}n(x))
. Lemma
5.6
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{F}}{\partial x}(x,0,0,0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(k_{\rho}n(x))=\Psi(v(x)),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{F}}{\partial t}(x,0,0,0)=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(A(k_{\rho}n(x)a(t))-\rho t\right)\right|_{t=0}=\Theta(v(x))-\rho.

By Lemma
5.9
,

\displaystyle v(x)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(k_{\rho}n(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\rho}&i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
i\sqrt{1-\rho}&\sqrt{1+\rho}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\rho}&x\sqrt{1+\rho}+i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
i\sqrt{1-\rho}&\sqrt{1+\rho}+ix\sqrt{1-\rho}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\rho}-ix\sqrt{1-\rho}&i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
i\sqrt{1-\rho}&\sqrt{1+\rho}+ix\sqrt{1-\rho}\end{pmatrix},

so

\Psi(v(x))=\frac{-2(1-\rho)x}{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2},

and

\Theta(v(x))=\frac{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2\rho}{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}}{\partial x^{2}}(0,0,0,0)=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Psi(v(x))\right|_{x=0}=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{-2(1-\rho)x}{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2}\right)\right|_{x=0}=-(1-\rho),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}}{\partial x\partial t}(0,0,0,0)=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\Theta(v(x))-\rho\right)\right|_{x=0}=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2\rho}{(1-\rho)x^{2}+2}-\rho\right)\right|_{x=0}=0.

To calculate
\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}/\partial t^{2}
, we define a map
w:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow K_{0}
by the condition that
k_{\rho}a(t)\in NAw(t)
, i.e.,
w(t)=\kappa(k_{\rho}a(t))
. Lemma
5.6
gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{F}}{\partial t}(0,t,0,0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(A(k_{\rho}a(t))-\rho t\right)=\Theta(w(t))-\rho.

By Lemma
5.9
,

\displaystyle w(t)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(k_{\rho}a(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{1+\rho}&i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
i\sqrt{1-\rho}&\sqrt{1+\rho}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}e^{t/2}&0\\
0&e^{-t/2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{t/2}\sqrt{1+\rho}&e^{-t/2}i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
e^{t/2}i\sqrt{1-\rho}&e^{-t/2}\sqrt{1+\rho}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-\rho)e^{t}+(1+\rho)e^{-t}}}\begin{pmatrix}e^{-t/2}\sqrt{1+\rho}&e^{t/2}i\sqrt{1-\rho}\\
e^{t/2}i\sqrt{1-\rho}&e^{-t/2}\sqrt{1+\rho}\end{pmatrix},

and so

\displaystyle\Theta(w(t))=\frac{(1+\rho)e^{-t}-(1-\rho)e^{t}}{(1+\rho)e^{-t}+(1-\rho)e^{t}}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}}{\partial t^{2}}(0,0,0,0)=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Theta(w(t))\right|_{t=0}=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(\frac{(1+\rho)e^{-t}-(1-\rho)e^{t}}{(1+\rho)e^{-t}+(1-\rho)e^{t}}\right)\right|_{t=0}=-(1-\rho^{2}).

To calculate
\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}/\partial s_{i}\partial x
and
\partial^{2}\widetilde{F}/\partial s_{i}\partial t
, by Lemma
5.6
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{F}}{\partial x}(0,0,s_{1},s_{2})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho}n(x))\right|_{x=0}=\Psi(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho}),

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\widetilde{F}}{\partial t}(0,0,s_{1},s_{2})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(A(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho}a(t))-\rho t\right)\right|_{t=0}=\Theta(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho})-\rho.

Lemma
5.10
implies that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s_{1}\partial x},\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s_{2}\partial x}\right)\widetilde{F}(0,0,0,0)=\left(0,-2\right)

and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s_{1}\partial t},\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial s_{2}\partial t}\right)\widetilde{F}(0,0,0,0)=\left(-2\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}},0\right).

We have
\partial^{2}{\widetilde{F}}/\partial s_{i}\partial s_{j}(0,0,0,0)=0
because
\widetilde{F}(0,0,s_{1},s_{2})=A(\exp(s_{1}X_{1}+s_{2}X_{2})k_{\rho})\equiv 0
.
∎

Therefore, the critical point is non-degenerate uniformly in the parameter
\rho
, provided
\rho\in[0,\delta]
for a fixed
0<\delta<1
.
The method of stationary phase implies

\displaystyle J(r;\rho)=\widetilde{J}(r;\rho)+O_{N}(r^{-N})\ll r^{-2}.

5.3.
Bound of
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)
for big
s

It remains to prove the inequality (
5.10
).
We assume that
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
and
s\notin I_{\beta/4}
satisfying
s\geq\lambda/2
. Recall the expansion (
5.11
) of
\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}
under the polar coordinates. We unfold that integral by the inverse Harish-Chandra transform to obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}(s)

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{\infty}k_{\lambda}(a(t))b_{1}(t)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}h_{\lambda}(r)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))d\nu(r)\right)b_{1}(t)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r).

Recall that
b_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
b_{1}(t)k_{\lambda}(a(t))=k_{\lambda}(a(t))
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
b_{1}\equiv 1
on
[-1,1]
and vanishes outside
[-2,2]
.
Since
h_{\lambda}(r)
concentrates around
\pm\lambda
and rapidly decays outside, it suffices to show the bound

(5.17)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt\ll\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}

uniformly for
s\notin I_{\beta/4}
satisfying
s\geq\lambda/2
, and
|r-\lambda|\leq\beta/8
.
First, we decompose the integral as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt=I_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I_{n}

where

\displaystyle I_{0}=\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)b_{1}(\beta t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt,

and

\displaystyle I_{n}=\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt.

The bounds in
[
30
, Theorem 1.3]
implies that

(5.18)

\displaystyle|b_{1}(t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))|\ll\left(1+|rt|\right)^{-1},

and

(5.19)

\displaystyle|b_{1}(t)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))|\ll\left(1+|st|\right)^{-1/2}.

Recall that we assume
b_{1}
is supported in
[-2,2]
.
The bounds (
5.18
), (
5.19
) and the fact
\sinh(x)=x+O(x^{3})
imply

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2/\beta}b_{1}(t)b_{1}(\beta t)\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt

\displaystyle\ll\int_{0}^{2/\beta}b_{1}(\beta t)\left(1+rt\right)^{-1}\left(1+st\right)^{-1/2}tdt

\displaystyle\ll r^{-1}s^{-1/2}\int_{0}^{2/\beta}b_{1}(\beta t)t^{-1/2}dt

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-3/2}\int_{0}^{2}b_{1}(t)\left(\frac{t}{\beta}\right)^{-1/2}\frac{dt}{\beta}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}.

To bound
I_{n}
, we use the exponential asymptotic formulas for
\varphi_{r}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(t))
and
\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))
given in
[
30
, Theorem 1.5]
. There are functions
f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{\pm},\,f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{\pm}\in C^{\infty}\left((0,3)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\right)
such that

(5.20)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{\pm}(t,r)

\displaystyle\ll_{n}t^{-n}(rt)^{-1}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{\pm}(t,s)

\displaystyle\ll_{n}t^{-n}(st)^{-1/2}

and

(5.22)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{r}(a(t))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(t,r)e^{irt}+f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{-}(t,r)e^{-irt}+O_{N}\left((rt)^{-N}\right)

(5.23)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{s}(a(t))

\displaystyle=f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{+}(t,s)e^{ist}+f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(t,s)e^{-ist}+O_{N}\left((st)^{-N}\right)

for
t\in(0,3)
and
r,s\geq 0
.
We substitute
\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{s}
in
I_{n}
via (
5.22
). The error term in (
5.22
) makes a contribution of

\displaystyle\ll_{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)(rt)^{-N}(1+st)^{-1/2}tdt

\displaystyle\ll_{N}(\lambda\beta^{-1}2^{n})^{-N}(\lambda\beta^{-1}2^{n})^{-1/2}(\beta^{-1}2^{n})^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-N-1/2}\beta^{N-3/2}2^{-nN+3n/2}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-5/2}\beta^{1/2}2^{-n/2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}2^{-n/2}

to
I_{n}
. Thus, summing over
n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)\left(f_{+}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(t,r)e^{irt}+f_{-}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(t,r)e^{-irt}\right)\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(a(t))\sinh(t)dt

\displaystyle+O(\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}).

We now substitute
\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-s}=\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{s}
via (
5.23
) in
I_{n}
. Because of the bound (
5.20
), the error term in (
5.23
) makes a contribution of

\displaystyle\ll_{N}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)(st)^{-N}(rt)^{-1}tdt

\displaystyle\ll_{N}(\lambda\beta^{-1}2^{n})^{-N}(\beta^{-1}2^{n})^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-N}\beta^{N-2}2^{-nN+2n}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}2^{-n/2}

to
I_{n}
. After summing over
n
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}I_{n}=O(\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)

\displaystyle\left(f_{+}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(t,r)e^{irt}+f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{-}(t,r)e^{-irt}\right)

\displaystyle\left(f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{+}(t,s)e^{ist}+f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(t,s)e^{-ist}\right)\sinh(t)dt.

We shall only consider the term involving
f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(t,r)f_{-}^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}(t,s)
, as the other terms are similar. We must then estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)\left(b_{1}(2^{-n}\beta t)-b_{1}(2^{-n+1}\beta t)\right)f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(t,r)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(t,s)e^{i(r-s)t}tdt.

If we replace
t
with
2^{n}\beta^{-1}t
, this becomes

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}2^{2n}\beta^{-2}b_{1}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t)\left(b_{1}(t)-b_{1}(2t)\right)f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)e^{i(r-s)2^{n}\beta^{-1}t}tdt.

The conditions
s\notin I_{\beta/4}
and
|r-\lambda|\leq\beta/8
imply that
|(r-s)2^{n}\beta^{-1}|\geq 2^{n-3}
. Also (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) imply that

(5.24)

\displaystyle f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)\ll\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{3/2}2^{-3n/2}t^{-3/2},

and

(5.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)\right)\\
=&\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)\right)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)\ +f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)\right)\\
\ll&\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{3/2}2^{-3n/2}t^{-5/2}.\end{split}

Integrating by parts once, and then applying the bounds (
5.24
) and (
5.25
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}2^{2n}\beta^{-2}b_{1}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t)\left(b_{1}(t)-b_{1}(2t)\right)f^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{+}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,r)f^{\mathbb{H}^{2}}_{-}(2^{n}\beta^{-1}t,s)e^{i(r-s)2^{n}\beta^{-1}t}tdt

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|(r-s)2^{n}\beta^{-1}|}\left(2^{2n}\beta^{-2}\right)\left(\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{3/2}2^{-3n/2}\right)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\lambda^{-3/2}\beta^{-1/2}2^{-n/2},

and summing over
n
completes the proof of (
5.10
).

6.
Estimates of oscillatory integrals

6.1.
Lemmas on the function
A

We begin with some results we shall use repeatedly in this section.
For
g\in G_{0}
, let
\Phi_{g}:K_{0}\rightarrow K_{0}
be the map sending
k
to
\kappa(kg)
, i.e
kg\in NA\Phi_{g}(k)
.

Lemma 6.1
.

\Phi
is a smooth group action of
G_{0}
on
K_{0}
from right.

Moreover,
\Phi_{g}
induces a diffeomorphism from
M\backslash K_{0}
to itself.

Proof.

The smoothness of the Iwasawa decomposition implies that
\Phi_{g}
is smooth and depends smoothly on
g
. We identify
K_{0}
with the quotient
NA\backslash G_{0}
via the Iwasawa decomposition. Then
\Phi_{g}
is obtained by composing the diffeomorhpism
K_{0}\rightarrow NA\backslash G_{0}
, the right multiplication by
\cdot g:NA\backslash G_{0}\rightarrow NA\backslash G_{0}
and the diffeomorphism
NA\backslash G_{0}\rightarrow K_{0}
.
It remains to show
\Phi_{gh}=\Phi_{h}\circ\Phi_{g}
for
g,h\in G_{0}
. By definition

\displaystyle kgh\in NA\Phi_{gh}(k)

\displaystyle kg\in NA\Phi_{g}(k),

which implies

\displaystyle NA\Phi_{g}(k)h=NA\Phi_{gh}(k),

\displaystyle\Phi_{g}(k)h\in NA\Phi_{gh}(k),

that is
\Phi_{h}\left(\Phi_{g}(k)\right)=\Phi_{gh}(k)
.

The second statement follows from the fact that
M
normalizes
NA
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
y,z\in G_{0}
and let
k\in K_{0}
. Then we have

\displaystyle A(ky^{-1}z)=A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)z\right)-A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)y\right).

Proof.

Let
ky^{-1}=na\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)
. We have

\displaystyle A(ky^{-1}z)

\displaystyle=A\left(na\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)z\right)

\displaystyle=A(na)+A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)z\right)

\displaystyle=A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)z\right)-A\left((na)^{-1}k\right)

\displaystyle=A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)z\right)-A\left(\Phi_{y^{-1}}(k)y\right).

∎

Recall that the tangent space of
M\backslash K_{0}
at the origin
M
can be viewed as a two-dimensional subspace of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{k}
spanned by the vectors
X_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}
and
X_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
.
Let
U_{\mathfrak{k}}
be a circle centered at 0 in the tangent space defined by

(6.1)

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{k}}=\left\{r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2}:r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}=1,r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

For a given
C>0
, we let

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{C}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<C\}

be the open disc of radius
C
in
\mathbb{C}
.
We shall need the following uniformization lemma for the function
A
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
C>0
.
There exists
\delta,\sigma>0
depending on
C
, and a real analytic function

\displaystyle\xi:(-\delta,\delta)\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(rX)n(z)a(t))=1-r^{2}\xi(r,X,z,t),

and

(6.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|\xi(r,X,z,t)|&\geq\sigma,\\
\left|\frac{\partial^{n}\xi}{\partial t^{n}}(r,X,z,t)\right|&\ll_{C,n}1,\end{split}

for
(r,X,z,t)\in(-\delta,\delta)\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)
.

Proof.

Let
\delta_{0}>0
be a small number so that the exponential map from the set

\displaystyle U_{\mathfrak{k}}(\delta_{0}):=\{rX\,|\,r\in(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}),X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}\}

onto its image in
K_{0}
is a diffeomorphism.
Moreover,
\exp(U_{\mathfrak{k}}(\delta_{0}))
is transversal to
M
at the origin.
Hence, we may think of it as a neighborhood near the origin in
M\backslash K_{0}
.
We define the functions

\displaystyle\alpha,\,\beta:(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

by requiring that

\displaystyle\exp(rX)n(z)a(t)\in NAk(\alpha(r,X,z,t),\beta(r,X,z,t)),

where

\displaystyle k(\alpha,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}.

The analyticity of the Iwasawa decomposition implies that
\alpha
and
\beta
are analytic, and in particular
|\alpha|^{2}
and
|\beta|^{2}
are analytic, as functions of
(rX,z,t)\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}(\delta_{0})\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)
.
(
5.15
) in Lemma
5.6
implies that

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(rX)n(z)a(t))=1-\Theta(k(\alpha,\beta))=(1-|\alpha|^{2})+|\beta|^{2}=2|\beta|^{2},

which is equal to
0
if and only if
\beta=0
. This is equivalent to
k(\alpha,\beta)\in M
.
By Lemma
6.1
, this is also equivalent to
\exp(rX)\in M
.
By the transversality,
we can choose
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
such that
\beta(r,X,z,t)
vanishes on
(-\delta,\delta)\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)
if and only if
r=0
.

Lemma
6.1
implies that
\partial\beta/\partial r
is analytic and never vanishes on
\{0\}\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)
.
It may be seen that there is a real analytic function
\xi_{0}
on
(-\delta,\delta)\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)
such that
\beta(r,X,z,t)=r\xi_{0}(r,X,z,t)
. Defining
\xi=2|\xi_{0}|^{2}
gives the result. ∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
C,\delta>0
. There exists
\sigma>0
depending on
C
and
\delta
such that

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(kn(z)a(t))\geq\sigma,

for arbitrary
k\in K_{0}
with
d(k,M)\geq\delta
,
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
and
t\in(-C,C)
.

Proof.

We fix the element
k\in K_{0}
with
d(k,M)\geq\delta
.
Define the functions
\alpha,\beta:\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C,C)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by requiring that

\displaystyle kn(z)a(t)\in NAk(\alpha(z,t),\beta(z,t)),

i.e.

\displaystyle k(\alpha(z,t),\beta(z,t))=\Phi_{n(z)a(t)}(k).

Here

\displaystyle k(\alpha,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
d(k,M)\geq\delta
and
\Phi_{n(z)a(t)}
acts trivially on
M
for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
and
t\in(-C,C)
,
by Lemma
6.1
, there is some
C_{1}>0
such that
d(\Phi_{n(z)a(t)}(k),M)>C_{1}\delta
. Hence,
|\beta(z,t)|>C_{2}\delta
for some other constant
C_{2}>0
, for any
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
and
t\in(-C,C)
.
Lemma
5.6
(
5.15
) implies that

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(kn(z)a(t))=1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(k(\alpha,\beta)a(t))|_{t=0}=(1-|\alpha|^{2})+|\beta|^{2}=2|\beta|^{2}>2C_{2}^{2}\delta^{2}.

Then the result follows by taking
\sigma=2C_{2}^{2}\delta^{2}
.
∎

The above two lemmas, in particular, hold for
n:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow N
restricted to
\mathbb{R}\rightarrow N_{0}
and for
B
instead of
M\backslash K_{0}
. Notice that we have the embedding
B=\{\pm I\}\backslash\operatorname{SO}(2)\hookrightarrow M\backslash K_{0}
with
\operatorname{SO}(2)\subset K_{0}=\operatorname{SU}(2)
and
\operatorname{SO}(2)\cap M=\{\pm I\}
.
Therefore, we obtain the following corollary, which we have proved by a direct formula for the
A
function on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
in Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
5.2
.

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
C>0
. There exists
\delta,\sigma>0
depending on
C
, and a real analytic function

\displaystyle\xi:(-\delta,\delta)\times(-C,C)^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=1-\theta^{2}\xi(\theta,x,t),

and

\displaystyle|\xi(\theta,x,t)|

\displaystyle\geq\sigma,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial^{n}\xi}{\partial t^{n}}(\theta,x,t)\right|

\displaystyle\ll_{C,n}1,

for
(\theta,x,t)\in(-\delta,\delta)\times(-C,C)^{2}
. Moreover, if
\theta\notin(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
and
x,t\in(-C,C)
, then

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\geq\sigma.

6.2.
Oscillatory integrals for geodesic tubes

We first estimate two one-dimensional integrals, which can be thought of as the integrals along a geodesic.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
B,C,D>0
and
\epsilon_{0}>0
be constants.
Let
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function supported in
[-1,1]
, and let
\rho
be a smooth function defined on
(-1,1)
.
If
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}\subset\mathbb{C}
,
t\in(-C,C)
,
k\in K_{0}
,
s^{\prime}>0
satisfying

(6.3)

\displaystyle d(k,M)\geq Bs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\beta

for some
s,\beta\geq 1
with
\beta\leq 3s^{1/4}
, and

(6.4)

\displaystyle\left|s^{\prime}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right|\leq Ds^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|s^{\prime}\frac{\partial^{n}\rho}{\partial t^{\prime n}}(t^{\prime})\right|\ll_{n}s^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ for }t^{\prime}\in(-1,1),

then

(6.5)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))-isA(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dt^{\prime}\ll s^{-A}.

The implied constant depends on
A,B,C,D,\epsilon_{0}
, the first
n
implied constants in (
6.4
), and the size of the first
n
derivatives of
\chi_{0}
, where
n
depends on
\epsilon_{0}
and
A
.

Proof.

By applying Lemma
6.3
, we see that there is a
\delta>0
and a nonvanishing real analytic function
\xi
on
(-\delta,\delta)\times U_{\mathfrak{k}}\times\mathbb{D}_{C}\times(-C-2,C+2)
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(\exp(rX)n(z)a(t))=1-r^{2}\xi(r,X,z,t),

when
r\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}
,
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
and
t\in(-C-2,C+2)
. If
Z(r,X,z,t)
is an antiderivative of
\xi
with respect to
t
that is smooth as a function of
(r,X,z,t)
, we may integrate this to obtain

\displaystyle A(\exp(rX)n(z)a(t))=t-r^{2}Z(r,X,z,t)+c(r,X,z)

for some function
c(r,X,z)
.

If
k=\exp(rX)
for some
r\in(-\delta,\delta)
and
X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}
, we may use this to rewrite the integral (
6.5
) as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))-isA(\exp(rX)n(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))-is(t+t^{\prime})+isr^{2}Z(r,X,z,t+t^{\prime})-isc(r,X,z)\right)dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-is(t+c(r,X,z))}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(i(s^{\prime}-s)t^{\prime}+is^{\prime}\rho(t^{\prime})+isr^{2}Z(r,X,z,t+t^{\prime})\right)dt^{\prime}

(6.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-is(t+c(r,X,z))}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(isr^{2}\Psi(t^{\prime})\right)dt^{\prime},

where we define

\displaystyle\Psi(t^{\prime})=Z(r,X,z,t+t^{\prime})+s^{-1}r^{-2}(s^{\prime}-s)t^{\prime}+s^{-1}r^{-2}s^{\prime}\rho(t^{\prime}).

Our assumption (
6.3
) implies that

r\geq B_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
for some constant
B_{1}>0
, and

\displaystyle|s^{-1}r^{-2}(s^{\prime}-s)|\leq s^{-1}\left(B_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}\right)^{-2}\beta=B_{1}^{-2}s^{-2\epsilon_{0}}.

The assumption (
6.4
) implies that

\displaystyle\left|s^{-1}r^{-2}s^{\prime}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right|\leq s^{-1}\left(B_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}\right)^{-2}Ds^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta=B_{1}^{-2}Ds^{-\epsilon_{0}}.

Hence,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi&=Z(r,X,z,t+t^{\prime})+s^{-1}r^{-2}s^{\prime}\rho(t^{\prime})+O\left(s^{-2\epsilon_{0}}\right)t^{\prime},\quad\text{ and }\\
\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial t^{\prime}}&=\xi(r,X,z,t+t^{\prime})+O\left(s^{-\epsilon_{0}}\right).\end{split}

It follows from (
6.2
) and (
6.7
) that there exists a
\sigma>0
so that, for
s
sufficiently large,
|\partial\Psi/\partial t^{\prime}|>\sigma/2
for all
r\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}
,
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
,
t\in(-C,C)
and
t^{\prime}\in(-1,-1)
.
In addition, all higher derivatives of
\Psi
are bounded from above. As (
6.3
) implies that

\displaystyle sr^{2}\geq B_{1}^{2}s^{2\epsilon_{0}}\beta\geq B_{1}^{2}s^{2\epsilon_{0}},

the bound (
6.5
) follows by integration by parts in (
6.6
).

In the case where
k
can not be written as
\exp(rX)
for
r\in(-\delta,\delta)
, we can assume
d(k,M)
to be bounded below by some constant multiple of
\delta
, so Lemma
6.4
implies that

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\prime}}A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\geq C_{1}

for some
C_{1}>0
. It gives

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\prime}}\left(s^{\prime}s^{-1}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))-A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\prime}}A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)-\left|s^{\prime}s^{-1}-1\right|-\left|s^{\prime}s^{-1}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,C_{1}-\beta s^{-1}-s^{-1}Ds^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta

\displaystyle\gg

\displaystyle 1.

The result also follows by integration by parts.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
C>0
and
1/4>\delta>0
. There exists
C_{1}>0
depending on
C
, such that

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{2}}\right)A(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))\right|

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{i}}A(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle|z|,

for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
with
|z|\geq\delta
,
t\in(-C,C)
, and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(-C_{1}\delta,C_{1}\delta)
, where the implied constants are absolute. Here
X_{1},X_{2}\in\mathfrak{k}
are defined as (
5.16
).

Proof.

By computing the matrix exponential directly, we obtain

(6.8)

\displaystyle\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(r)&(ir_{1}-r_{2})\sin(r)/r\\
(ir_{1}+r_{2})\sin(r)/r&\cos(r)\end{pmatrix},

where
r=\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}}
. It may be seen from (
5.13
) that

\displaystyle A\left(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t-\log\left(\cos^{2}(r)+\sin^{2}(r)(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right)\right).

If we fix
\delta_{0}>0
sufficiently small, for
r_{1},r_{2}\in(-\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
,
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
and
t\in(-C,C)
,
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{1}}\left(\cos^{2}(r)+\sin^{2}(r)(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{1}}{r}+2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{1}}{r}(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left(-iz+i\bar{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{r(\cos^{2}(r)-\sin^{2}(r))-\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r^{2}}\frac{r_{1}}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right).

Since
\sin(r)=r+O(r^{3})
,
\cos(r)=1+O(r^{2})
and
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
are
\ll r
, we have

\displaystyle-2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{1}}{r}+2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{1}}{r}(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})\ll r,

\displaystyle-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left(-iz+i\bar{z}\right)=i(z-\overline{z})+O(r^{2}).

Because
r(\cos^{2}(r)-\sin^{2}(r))-\sin(r)\cos(r)\ll r^{3}
,

\displaystyle-\frac{r(\cos^{2}(r)-\sin^{2}(r))-\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r^{2}}\frac{r_{1}}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right)\ll r^{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{1}}A\left(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t)\right)=-i(z-\bar{z})+O(r)=2\operatorname{Im}(z)+O(r).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{2}}\left(\cos^{2}(r)+\sin^{2}(r)(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{2}}{r}+2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{2}}{r}(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left(-z-\bar{z}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{r(\cos^{2}(r)-\sin^{2}(r))-\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r^{2}}\frac{r_{2}}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right).

and we have the following estimates

\displaystyle-2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{2}}{r}+2\sin(r)\cos(r)\frac{r_{2}}{r}(|z|^{2}+e^{2t})\ll r,

\displaystyle-\frac{\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r}\left(-z-\bar{z}\right)=(z+\overline{z})+O(r^{2}),

\displaystyle-\frac{r(\cos^{2}(r)-\sin^{2}(r))-\sin(r)\cos(r)}{r^{2}}\frac{r_{2}}{r}\left((-ir_{1}-r_{2})z+(ir_{1}-r_{2})\bar{z}\right)\ll r^{2}

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{2}}A\left(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t)\right)=-(z+\bar{z})+O(r)=-2\operatorname{Re}(z)+O(r).

We pick
C_{1}
small enough so that

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{2}}\right)A\left(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t)\right)\right|=2|z|+O(r)\asymp|z|

for
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
with
|z|\geq\delta
,
t\in(-C,C)
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in(-C_{1}\delta,C_{1}\delta)
.
∎

The second integral we want to estimate includes the spherical functions on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
B,C,D>0
and
1/8>\epsilon_{0}>0
be constants.
Let
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function supported in
[-1,1]
,
and let
\rho
be a smooth function defined on
(-1,1)
.
If
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
,
t\in(-C,C)
and
s^{\prime}>0
satisfying

(6.9)

\displaystyle|z|\geq Bs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}\;\text{ and }\;|s^{\prime}-s|\leq\beta

for some
s,\beta\geq 1
with
\beta\leq 3s^{1/4}
, and

(6.10)

\displaystyle\left|s^{\prime}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\right|\leq Ds^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|s^{\prime}\frac{\partial^{n}\rho}{\partial t^{\prime n}}(t^{\prime})\right|\ll_{n}s^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ for }t^{\prime}\in(-1,1),

then

(6.11)

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))\right)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\left(n(z)a(t+t^{\prime})\right)dt^{\prime}\ll s^{-A}.

The implied constant depends on
A,B,C,D,\epsilon_{0}
, the first
n
implied constants in (
6.10
), and the size of the first
n
derivatives of
\chi_{0}
, where
n
depends on
\epsilon_{0}
and
A
.

Proof.

If we apply the functional equation
\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{s}=\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{-s}
, and substitute
\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{-s}
by the integral formula (
3.2
) into the left-hand side of (
6.11
), it becomes

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{K_{0}}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))+(1-is)A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dkdt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))+(1-is)A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dkdt^{\prime}.

Let
C_{1}>0
be a constant to be chosen later. Let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(M\backslash K_{0})
be such that
0\leq\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\leq 1
,
\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}\equiv 1
, and

\displaystyle\supp(\chi_{1})\subset\left\{Mk\in M\backslash K_{0}\,|\,k=\exp(rX),r\in[-2C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2},2C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}],X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\},

\displaystyle\supp(\chi_{2})\subset M\backslash K_{0}-\left\{Mk\in M\backslash K_{0}\,|\,k=\exp(rX),r\in[-C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2},C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}],X\in U_{\mathfrak{k}}\right\}.

Proposition
6.6
implies that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{2}(k)\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))+(1-is)A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dkdt^{\prime}\ll_{A}s^{-A}.

Hence, it suffices to estimate

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{1}(k)\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))+(1-is)A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dkdt^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{1}(k)\exp\left((1-is)A(kn(z)a(t+t^{\prime}))\right)dk\right)\chi_{0}(t^{\prime})\exp\left(is^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\rho(t^{\prime}))\right)dt^{\prime}.

We shall do this by estimating the inner integral above. That is,

\displaystyle\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{1}(k)\exp\left((1-is)A(kn(z)a(t))\right)dk

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widetilde{\chi_{1}}(r_{1},r_{2})\exp\left(-isA(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))\right)dr_{1}dr_{2}.

Here
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
,
|z|\geq Bs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
and
t\in(-C-1,C+1)
, and
\widetilde{\chi_{1}}(r_{1},r_{2})=\widetilde{\chi_{1}}(r_{1},r_{2};z,t)
is the smooth compactly supported function obtained by combining all of the amplitude factors. Moreover,

\displaystyle\supp(\widetilde{\chi_{1}})\subset\left\{(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,r=(r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\in[-2C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2},{2}C_{1}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}]\right\}.

The proposition now follows by applying the following Lemma to
\delta=Bs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
and choosing
C_{1}
to be sufficiently small.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
C>0
be a constant and let
1>\delta>0
. Then there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that the following holds. Suppose that
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a smooth function supported in the unit ball centered at the origin
(0,0)
.
If
t\in(-C,C)
,
z\in\mathbb{D}_{C}
satisfying
|z|\geq\delta,

then

\displaystyle\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}\left((C_{1}\delta)^{-1}(r_{1},r_{2})\right)\exp\left(isA(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))\right)dr_{1}dr_{2}\ll\delta^{2}(s\delta^{2})^{-A}.

The implied constant depends only on
A,C
, and the size of the first
n
derivatives of
\chi_{0}
, where
n
depends on
A
.

Proof.

Lemma
6.7
implies that if
C_{1}
is chosen to be sufficiently small then

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{1}},\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{2}}\right)A(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))\right|\geq|z|\geq\delta,

when
|z|,|t|<C
,
|z|\geq\delta
and
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\supp(\chi_{0}((C_{1}\delta)^{-1}\cdot))
. It is clear that all the derivatives of
A(\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})n(z)a(t))
with respect to
r_{1},r_{2}
are bounded by the compactness. The lemma now follows from the following Lemma
6.10
.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

If
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a cutoff function at scale
1\geq\delta_{1}>0
, and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is real-valued and satisfies

\displaystyle\nabla\phi(x)\gg\delta_{2}>0,\;\;\phi^{(n)}(x)\ll_{n}1

for
x\in\supp(\chi)
and
n>1
. If
\delta_{1}\ll\delta_{2}
, then

\displaystyle\int\chi(x)e^{is\phi(x)}dx\ll_{A}\delta_{1}^{2}(s\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-A}.

In particular, if
\delta_{1}\asymp\delta_{2}\asymp\delta
, then

\displaystyle\int\chi(x)e^{is\phi(x)}dx\ll_{A}\delta^{2}(s\delta^{2})^{-A}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int\chi(x)e^{is\phi(x)}dx=\delta_{1}^{2}\int\chi(\delta_{1}x)e^{i(s\delta_{1}\delta_{2})(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1}\phi(\delta_{1}x)}dx.

The function
\chi(\delta_{1}x)
is now a cutoff function at scale
1
, and
(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1}\phi(\delta_{1}x)
satisfies

\displaystyle\nabla\left((\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1}\phi(\delta_{1}x)\right)\gg 1.

Since
\delta_{2}\gg\delta_{1}
, the
n
-th derivatives are bounded
when
x\in\supp(\chi(\delta_{1}\cdot))
for all
n>1
. The lemma now follows by integration by parts.
Note that here we are not putting a bound on the first derivative of the phase function
(\delta_{1}\delta_{2})^{-1}\phi(\delta_{1}x)
. This is because the first derivative will only appear on the denominator after integrating by parts.
∎

Let
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function supported in
[-1,1]
,
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
two real-valued smooth functions defined on
(-1,1)
,
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in G_{0}
. We define

(6.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&J(s,s_{1},s_{2},g;\chi_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\\
=&\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_{0}(t_{1})\chi_{0}(t_{2})\exp\left({-is_{1}(t_{1}+\rho_{1}(t_{1}))+is_{2}(t_{2}+\rho_{2}(t_{2}))}\right)\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\left(a(-t_{1})ga(t_{2})\right)dt_{1}dt_{2}.\end{split}

The main result of this section is the following estimate of the oscillatory integral
J(s,s_{1},s_{2},g;\chi_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
. We now combine the one-dimensional results Proposition
6.6
and
6.8
to prove it.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
B_{0},C_{0},D>0
and
1/8>\epsilon_{0}>0
be given. If
g\in G_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle d(g,e)\leq C_{0}\quad\text{ and }\quad d(g,MA)\geq B_{0}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}

for some
s,\beta\geq 1
satisfying
\beta\leq 3s^{1/4}
, and

(6.13)

\displaystyle\left|s_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{j}}{\partial t_{j}}(t_{j})\right|\leq Ds^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ and }\quad\left|s_{j}\frac{\partial^{n}\rho_{j}}{\partial t_{j}^{n}}(t_{j})\right|\ll_{n}s^{\epsilon_{0}}\beta,\quad\text{ for }t_{j}\in(-1,1),\;j=1,2,

and
s_{1},s_{2}\in[s-\beta,s+\beta]
, then

(6.14)

\displaystyle J(s,s_{1},s_{2},g;\chi_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\ll s^{-A}.

The implied constant depends on
A,B_{0},C_{0},D,\epsilon_{0}
, the first
n
implied constants in (
6.13
), and the size of the first
n
derivatives of
\chi_{0}
, where
n
depends on
\epsilon_{0}
and
A
.

Proof.

We define the map
u:K_{0}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow K_{0}
by
ka(-t_{1})\in NAu(k,t_{1})
, i.e.,
u(k,t_{1})=\kappa(ka(-t_{1}))
. By Lemma
6.1
, for each fixed
t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
u(\cdot,t_{1})
is a diffeomorphism from
K_{0}
onto itself and induces a diffeomorphism from
M\backslash K_{0}
onto itself. If we apply the functional equation
\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}=\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
, write
\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
as an integral over
K_{0}
by the integral formula (
3.2
), and apply Lemma
6.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\left(a(-t_{1})ga(t_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\exp\big((1-is)(A\left(ka(-t_{1})ga(t_{2})\right)\big)dk

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\exp\big((1-is)\left(A\left(u(k,t_{1})ga(t_{2})\right)-A\left(u(k,t_{1})a(t_{1})\right)\right)\big)dk

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\exp\big((1-is)\left(A\left(uga(t_{2})\right)-A\left(ua(t_{1})\right)\right)\big)\left|\det\frac{\partial k}{\partial u}\right|du.

Here we use
\partial u/\partial k
to denote the Jacobian matrix of the diffeomorphism
u(\cdot,t_{1}):M\backslash K_{0}\rightarrow M\backslash K_{0}
, and
\partial k/\partial u
is the inverse matrix.
Substituting this into the definition (
6.12
) of
J(s,s_{1},s_{2},g;\chi_{0},\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
gives

(6.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\chi_{0}(t_{1})\chi_{0}(t_{2})&\exp\left({-is_{1}(t_{1}+\rho_{1}(t_{1}))+is_{2}(t_{2}+\rho_{2}(t_{2}))}\right)\\
&\exp\big((1-is)\left(A\left(uga(t_{2})\right)-A\left(ua(t_{1})\right)\right)\big)\left|\det\frac{\partial k}{\partial u}\right|dudt_{1}dt_{2}.\end{split}

Let
g=k^{\prime}n(z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})
with
k^{\prime}\in K_{0}
,
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
and
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.
The condition
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
implies that
z^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
are bounded.
Choose a constant
C>0
.
If
d(u,M)\geq Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
, then integrating (
6.15
) in
t_{1}
and applying Proposition
6.6
shows that the integral of (
6.15
) over
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
with this
u
is
\ll_{C,A,\epsilon_{0}}s^{-A}
.
And if
d(u,M)<Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
but
d(k^{\prime},M)\geq C_{1}Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
for some constant
C_{1}>0
independent of the choice of
C
such that
d(uk^{\prime},M)\geq Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
, then we obtain the same conclusion by integrating in
t_{2}
.
Combining these, we see that (
6.15
) will be
\ll_{C,A,\epsilon_{0}}s^{-A}
unless
d(k^{\prime},M)<C_{1}Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
, and we assume that this is the case.

If
C
is chosen sufficiently small, the condition
d(k^{\prime},M)<C_{1}Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
and our assumption that
d(g,MA)\geq B_{0}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
imply that
|z^{\prime}|\geq C_{2}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
for some
C_{2}>0
depending only on
B_{0}
.
For
-1\leq t_{1}\leq 1
, we define
k^{\prime}(t_{1})\in K_{0}
,
z^{\prime}(t_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
and
t^{\prime}(t_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle a(-t_{1})g=a(-t_{1})k^{\prime}n(z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})=k^{\prime}(t_{1})n(z^{\prime}(t_{1}))a(t^{\prime}(t_{1})),

so that

\displaystyle a(-t_{1})k^{\prime}=k^{\prime}(t_{1})n(z^{\prime}(t_{1})-e^{t^{\prime}(t_{1})-t^{\prime}}z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime}(t_{1})-t^{\prime}).

Since
d(a(-t_{1})k^{\prime},MA)<C_{1}Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
, it may be seen that

\displaystyle\left|z^{\prime}(t_{1})-e^{t^{\prime}(t_{1})-t^{\prime}}z^{\prime}\right|<C_{3}Cs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}

for some
C_{3}>0
.
It follows from
|z^{\prime}|\geq C_{2}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
that if
C
is sufficiently small,
we have
|z^{\prime}(t_{1})|\geq C_{4}s^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
for some
C_{4}>0
and for all
-1\leq t_{1}\leq 1
.
The result now follows by applying Proposition
6.8
to the integral (
6.12
) for each fixed
t_{1}
.
∎

7.
Geodesics in
\mathbb{H}^{3}

Let
l
be the image of
A
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, which is the vertical geodesic through the origin.
We choose an orientation for
l
so that the upward orientation of
l
is positive.
Given
T,\delta>0
, we define the
(\delta,T)
tubular neighborhood of
l
centered at
o
as

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}^{T}_{\delta}(l,o)=\{(z,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{3}\,:\,|z|\leq\delta,|t|\leq T\}=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{C},|z|\leq\delta}n(z)\cdot\{a(t)\cdot o\,:\,|t|\leq T\},

which is the union of vertical geodesics that are close to
l
near the point
o
. Here
\delta
is the "radius" of the discs of the tube, and
T
is the length of the geodesic segment.
We will denote by
l_{[-T,T]}
the oriented geodesic segment

\displaystyle l_{[-T,T]}=\{a(t)\cdot o\,|\,-T\leq t\leq T\}

with the orientation as
t
increases.
In the general case, consider an oriented geodesic
\gamma
in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with a point
p
on
\gamma
.
There exists a
g\in G_{0}
so that
\gamma=g\cdot l
preserving the orientations and
p=g\cdot o
.
We define the
(\delta,T)
tubular neighborhood of
\gamma
centered at
p
to be

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}^{T}_{\delta}(\gamma,p)=g\cdot\mathscr{T}^{T}_{\delta}(l,o)=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{C},|z|\leq\delta}gn(z)\cdot\{a(t)\cdot o\,:\,|t|\leq T\}.

We define the projection along the horocycles from the tube onto the segment to be

(7.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{\gamma}:\mathscr{T}^{T}_{\delta}(\gamma,p)\rightarrow\gamma,\qquad gn(z)a(t)\cdot o\mapstochar\rightarrow ga(t)\cdot o.

Note that
MA
consists of all orientation-preserving isometries of
l
in
G_{0}
.
Recall that
M^{\prime}
is the normalizer of
A
in
K_{0}
.
All isometries of
l
in
G_{0}
, which do not need to preserve orientation, form the group

\displaystyle M^{\prime}A=\{e,w_{0}\}MA,

where
e
is the identity and
w_{0}
is a representative of the nontrivial element in the Weyl group. We will take

\displaystyle w_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}&i\\
i&\end{pmatrix}.

Using geodesic tubes, we give a geometric description of the condition for a group element
g\in G_{0}
to be close to
MA
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
T>0
be given. There exist constants
C,D>0
, depending only on
T
, so that

\displaystyle k\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T/2}(l,o)=\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T/2}(k\cdot l,o)\subset\mathscr{T}_{C\delta}^{T}(l,o)

for all
0<\delta<D
and
k\in K_{0}
satisfying
d(k,M^{\prime})\leq\delta
.

Proof.

The group
M^{\prime}=M\cup w_{0}M
has two connected components.
For an element
k\in K_{0}
satisfying
d(k,M^{\prime})\leq\delta
,
there are two cases, either
d(k,M)\leq\delta
, or
d(k,w_{0}M)\leq\delta
.
We first assume
d(k,M)\leq\delta
.
We may choose a constant
D
sufficiently small so that the exponential map is a diffeomorphism when restricted to any
\delta
-neighborhood of the origin in
K_{0}/M
. We then assume
k\in\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})M
with
r=\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}+r_{2}^{2}}\leq C_{1}\delta
for some constant
C_{1}>0
. Since
M
stablizes
\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T/2}(l,o)
, we assume
k=\exp(r_{1}X_{1}+r_{2}X_{2})
. By (
6.8
), we get

\displaystyle k=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(r)&(ir_{1}-r_{2})\sin(r)/r\\
(ir_{1}+r_{2})\sin(r)/r&\cos(r)\end{pmatrix}.

Let
(z,e^{t})
be an arbitrary point in
\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T/2}(l,o)
, i.e.
|z|\leq\delta
and
|t|\leq T/2
. Via the formula (
2.1
), the Iwasawa
N
-projection and
A
-projection of
kn(z)a(t)
are

\displaystyle N(kn(z)a(t))=\frac{z+O_{T}(r)}{1+O_{T}(r)}=z+O_{T}(r)

and

\displaystyle A(kn(z)a(t))=\log\frac{e^{t}}{1+O_{T}(r)}=t+O_{T}(r).

Then the result follows.
If
d(k,w_{0}M)\leq\delta
, we can assume

\displaystyle k=w_{0}\begin{pmatrix}\cos(r)&(ir_{1}-r_{2})\sin(r)/r\\
(ir_{1}+r_{2})\sin(r)/r&\cos(r)\end{pmatrix}.

The same argument finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
C_{0}>0
be given. There exist constants
C,D,T>0
, depending only on
C_{0}
, such that the following holds. If
0<\delta<D
and
g\in G_{0}
satisfying
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
and
d(g,M^{\prime}A)\leq\delta
, then the geodesic segment

\displaystyle g\cdot l_{[-1,1]}=\{ga(t)\cdot o\,|\,-1\leq t\leq 1\}

lies inside the tube
\mathscr{T}_{C\delta}^{T}(l,o)
.

Proof.

Let
g=kn(z)a(t)
with
k\in K_{0}
,
z\in\mathbb{C}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. The condition
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
implies that
z
and
t
are bounded. Choose
T>0
sufficiently large so that
|t+1|<T/2
and
|t-1|<T/2
hold for all possible choices of
t
for
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
.
The condition
d(g,M^{\prime}A)\leq\delta
implies that
d(k,M^{\prime})\leq C_{1}\delta
and
|z|\leq C_{1}\delta
for some constant
C_{1}>0
.
Then we have

\displaystyle n(z)a(t)\cdot l_{[-1,1]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{n(z)a(t+t_{0})\cdot o\,|\,-1\leq t_{0}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\{(z,e^{t})\,|\,|z|\leq C_{1}\delta,|t|\leq T/2\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{C_{1}\delta}^{T/2}(l,o)

Now the result follows by Lemma
7.1
.
∎

Next, we show the converse of Proposition
7.2
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
C_{0},T>0
be given. There exist constants
C,D>0
, depending on
C_{0}
and
T
, such that the following holds. If
0<\delta<D
and
g\in G_{0}
satisfying
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
and

\displaystyle g\cdot l_{[0,1]}=\{ga(t)\cdot o\mid 0\leq t\leq 1\}\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o),

then

\displaystyle d(g,M^{\prime}A)\leq C\delta.

Proof.

We write
g=k^{\prime}n(z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})
with
k^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
-\bar{\beta}&\bar{\alpha}\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
,
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
and
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
by the Iwasawa decomposition.
It suffices to prove
d(k^{\prime},M^{\prime})\ll\delta
and
|z^{\prime}|\ll\delta
.
Since
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
, both
z^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
can only vary in a bounded set, say
|z^{\prime}|\leq C_{1}
and
|t^{\prime}|\leq C_{1}
.
We may assume
T>10(C_{1}+1)
.
Then by (
2.1
),

\displaystyle g\cdot l_{[0,1]}

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}\cdot\{(z^{\prime},e^{t})\,|\,t^{\prime}\leq t\leq t^{\prime}+1\}

\displaystyle=\left.\left\{\left(\frac{(\alpha z^{\prime}+\beta)\overline{(-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha})}-\bar{\alpha}\beta e^{2t}}{|-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha}|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}e^{2t}},\frac{e^{t}}{|-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha}|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}e^{2t}}\right)\right|t^{\prime}\leq t\leq t^{\prime}+1\right\}

\displaystyle\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)

implies that, for
t^{\prime}\leq t\leq t^{\prime}+1
,

(7.2)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{(\alpha z^{\prime}+\beta)\overline{(-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha})}-\bar{\alpha}\beta e^{2t}}{|-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha}|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}e^{2t}}\right|\leq\delta,

and

(7.3)

\displaystyle e^{-T}\leq\frac{e^{t}}{|-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha}|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}e^{2t}}\leq e^{T}.

The assumptions on
T
and
t^{\prime}
with (
7.3
) imply that

\displaystyle e^{-2T}\leq|-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha}|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}e^{2t}\leq e^{2T},

so (
7.2
) gives

(7.4)

\displaystyle\left|(\alpha z^{\prime}+\beta)\overline{(-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha})}-\bar{\alpha}\beta e^{2t}\right|\leq e^{2T}\delta,

for
t^{\prime}\leq t\leq t^{\prime}+1
.
We can plug in
t=t^{\prime}+1
and
t=t^{\prime}
for (
7.4
).
This gives

\displaystyle\left|\bar{\alpha}\beta(e^{2(t^{\prime}+1)}-e^{2t^{\prime}})\right|\leq

\displaystyle\left|(\alpha z^{\prime}+\beta)\overline{(-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha})}-\bar{\alpha}\beta e^{2(t^{\prime}+1)}\right|+\left|(\alpha z^{\prime}+\beta)\overline{(-\bar{\beta}z^{\prime}+\bar{\alpha})}-\bar{\alpha}\beta e^{2t^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2e^{2T}\delta.

Hence,
|\alpha\beta|\ll\delta
.
Since
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
and
|\alpha\beta|\ll\delta
, either
|\alpha|\ll\delta
or
|\beta|\ll\delta
. Hence,
d(k^{\prime},M^{\prime})\ll\delta
.
Combing this with (
7.4
) again gives
|z^{\prime}|\ll\delta
.
∎

Next we consider the following family of geodesics, which we will care about in Section
9.1
.
Let
\epsilon_{1}>0
be a small constant.
We will let
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
be two large integers so that
N_{1}=\lambda^{1/2-\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2}+O(1)
and
N_{2}=\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}+O(1)
.
We define
N_{1}
many intervals covering
B

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{m}=\left\{b_{\theta}\in B\,:\,\theta\in\left[\frac{2\pi}{N_{1}}(m-2/3),\frac{2\pi}{N_{1}}(m+2/3)\right]\mod 2\pi\right\},\quad m=0,\dots,N_{1}-1,

and define
N_{2}+1
many intervals in
\mathbb{R}
that cover
[-2,2]

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{n}=\left[\frac{4}{N_{2}}(n-N_{2}/2-2/3),\frac{4}{N_{2}}(n-N_{2}/2+2/3)\right],\;\;\;n=0,\dots,N_{2}.

We let

(7.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{m}\in B\text{ be the center point of }\mathbf{I}_{m},\\
\text{ and }x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ be the center point of }\mathbf{J}_{n}.\end{split}

Each pair
(b,x)\in B\times\mathbb{R}
uniquely determines an oriented geodesic
bn(x)\cdot l
in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
Moreover, the next lemma shows that if
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, and the element
(b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2})
almost fix the geodesic
l
and its orientation, then the geodesics determined by
(b_{1},x_{1})
and
(b_{2},x_{2})
are almost the same.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
C>0
be given.
There exists a small constant
D>0
, depending only on
C
, such that the following holds.
Suppose
b_{1},b_{2}\in B=\operatorname{SO}(2)/\{\pm I\}
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in[-C,C]
. If
0<\delta<D
and

\displaystyle d\left((b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2}),M^{\prime}A\right)\leq\delta,

then

(a)

If
d\left((b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2}),MA\right)\leq\delta
, we have
d(b_{1},b_{2})\ll\delta
and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\ll\delta
;

(b)

If
d\left((b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2}),w_{0}MA\right)\leq\delta
, we have
d\left(b_{1}^{-1}b_{2},\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+x_{1}^{2}}}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&1\\
-1&x_{1}\end{pmatrix}\right)\ll\delta
and
|x_{1}+x_{2}|\ll\delta
.

Here all the implied constants depend only on
C
.

Proof.

We suppose

b_{1}^{-1}b_{2}=b_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta/2&\sin\theta/2\\
-\sin\theta/2&\cos\theta/2\end{pmatrix},\;\;\;\text{ with }\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

Then

\displaystyle(b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2})

\displaystyle=n(-x_{1})b_{\theta}n(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&-x_{1}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta/2&\sin\theta/2\\
-\sin\theta/2&\cos\theta/2\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{2}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta/2)+x_{2}\sin(\theta/2)&(x_{1}-x_{2})\cos(\theta/2)+(x_{1}x_{2}+1)\sin(\theta/2)\\
-\sin(\theta/2)&\cos(\theta/2)-x_{1}\sin(\theta/2)\end{pmatrix}.

We first assume that
d((b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2}),MA)\leq\delta
.
Then

\displaystyle\sin(\theta/2)\ll\delta\quad\text{ and }\quad(x_{1}-x_{2})\cos(\theta/2)+(x_{1}x_{2}+1)\sin(\theta/2)\ll\delta,

so the results
d(b_{1},b_{2})\ll\delta
and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\ll\delta
follow.

Next, we assume
d((b_{1}n(x_{1}))^{-1}b_{2}n(x_{2}),w_{0}MA)\leq\delta
.
We have

(7.8)

\displaystyle\cos(\theta/2)+x_{2}\sin(\theta/2)\ll\delta,\quad\text{ and }\quad\cos(\theta/2)-x_{1}\sin(\theta/2)\ll\delta.

We claim that
|\sin(\theta/2)|\geq c>0
for some small constant
c
. If we suppose that
|\sin(\theta/2)|<c
, then (
7.8
) implies that
|\cos(\theta/2)|\leq 2c+O(\delta)
, which is a contradiction if
D
and
c
are chosen to be sufficiently small.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle|x_{1}+x_{2}|\ll|(x_{1}+x_{2})\sin(\theta/2)|\leq|\cos(\theta/2)+x_{2}\sin(\theta/2)|+|\cos(\theta/2)-x_{1}\sin(\theta/2)|\ll\delta.

The identity
\cos^{2}(\theta/2)+\sin^{2}(\theta/2)=1
implies that

\displaystyle\left(x_{1}\sin(\theta/2)+O(\delta)\right)^{2}+\sin^{2}(\theta/2)=1

so that

\displaystyle\sin^{2}(\theta/2)=\frac{1}{1+x_{1}^{2}}+O(\delta)=\frac{1}{1+x_{1}^{2}}(1+O(\delta))\Rightarrow\sin(\theta/2)=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+x_{1}^{2}}}+O(\delta).

By (
7.8
), we have
\cos(\theta/2)=x_{1}\sin(\theta/2)+O(\delta)=\pm\frac{x_{1}}{\sqrt{1+x_{1}^{2}}}+O(\delta)
.
∎

We next derive a trivial bound, in the following Proposition
7.6
, for the number of geodesic segments corresponding to
(b_{m},x_{n})
contained in a tube.
Suppose
\gamma
is an oriented geodesic in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with a point
p\in\gamma
, and
\delta>0
.
Moreover, let
C_{0}>0
be a fixed constant,
and we assume that
(\gamma,p)=g\cdot(l,o)
with
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
.
We let
N_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)
be the set of pairs
(b_{m},x_{n})
,
0\leq m\leq N_{1}-1
,
0\leq n\leq N_{2}
, satisfying

\displaystyle b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}=\{b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t)\cdot o\,|\,-1\leq t\leq 1\}\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p).

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
C_{0},T>0
be given.
There exist constants
C,D>0
, depending on
C_{0},T
, such that the following holds.
For any
0<\delta<D
,
if
l_{[-1,1]}
is contained in some tube
\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)=g\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)
with
g\in G_{0}
and
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
,
then
\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)\subset\mathscr{T}_{C\delta}^{CT}(l,o)
.

Proof.

Since
g^{-1}l_{[-1,1]}\subset\mathscr{T}^{T}_{\delta}(l,o)
, by Proposition
7.3
,
d(g,M^{\prime}A)\ll\delta
.
Hence, we can write
g=k^{\prime}n(z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})

with
k^{\prime}\in K_{0}
,
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
and
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying

d(k^{\prime},M^{\prime})\ll\delta
,
|z^{\prime}|\ll\delta
and
|t^{\prime}|\ll 1
.
Hence, there exists
C_{1}>0
so that

\displaystyle g\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)=k^{\prime}n(z^{\prime})a(t^{\prime})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)\subset k^{\prime}\cdot\mathscr{T}_{C_{1}\delta}^{C_{1}T}(l,o).

Then the lemma follows from Lemma
7.1
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
C_{0},T>0
be given. There exists a small constant
D>0
, depending on
C_{0}
and
T
, such that for any
0<\delta<D
, and any
(\gamma,p)=g\cdot(l,o)
with
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
,

\displaystyle\#N_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)\ll(1+\delta N_{1})(1+\delta N_{2}).

Here the implied constant also depends on
C_{0}
and
T
.

Proof.

If
N_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)
is empty, then there is nothing to prove, so we assume that there exists some
m,n
so that
(b_{m},x_{n})\in N_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)
, i.e.
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)
.
By Lemma
7.5
, there exists a constant
C>0
so that
\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)\subset b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{C\delta}^{CT}(l,o)
. Therefore, the problem can be reduced to prove the case where
(\gamma,p)=b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot(l,o)
.

We assume
(\gamma,p)=b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot(l,o)
. Suppose
(b_{m^{\prime}},x_{n^{\prime}})\in N_{\delta}^{T}(\gamma,p)
. Then we have

\displaystyle b_{m^{\prime}}n(x_{n^{\prime}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\left(b_{m}n(x_{n})\right)^{-1}b_{m^{\prime}}n(x_{n^{\prime}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)

By Proposition
7.3
, there exists
C>0
so that

\displaystyle d\left(\left(b_{m}n(x_{n})\right)^{-1}b_{m^{\prime}}n(x_{n^{\prime}}),M^{\prime}A\right)\leq C\delta.

Lemma
7.4

implies that the number of possible
b_{m^{\prime}}
is
\ll 1+\delta N_{1}
, and
the number of possible
x_{n^{\prime}}
is
\ll 1+\delta N_{2}
.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
C_{0}>0
and
T>2
be given. There exist constants
C,D,D^{\prime}>0
, depending on
C_{0},T
, such that the following holds. Let
\delta,\omega>0
, and
g\in G_{0}
, which satisfies
d(g,e)\leq C_{0}
and
d(g,H^{\prime})\geq C\omega
. If
\delta
and
\omega
satisfy

\displaystyle\delta,\omega<D\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta<D^{\prime}\omega,

the number of pairs
(b_{m},x_{n})
(which are defined in (
7.7
)) so that

(7.9)

\displaystyle\text{there is a geodesic segment of length }2\text{ contained in }b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}

is
\ll(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{1})(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{2})
.
Here the implied constant depends on
C_{0}
and
T
.

Proof.

We take
R>0
so that the ball
B(o,R)\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}
centered at
o
of radius
R
contains
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}
for any
m,n
. If
B(o,2R)\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}=\varnothing
, then

\displaystyle d(B(o,R),g\mathbb{H}^{2})\gg_{R,C_{0}}d(g,H^{\prime})\geq C\omega>\frac{C}{D^{\prime}}\delta.

If we suppose that
C/D^{\prime}
is sufficiently large so that
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}=\varnothing
, then, in this case, the number of pairs satisfying (
7.9
) is 0.

Now we suppose that
B(o,2R)\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}\neq\varnothing
. In particular,
\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}=\gamma
is a geodesic.
Since a segment of
\gamma
lies in the ball
B(o,2R)
, there exists
h_{0}\in H_{0}
, which is bounded depending on
R
, so that
h_{0}\gamma=l
is the vertical geodesic through the origin
o
. Hence,
l=h_{0}\gamma=h_{0}(\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2})=\mathbb{H}^{2}\cap h_{0}g\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Hence,
h_{0}g\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a vertical plane in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
through
l
. We may find an element
k(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}e^{i\theta}&\\
&e^{-i\theta}\end{pmatrix}\in M
with
\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z},
so that
h_{0}g\mathbb{H}^{2}=k(\theta)\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Since
k(\theta)^{-1}h_{0}g
stabilizes
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we have
k(\theta)^{-1}h_{0}g\in H^{\prime}
. Using the Iwasawa decomposition for
H_{0}
, we can write

g=\big(k_{0}n(x_{0})a(t_{0})\big)k(\theta)h^{\prime}=k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)a(t_{0})h^{\prime}

where
k_{0}\in\mathrm{SO}(2)
,
t_{0},x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
are bounded, and
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
.
In particular,
h^{\prime}
is also bounded because of the boundedness of
g
.
Since both
k_{0}n(x_{0})
and
a(t_{0})h^{\prime}
are in
H^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle C\omega\leq d(g,H^{\prime})=d(k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)a(t_{0})h^{\prime},H^{\prime})\asymp d(k(\theta),H^{\prime}).

If
C
is sufficiently large, it may be seen that
\theta\notin[-\omega,\omega]\mod\pi/2
. Moreover, as

g\mathbb{H}^{2}=k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)\big(a(t_{0})h^{\prime}\mathbb{H}^{2}\big)=k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)\mathbb{H}^{2},

without loss of generality, we reduce to the case where
g=k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)
with
k_{0}\in\mathrm{SO}(2)
,
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
being bounded and
\theta\notin[-\omega,\omega]\mod\pi/2
.
Assume that
(b_{m},x_{n})
is a pair satisfying (
7.9
), i.e. there is a geodesic segment of length 2 contained in
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)\cap g\mathbb{H}^{2}
, and we denote the endpoints of such segment by
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. Because
p_{i}\in b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{\delta}^{T}(l,o)
, we have
d(p_{i},q_{i})\ll\delta
, where
q_{i}=\pi_{b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l}(p_{i})
and
\pi_{b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l}
is the projection map to
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l
defined as in (
7.1
). Since
p_{i}\in g\mathbb{H}^{2}
, we have
d(q_{i},g\mathbb{H}^{2})\leq d(q_{i},p_{i})\ll\delta
. Therefore, there exists another constant
C_{2}>0
so that

\displaystyle q_{i}\in g\cdot B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2}),\quad\text{ where }B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2}):=\{n(x+iy)a(t)\cdot o\mid x,y,t\in\mathbb{R},\,|y|\leq C_{2}\delta\}.

Therefore,
q_{i}
lies in

\displaystyle\big(g\cdot B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\big)\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}

\displaystyle=\big(k_{0}n(x_{0})k(\theta)\cdot B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\big)\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}=k_{0}n(x_{0})\Big(\big(k(\theta)\cdot B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\big)\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}\Big).

By a direct trigonometric calculation with the group action formula (
2.1
), it may be seen that

\displaystyle T_{\theta,C_{2}\delta}

\displaystyle:=\big(k(\theta)\cdot B_{C_{2}\delta}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\big)\cap\mathbb{H}^{2}=\{n(x)a(t)\cdot o\mid x,t\in\mathbb{R},\,|x|\leq C_{2}\delta\csc(2\theta)\}.

The same argument shows that for every point
p
lying in the geodesic segment between
p_{1},p_{2}
, its projection
\pi_{b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l}(p)
is in
k_{0}n(x_{0})T_{\theta,C_{2}\delta}
as well, i.e., the geodesic segment joining
q_{1},q_{2}
(which is a segment of the geodesic
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l
) is contained in
k_{0}n(x_{0})T_{\theta,C_{2}\delta}
. Moreover, the triangle inequality implies that

\displaystyle d(q_{1},q_{2})\geq d(p_{1},p_{2})-d(p_{1},q_{1})-d(p_{2},q_{2})=2-O(\delta)\geq 1

if
D
is sufficiently small. It follows that there is some
t_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, which is bounded depending on
C_{0}
and
T
, such that
b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t_{1})\cdot l_{[0,1]}\subset k_{0}n(x_{0})T_{\theta,C_{2}\delta}
, which implies that
n(x_{0})^{-1}k_{0}^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t_{1})\cdot l_{[0,1]}\subset T_{\theta,C_{2}\delta}

By the boundedness of
n(x_{0})^{-1}k_{0}^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t_{1})\cdot l_{[0,1]}
, there exists
T_{1}>0
, depending on
C_{0}
and
T
, so that

\displaystyle n(x_{0})^{-1}k_{0}^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t_{1})\cdot l_{[0,1]}\subset\{n(x)a(t)\cdot o\mid|x|\leq C_{2}\delta\csc(2\theta),|t|\leq T_{1}\}=\mathscr{T}^{T_{1}}_{C_{2}\delta\csc(2\theta)}(l,o).

By Proposition
7.3
, we have

\displaystyle d\left(n(x_{0})^{-1}k_{0}^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n})a(t_{1}),M^{\prime}A\right)\ll\delta\csc(2\theta)\ll\omega^{-1}\delta\Rightarrow d\left(\left(k_{0}n(x_{0})\right)^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n}),M^{\prime}A\right)\ll\omega^{-1}\delta.

Lemma
7.4

implies that the number of possible
b_{m}
is
\ll 1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{1}
, and
the number of possible
x_{n}
is
\ll 1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{2}
.
∎

The main result in this section is that most of
b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}},b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}}
will satisfy
the condition
(
9.12
), provided certain condition that
g\in G_{0}
is away from
H^{\prime}
.
Recall that
b_{m},x_{n}
are defined as in (
7.7
).

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
C_{0}>0
be given.
There exist constants
C,D,D^{\prime}>0
, depending on
C_{0}
such that the following holds.
Suppose that
\delta,\omega>0
satisfy

\displaystyle\delta,\omega<D\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta<D^{\prime}\omega.

Let
g\in G_{0}
satisfy

\displaystyle d(g,e)\leq C_{0}\quad\text{ and }\quad d(g,H^{\prime})\geq C\omega.

Then the number of quadruples
(b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}},b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}})
satisfying

(7.10)

\displaystyle d(n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}}),MA)\leq\delta

is
\ll(1+\delta N_{1})(1+\delta N_{2})(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{1})(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}},b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}})
is a quadruple satisfying (
7.10
).
By Proposition
7.2
,
there exists
C_{1},D_{1},T>0
so that for any
0<\delta<D_{1}

\displaystyle n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset\mathscr{T}_{C_{1}\delta}^{T}(l,o).

Hence,

(7.11)

\displaystyle gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset b_{m_{1}}n(x_{n_{1}})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{C_{1}\delta}^{T}(l,o).

Since
gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}
is a geodesic segment of length
2
in
g\mathbb{H}^{2}
,
by Proposition
7.7
, there exist
C>0
,
0<D<D_{1}
and
D^{\prime}>0
so that
the number of possible pairs of
(b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}})
satisfying (
7.11
) for some
