(b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}})
with
0<\delta,\omega<D
,
\delta<D^{\prime}\omega
and
d(g,H^{\prime})\geq C\omega
is
\ll(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{1})(1+\omega^{-1}\delta N_{2})
.
Writing (
7.11
) as

(7.12)

\displaystyle b_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}})\cdot l_{[-1,1]}\subset g^{-1}b_{m_{1}}n(x_{n_{1}})\cdot\mathscr{T}_{C_{1}\delta}^{T}(l,o),

by Proposition
7.6
,
for each fixed
(b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}})
, the number of
(b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}})
satisfying (
7.12
) is
\ll(1+\delta N_{1})(1+\delta N_{2})
.
Taking the product of the numbers gives the result.
∎

8.
Estimates of Hecke returns

In this section, we give two Hecke return estimates, which will be used in the amplification arguments.

8.1.
Estimates with respect to maximal tori

We let
g\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
,
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
and
\delta>0
, and suppose that
\mathfrak{n}
is only divisible by prime ideals in
\mathscr{Q}
.
We recall that
\mathscr{Q}
is the set of finite places of
F
that is not in
S
and is inert in
E
.
We define the counting function

\displaystyle N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})=\left\{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\cap K(\mathfrak{n})\,|\,d(g^{-1}\gamma g,e)\leq 2,d(g^{-1}\gamma g,MA)\leq\delta\right\},

which describes how many times the Hecke operators map
gl
close to itself that are orientation preserving.

Lemma 8.1
.

There exists a constant
C>0
with the following property. Let
g\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
be an ideal divisible only by primes in
\mathscr{Q}
. If
X=\mathrm{N}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\delta>0
satisfies

(8.1)

\displaystyle\delta X\leq C,

then there exists a quadratic field extension
L
of
E
in
B
so that
N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
is contained in
L
.

Proof.

We first prove that the elements in
N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
are commutative with each other. For the commutativity, it suffices to check that
xy-yx=0
for any
x,y\in N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
. We fix a basis
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\}
for
B
over
E
. By possibly enlarging
S
, we can assume that
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}\}
forms an
\mathcal{O}_{E,v}
basis for
\mathcal{O}_{B,v}
whenever
v\notin S
. Every element
x\in B
can be written as
x=\sum_{i}x_{i}\alpha_{i}
and we shall write
x
as a vector
x=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{4}
. If we suppose that
N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
is not commutative, then we can let
x=(x_{i}),y=(y_{i})
be one noncommutative pair and let
z=(z_{i})=xy-yx
. It is clear that
|x_{i}|_{w},|y_{i}|_{w}\ll 1
whenever
w
is archimedean, and so
|z_{i}|_{w}\ll 1
. At the place
w_{0}
, there are
a,b\in MA
such that
d(g^{-1}xg,a)\leq\delta
and
d(g^{-1}yg,b)\leq\delta
. Since the absolute value at the complex archimedean place
w_{0}
is the square of the usual distance,
|z_{i}|_{w_{0}}\ll\delta^{2}
. Since we have assumed
z\neq 0
, there is at least one
z_{i}\neq 0
. Since
x,y\in K(\mathfrak{n})
, it may be seen that
\prod_{w<\infty}|z_{i}|_{w}\ll X^{2}
. By the product formula,
1=|z_{i}|_{E}\ll\delta^{2}X^{2}\leq C^{2}
. Hence, we get a contradiction if a condition of the form (
8.1
) holds for
C
sufficiently small.
Since
N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
is commutative, there exists a quadratic field over
E
in
B
containing
N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
, which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

There exists a constant
C>0
with the following property. Let
g\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
be an ideal divisible only by primes in
\mathscr{Q}
. If
X=\mathrm{N}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\delta>0
satisfies
\delta\leq CX^{-1}
, then
\#N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})\ll_{\epsilon}X^{\epsilon}
.

Proof.

We pick
C>0
as in Lemma
8.1
, and let
L\subset B
be the quadratic extension of
E
produced by the lemma.
Since
\mathscr{Q}
consists of finite places for
F
that are inert in
E
, we will denote by
w
the unique place of
E
above
v\in\mathscr{Q}
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be a set of finite places of
E
generating the ideal class group of
E
and containing all places lying over
S
and we assume that
S^{\prime}
is disjoint from the places over
\mathscr{Q}
.
Let
n\in E
satisfy
|n|_{w}=\operatorname{N}_{E}(\mathfrak{n}_{w}^{-1})=\operatorname{N}(\mathfrak{n}_{v}^{-2})
for all
v\in\mathscr{Q}
, and
|n|_{w}\asymp 1
if
w\in S^{\prime}
and
w\neq w_{0}
. Moreover, for all other
E
-places
w\neq w_{0}
, we require
|n|_{w}=1
. It implies that
|n|_{w_{0}}\asymp\operatorname{N}_{E}(\mathfrak{n})=X^{2}
. The reduced norm
\operatorname{nr}_{B}
restricted to
L
agrees with the norm map from
L
to
E
. We see that
n\cdot N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})=\{n\eta\,|\,\eta\in N(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})\}
is contained in the set of
\gamma
in
L
so that its norm in
E
is
n^{2}
;
\gamma
is integral away from
S^{\prime}
; All the norms of
\gamma
in the archimedean places over
w_{0}
must be
\ll|n|_{w_{0}}\asymp X^{2}
, and all the other norms must be
\ll 1
.
The number of these algebraic numbers must be
\ll_{\epsilon}X^{\epsilon}
. ∎

8.2.
Estimates with respect to
H^{\prime}

We define another counting function that will be used in another arithmetic amplification.
We let
g\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
,
\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}
and
\delta>0
, and suppose that
\mathfrak{n}
is only divisible by prime ideals in
\mathscr{P}
.
We recall that
\mathscr{P}
is the set of finite places of
F
that is not in
S
and is split in
E
.

\displaystyle M(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})=\left\{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\cap K(\mathfrak{n})\,|\,d(g^{-1}\gamma g,e)\leq 1,d(g^{-1}\gamma g,H^{\prime})\leq\delta\right\},

which describes how many times the Hecke operators map
g\mathbb{H}^{2}
close to itself. We have the following estimate for
M(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})
, which is proved in
[
22
, §4]
.

Proposition 8.3
.

There exists a constant
C>0
with the following property. Let
g\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
and
X>0
. There exist a set
\mathscr{P}_{0}\subset\mathscr{P}
, depending on
g
and with
\#(\mathscr{P}\backslash\mathscr{P}_{0})\ll\log X
, and with the following property. If
\delta<CX^{-8}
, and the ideal
\mathfrak{n}
is divisible only by primes in
\mathscr{P}_{0}
, and satisfies
\operatorname{N}(\mathfrak{n})<X
,
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
, then
M(g,\delta,\mathfrak{n})=\varnothing
.

9.
Bounds near the spectrum

In this section, we prove Proposition
4.2
.

9.1.
Decomposition into geodesic beams

Let
\eta:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an even smooth function with compact support satisfying:

•

The support of
\eta
is
[-2/3,2/3]
,

•

\eta(x)\equiv 1
for
x\in[-1/3,1/3]
,

•

0<\eta(x)<1
if
1/3<|x|<2/3
,

•

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta(x+n)\equiv 1
.

Using
\eta
, we may construct a partition of unity of the boundary
B
. Recall that we identify
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
with
B
via the map
\theta\mapstochar\rightarrow b_{\theta}
.
Let
N_{1},N_{2}>0
be two large integers.
For
\epsilon_{1}>0
that can be arbitrarily small, we suppose that
N_{1}=\lambda^{1/2-\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2}+O(1)
and
N_{2}=\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2}+O(1)
. We define
N_{1}
many intervals
\mathbf{I}_{m}
covering
B
to be as in (
7.5
).
We denote by
b_{m}=b_{\theta_{m}}
the center point of
\mathbf{I}_{m}
, and let
\tau_{0}(x)=\eta(N_{1}x/2\pi)
. We may think of it as a smooth bump function defined on
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
by choosing the fundamental domain
[-\pi,\pi]
of the quotient space.
We will still denote by
\tau_{0}
the function on
B
by
\tau_{0}(b_{\theta})=\tau_{0}(\theta)
.
For any other
0\leq m\leq N_{1}-1
, we define

\displaystyle\tau_{m}(b)=\tau_{0}(b_{m}^{-1}b),\quad b\in B.

By our construction,
\tau_{m}
is a bump function with support
\mathbf{I}_{m}
and
\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\tau_{m}
is the constant function
1
on the boundary
B
.

Next, we construct a partition using bump functions for
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. Recall that
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
is the given cutoff function in Proposition
4.1
. Without loss of generality, we assume the support of
\chi
is strictly contained in the square

\displaystyle U=\{\mathbf{z}=(x,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{2}:-1\leq x,t\leq 1\}.

We use
\mathbf{z}
to denote a point in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, and use
(x,e^{t})
with
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
for the coordinates of the point
\mathbf{z}
.
We define
N_{2}+1
many intervals
\mathbf{J}_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}
that cover
[-2,2]
to be as in (
7.6
).
Let
x_{n}=4(n-N_{2}/2)/N_{2}
be the center point of
\mathbf{J}_{n}
. We define

\displaystyle\eta_{n}(x,e^{t})=\eta_{n}(x)=\eta(N_{2}(x-x_{n})/2),

that is a bump function in the
x
direction with support
\mathbf{J}_{n}
. By this construction, we have

\displaystyle\chi(\mathbf{z})=\chi(\mathbf{z})\sum_{n=0}^{N_{2}}\eta_{n}(b^{-1}\mathbf{z})

for any boundary element
b\in B
.

We recall that
{H}_{\beta}
is the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
so that its image under the Helgason transform is
L^{2}([\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]\times B)
. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
.
We define, for any
0\leq m\leq N_{1}-1
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m}(s,b):=\widetilde{\phi}(s,b)\tau_{m}(b)

if
s>0
and
b\in B
.
Hence,
\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
, and by Proposition
3.3
there exists an extension, which we still denote by
\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m}
, in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}\times B)_{W}
.
Hence,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.
We have

\displaystyle\chi\phi=\chi\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\widetilde{\phi}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\tau_{m}\right)=\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\chi\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})=\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{2}}\chi\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\cdot)\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m}).

We let

(9.1)

\displaystyle\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})=\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2}),

and so we have the decomposition
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
.
We call each
\phi_{m,n}
a geodesic beam along the geodesic
b_{m}n(x_{n})\cdot l
.

Fix a single beam
\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})=\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})
.
Let
2\mathbf{I}_{m},4\mathbf{I}_{m}\subset B
be the intervals centered at
b_{m}
with length
2|\mathbf{I}_{m}|
and
4|\mathbf{I}_{m}|
.
Let
\mathscr{B}\in C^{\infty}(B)
satisfy

•

0\leq\mathscr{B}\leq 1
;

•

\mathscr{B}\equiv 1
on
2\mathbf{I}_{m}
;

•

\supp(\mathscr{B})\subset 4\mathbf{I}_{m}
.

For
s>0
and
b\in B
, we define

(9.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{f_{m,n}}(s,b)=\widetilde{\phi_{m,n}}(s,b)\mathscr{B}(b),\\
&{g_{m,n}}(s,b)=\widetilde{\phi_{m,n}}(s,b)(1-\mathscr{B}(b)).\end{split}

It is clear that
f_{m,n},g_{m,n}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
and
\widetilde{\phi_{m,n}}=f_{m,n}+g_{m,n}
as functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
.
We shall prove that
g_{m,n}
is an error term.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
0\neq\theta\in\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
,
x,t\in\mathbb{R}
.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=-\frac{e^{2t}-(x-\cot(\theta/2))^{2}}{e^{2t}+(x-\cot(\theta/2))^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=-\frac{2(x-\cot(\theta/2))}{e^{2t}+(x-\cot(\theta/2))^{2}}.

Proof.

The results follow directly from (
5.2
).
∎

Proposition 9.2
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\phi_{m,n}
and
g_{m,n}
be as in (
9.1
) and (
9.2
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\|g_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}\ll_{A}\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
b\in B
. Then
\widetilde{\phi_{m,n}}(s,b)
is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})e^{(-is+1/2)A(\mathbf{z},b)}d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(b_{m}\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(\mathbf{z})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(b_{m}\mathbf{z})e^{(-is+1/2)A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},b)}d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(b_{m}\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(\mathbf{z})\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\widetilde{\phi}(r,d)\tau_{m}(d)e^{(ir+1/2)A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},d)}d\mu(r,d)\right)e^{(-is+1/2)A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},b)}d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{B}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(b_{m}\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(\mathbf{z})e^{(A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},d)+A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},b))/2}\exp(irA(d^{-1}b_{m}\mathbf{z})-isA(b^{-1}b_{m}\mathbf{z}))d\mathbf{z}\right)\widetilde{\phi}(r,d)\tau_{m}(d)d\mu(r,d).

We consider the integral inside the parenthesis,
which can be written as an oscillatory integral.
We denote it by
\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}}\eta_{n}(x)\chi_{1}(x,t)\exp\left(irA(d^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))-isA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))\right)dxdt

(9.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}\exp\left(irA(d^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))-isA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))\right)dt\right)\eta_{n}(x)dx.

Here
r\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
,
b\notin 2\mathbf{I}_{m}
,
d\in\mathbf{I}_{m}
and

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(x,t)=\chi(b_{m}\mathbf{z})\exp\left({(A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},d)+A(b_{m}\mathbf{z},b))/2-t}\right),\quad\text{ where }\mathbf{z}=n(x)a(t)\cdot o,

is a smooth compactly supported function. We have
d^{-1}b_{m}\in\mathbf{I}_{0}
and
b^{-1}b_{m}\notin 2\mathbf{I}_{0}
.
We can also write
\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)
as an oscillatory integral in the
x
-variable:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta(N_{2}(x-x_{n})/2)\chi_{1}\exp\left(irA(d^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))-isA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))\right)dx\right)dt

(9.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp\left(irA(d^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))-isA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))\right)dx\right)dt.

Here we let
\chi_{2}(x,t)=\eta(x)\chi_{1}(x_{n}+2x/N_{2},t)
.
We notice that
\chi_{2}
is a cutoff function with support at scale
\asymp 1\times 1
.
We define
\theta_{1}\in\mathbf{I}_{0},\theta_{2}\notin 2\mathbf{I}_{0}
so that they satisfy

\displaystyle d^{-1}b_{m}=b_{\theta_{1}}\quad\text{ and }\quad b^{-1}b_{m}=b_{\theta_{2}}.

By Corollary
6.5
, there exists
\delta,\sigma>0
so that for any
x,t\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=\theta^{2}\xi(\theta,x,t),

\displaystyle\xi(\theta,x,t)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial^{n}\xi}{\partial t^{n}}(\theta,x,t)\right|

\displaystyle\ll_{n}1,

if
\theta\in(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
, and

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))\geq\sigma,

if
\theta\notin(-\delta,\delta)\mod 2\pi
.
Here
\theta
can be either
\theta_{1}
or
\theta_{2}
, and we can assume
0<\sigma<1
.

If
0\leq s/r\leq(2-\sigma)/{2(1-\sigma)}
and
\theta_{2}\notin(-\delta,\delta)
,
we consider the oscillatory integral in (
9.3
)

(9.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}\exp(ir\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{1}))dt,

with

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x)a(t))-\frac{s}{r}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x)a(t))

\displaystyle=-\left(t-A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x)a(t))\right)+\frac{s}{r}\left(t-A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x)a(t))\right)+\frac{r-s}{r}t.

Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\theta_{1}^{2}\xi(\theta_{1},x,t)+\frac{s}{r}\left(1-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x)a(t))\right)+\frac{r-s}{r}

\displaystyle\geq-\theta_{1}^{2}\xi+\frac{s}{r}\sigma+\frac{r-s}{r}

\displaystyle\geq-\theta_{1}^{2}\xi+\sigma/2

\displaystyle\gg 1.

Also, it is clear that all higher derivatives
\partial^{n}\varphi/\partial t^{n}\ll_{n}1
.
Hence, in this case, the bound
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}e^{ir\varphi}dt\ll_{A}r^{-A}\ll\lambda^{-A}
follows from integrating by parts.

If
0\leq s/r\leq 2/3
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
we still consider the oscillatory integral in (
9.3
).
Again, we consider the phase function
\varphi
defined in (
9.5
).
We have

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=-\left(t-A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x)a(t))\right)+\frac{s}{r}\left(t-A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x)a(t))\right)+\frac{r-s}{r}t.

Since
(s/r)\theta_{2}^{2}\xi(\theta_{2},x,t)
is non-negative and
\frac{r-s}{r}\geq\frac{1}{3}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\theta_{1}^{2}\xi(\theta_{1},x,t)+\frac{s}{r}\theta_{2}^{2}\xi(\theta_{2},x,t)+\frac{r-s}{r}\geq-\theta_{1}^{2}\xi(\theta_{1},x,t)+\frac{1}{3}\gg 1+O(\theta_{1}^{2})\gg 1.

Since
\partial^{n}\varphi/\partial t^{n}\ll_{n}1
,
the bound
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{1}e^{ir\varphi}dt\ll_{A}\lambda^{-A}
follows by integrating by parts.

If
2/3\leq s/r\leq(2-\sigma)/{2(1-\sigma)}
and
\theta_{2}\in(-\delta,\delta)
,
we consider the oscillatory integral in (
9.4
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp\left(-i(\beta N_{2}\theta_{2})\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\right)dx

with

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=(r\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}^{-1})\left(-A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))+\frac{s}{r}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))\right).

We have
\beta N_{2}\theta_{2}\gg\beta(\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2})(\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2})\asymp\lambda^{\epsilon_{1}}\beta
. By (
5.4
) in Lemma
5.2
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\asymp(r\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}^{-1})N_{2}^{-1}(\frac{s}{r}\theta_{2}-\theta_{1})\asymp(r\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}^{-1})N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}\asymp r\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-2}\asymp 1.

Here
(s/r)\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\asymp\theta_{2}
holds because
\theta_{1}\in\mathbf{I}_{0},\theta_{2}\notin 2\mathbf{I}_{0}
and we have assumed
s/r\geq 2/3
.
For any
n\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}\varphi}{\partial x^{n}}\ll(r\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}^{-1})N_{2}^{-n}\ll\lambda\beta^{-1}N_{2}^{-3}|\mathbf{I}_{0}|^{-1}\ll\lambda\beta^{-1}(\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2})^{-3}(\lambda^{1/2-\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2})\ll 1.

Hence, by integrating by parts, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp\left(-i(\beta N_{2}\theta_{2})\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\right)dx\ll_{A}(\lambda^{\epsilon_{1}}\beta)^{-A}\ll_{A}\lambda^{-A}.

When
s/r\geq(2-\sigma)/{2(1-\sigma)}
, we will show the two dimensional integral
\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)
is
\ll_{A}s^{-A}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(s,r,b,d)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N_{2}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp\left(-is\varphi(x,t)\right)dxdt,

with the phase function

\displaystyle\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=-\frac{r}{s}A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))+A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t)).

We define

\displaystyle X_{2}=X_{2}(x,\theta_{2}):=x_{n}+\frac{2x}{N_{2}}-\cot(\theta_{2}/2)=x_{n}-\cot(\theta_{2}/2)+O(\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}),

and it is well-defined because
\theta_{2}
is defined to be away from
0
in
\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}
.
Lemma
9.1
implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))=-\frac{e^{2t}-X_{2}^{2}}{e^{2t}+X_{2}^{2}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))=-\frac{2X_{2}}{e^{2t}+X_{2}^{2}}\cdot\frac{2}{N_{2}}.

Since
\theta_{1}\in\mathbf{I}_{0}
, by (
5.4
),
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(b_{\theta_{1}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))\asymp N_{2}^{-1}\theta_{1}\ll\lambda^{-1+\epsilon_{1}}\beta.

Let
0<\sigma_{1}<1
be a small number to be chosen later.
We may divide the set
(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})\backslash(2\mathbf{I}_{0})
into three parts:

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{I}

\displaystyle:=\{\theta\in(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})\backslash(2\mathbf{I}_{0})\,|\,\sigma_{1}\leq|x_{n}-\cot(\theta/2)|\leq\sigma_{1}^{-1}\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{II}

\displaystyle:=\{\theta\in(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})\backslash(2\mathbf{I}_{0})\,|\,|x_{n}-\cot(\theta/2)|<\sigma_{1}\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{III}

\displaystyle:=\{\theta\in(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})\backslash(2\mathbf{I}_{0})\,|\,|x_{n}-\cot(\theta/2)|>\sigma_{1}^{-1}\}.

If
\theta_{2}\in\mathbf{I}_{I}
,
then there exists
\sigma_{2}>0
, depending on
\sigma_{1}
, so that

\displaystyle\left|N_{2}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))\right|=\frac{4|X_{2}|}{e^{2t}+X_{2}^{2}}\geq\sigma_{2}

for all
(x,t)\in\supp\chi_{2}
,
then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}N_{2}\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\geq\sigma_{2}+O(\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2})\gg\sigma_{2}>0.

For any
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x^{n}}N_{2}\varphi(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\ll N_{2}\cdot N_{2}^{-n}\ll 1.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int\chi_{2}\exp(-is\varphi)dx=\int\chi_{2}\exp(-i(sN_{2}^{-1})(N_{2}\varphi))dx\ll_{A}(sN_{2}^{-1})^{-A}\ll_{A}s^{-A}.

If
\theta_{2}\in\mathbf{I}_{II}
, then
|X_{2}|=|x_{n}-\cot(\theta_{2}/2)|+O(\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2})<\sigma_{1}+O(\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2})\leq 2\sigma_{1}
when
\lambda
is sufficiently large.
Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))=-1+\frac{2X_{2}^{2}}{e^{2t}+X_{2}^{2}}=-1+O(\sigma_{1}^{2}).

We should choose
\sigma_{1}
to be sufficiently small so that for
\theta_{2}\in\mathbf{I}_{II}

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=1+O(\sigma_{1}^{2})+\frac{r}{s}(1+O(\theta_{1}^{2}))\gg 1.

In this case, it is clear that
\partial^{n}\varphi/\partial t^{n}\ll_{n}1
.
Hence,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp(-is\varphi)dt\ll_{A}s^{-A}
.

If
\theta_{2}\in\mathbf{I}_{III}
, then
|X_{2}|=|x_{n}-\cot(\theta_{2}/2)|+O(\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2})>\sigma_{1}^{-1}+O(\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2})\geq\sigma_{1}^{-1}/2
. Hence

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta_{2}}n(x_{n}+2x/N_{2})a(t))=1-\frac{2e^{2t}}{e^{2t}+X_{2}^{2}}=1+O(\sigma_{1}^{2}).

Again, we can make sure
\sigma_{1}
is sufficiently small so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}(x,t;s/r,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=1+O(\sigma_{1}^{2})-\frac{r}{s}(1+O(\theta_{1}^{2}))\geq 1-\frac{r}{s}+O(\sigma_{1}^{2})\geq\frac{\sigma}{2-\sigma}+O(\sigma_{1}^{2})\gg 1.

Therefore
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{2}\exp(-is\varphi)dt\ll_{A}s^{-A}
.

The
L^{2}
-norm bound for
g_{m,n}
follows from all the above estimates and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

Proposition 9.3
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\phi_{m,n}
be as in (
9.1
).
Let
\mathcal{S}\subset\{(m,n)\,|\,0\leq m\leq N_{1}-1,0\leq n\leq N_{2}\}
be any subset.
There is a constant
C>0
independent of
\phi
and
\mathcal{S}
so that

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2})\in\mathcal{S}\\
(m_{1},n_{1})\neq(m_{2},n_{2})\end{subarray}}\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\rangle\Big|\leq C\|\phi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof.

We may split the sum into two parts

(9.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\neq n_{2}\\
(m,n_{1}),(m,n_{2})\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\langle\phi_{m,n_{1}},\phi_{m,n_{2}}\rangle+\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2}\\
(m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2})\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\right\rangle.

Since
\langle\phi_{m,n_{1}},\phi_{m,n_{2}}\rangle=0
unless
\mathbf{J}_{n_{1}}\cap\mathbf{J}_{n_{2}}\neq\varnothing
, the first term in (
9.6
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\neq n_{2}\\
(m,n_{2})\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\langle\phi_{m,n_{1}},\phi_{m,n_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{m}\sum_{n_{1}\neq n_{2}}\left|\langle\phi_{m,n_{1}},\phi_{m,n_{2}}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{m}\left(\sum_{n_{1}\neq n_{2}}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{n_{1}}\cap\mathbf{J}_{n_{2}}}\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\chi(x,e^{t})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})\left(b_{m}\cdot(x,e^{t})\right)\right|^{2}e^{-t}dxdt\right)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{m}\|\chi\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{m}\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\|\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\sum_{m}\tau_{m}(b)^{2}|\widetilde{\phi}(s,b)|^{2}d\mu(s,b)

\displaystyle\ll\|\widetilde{\phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}.

Apply the decomposition (
9.2
) and Proposition
9.2
to
\phi_{m,n}
,
we have
\widetilde{\phi_{m,n}}=f_{m,n}+g_{m,n}
with the properties

\supp({f_{m,n}})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times 4\mathbf{I}_{m}
and
\|g_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}\ll_{N}\lambda^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
. For each fixed
m
, we define

\displaystyle\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\phi_{m,n},\quad\quad f_{m,\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}f_{m,n},\quad\quad g_{m,\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}g_{m,n}.

We claim that

(9.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\|\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\ll\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

The claim follows from the definitions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{m}\|\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}&=\sum_{m}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})\right|^{2}d\mathbf{z}\\
&=\sum_{m}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}\end{subarray}}\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\right)^{2}|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})|^{2}d\mathbf{z}\\
&\ll\sum_{m}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})\right|^{2}d\mathbf{z}\\
&\ll\sum_{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\left|\widetilde{\phi}(s,b)\right|^{2}\tau_{m}(b)^{2}d\mu(s,b)\\
&\ll\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.\end{split}

It may be seen that
\widetilde{\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}}=f_{m,\mathcal{S}}+g_{m,\mathcal{S}}
as functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
,
\supp(f_{m,\mathcal{S}})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times 4\mathbf{I}_{m}
and

\displaystyle\|g_{m,\mathcal{S}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}\leq\sum\|g_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)}\ll_{N}\lambda^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Hence, the second term in (
9.6
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\langle\phi_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}},\phi_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\langle\widetilde{\phi_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}},\widetilde{\phi_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\left\langle f_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}},f_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle+O_{N}(\lambda^{-N}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}),

and
\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\langle f_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}},f_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}\rangle
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}{f_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)\overline{{f_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)}d\mu(s,b)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{4\mathbf{I}_{m_{1}}\cap 4\mathbf{I}_{m_{2}}}{f_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)\overline{{f_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)}d\mu(s,b)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{4\mathbf{I}_{m_{1}}\cap 4\mathbf{I}_{m_{2}}}\left|\widetilde{\phi_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b){\widetilde{\phi_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)}\right|d\mu(s,b)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m_{1}\neq m_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{4\mathbf{I}_{m_{1}}\cap 4\mathbf{I}_{m_{2}}}\left(\left|\widetilde{\phi_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)\right|^{2}+\left|{\widetilde{\phi_{m_{2},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)}\right|^{2}\right)d\mu(s,b)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{m_{2}\neq m_{1}}\int_{4\mathbf{I}_{m_{1}}\cap 4\mathbf{I}_{m_{2}}}\left|\widetilde{\phi_{m_{1},\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)\right|^{2}d\mu(s,b)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\left|\widetilde{\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}}(s,b)\right|^{2}d\mu(s,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\|\phi_{m,\mathcal{S}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Hence, we obtain the desired bound by applying (
9.7
).
∎

Proposition 9.4
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\phi_{m,n}
be as in (
9.1
) with the geodesic beam decomposition
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
.
There is a constant
C>0
independent of
\phi
so that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{2}}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\|\chi\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\chi\phi,\chi\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m_{1},n_{1}}\sum_{m_{2},n_{2}}\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+\sum_{(m_{1},n_{1})\neq(m_{2},n_{2})}\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\rangle.

Hence,

\displaystyle\sum_{m,n}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\leq\|\chi\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+\left|\sum_{(m_{1},n_{1})\neq(m_{2},n_{2})}\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\rangle\right|.

The proposition follows by applying Proposition
9.3
.
∎

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
We decompose the sum
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
further based on
L^{2}
-norms of
\phi_{m,n}
.
Let
C>0
be the constant as in Proposition
9.4
, so that

(9.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{1}-1}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{2}}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

holds. For any
0<\delta<1
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)=\{\phi_{m,n}:\,\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}<\delta C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)=\{\phi_{m,n}:\,\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\geq\delta C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\}.

Proposition 9.5
.

For any
0<\delta<1
, we have
\#\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)\leq\delta^{-1}
.

Proof.

By the inequality (
9.8
), we have

\displaystyle C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\geq\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\geq\#\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)\cdot\delta C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2},

so we conclude that
\#\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)\leq\delta^{-1}
.
∎

In the rest of the section, we will prove the following two main estimates for the sums of
\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle
. The first estimate is obtained by treating each
\phi_{m,n}
separately.

Proposition 9.6
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
If
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
, we have

(9.9)

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon,A}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+9\epsilon_{1}/4+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Moreover, for any
0<\delta<1
, we have

(9.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon}\delta^{-1/2}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+9\epsilon_{1}/4+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

For the geodesic beams with smaller
L^{2}
-norms, we take the summation of them to establish the second estimate.

Proposition 9.7
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
If
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}

and
\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}\leq\delta\leq\beta^{-3/2}
,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon}\delta^{1/138}\lambda^{1/4+5\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon}\beta^{1/92}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

Given
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
, by the decomposition from (
9.1
), and applying Propositions
9.6
and
9.7
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\psi,\chi\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle+\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\left(\delta^{-1/2}\lambda^{1/4-1/16+\epsilon}\beta^{13/16}+\delta^{1/138}\lambda^{1/4+\epsilon}\beta^{1/92}\right)\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Choosing
\delta=(\lambda^{-69}\beta^{885})^{1/560}>\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}
completes the proof. It may be seen that
\delta\leq\beta^{-3/2}
if
\beta\leq\lambda^{1/25}
.
∎

9.2.
Estimates of
\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
Given two geodesic beams
\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}
and
\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}
, and
g\in G_{0}
, we define the pairing

(9.11)

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}.

We apply Proposition
6.11
to bound
P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)
in the following case.

Corollary 9.8
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
Suppose that
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and let
C_{0}>0
be given. If

(9.12)

\displaystyle d(g,e)\leq C_{0}\quad\text{ and }\quad d(n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}}),MA)\geq\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2},

we have

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Proof.

We unfold the integral
P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)
by applying the definition of
\phi_{m,n}
and the inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
k_{\lambda}
so
P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)
is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(\mathbf{z}_{1})\chi(\mathbf{z}_{2})\eta_{n_{1}}(b_{m_{1}}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1})\eta_{n_{2}}(b_{m_{2}}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2})\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{1}})(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{2}})(\mathbf{z}_{2})\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}\right)h_{\lambda}(s)d\nu(s).

We only need to show the inner integral above is
\ll_{A}s^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}
by assuming
|s-\lambda|\leq\beta
.
By the change of variables
\mathbf{z}_{1}\rightarrow b_{m_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{z}_{2}\rightarrow b_{m_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2}
, the inner integral above is equal to

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(b_{m_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1})\chi(b_{m_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2})\eta_{n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\eta_{n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{1}})(b_{m_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1})}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{2}})(b_{m_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2})\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}.

Then we unfold the inverse Helgason transforms inside the integral to obtain

(9.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iint_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\overline{\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{1}}(s_{1},b_{1})}\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m_{2}}(s_{2},b_{2})\left(\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi(b_{m_{1}}\mathbf{z}_{1})\chi(b_{m_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2})\eta_{n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\eta_{n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2})\right.\\
\exp\big((1/2-is_{1})A(b_{1}^{-1}b_{m_{1}}\mathbf{z}_{1})+(1/2+is_{2})A(b_{2}^{-1}b_{m_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{2})\big)d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}\Big)d\mu(s_{1},b_{1})d\mu(s_{2},b_{2}).\end{split}

Since
\supp(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})\cap(\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B)
is contained in
[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]\times\mathbf{I}_{m}\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B
, and the support of
\eta_{n}
is contained in
\mathbf{J}_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}
, after we use the coordinates
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow n(x)a(t)\cdot o\in\mathbb{H}^{2}
, it suffices to prove the following integral

(9.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iint_{\mathbb{R}}&\chi_{1}(x_{1},t_{1},x_{2},t_{2})\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(a(-t_{1})g_{1}a(t_{2}))\exp\left(-is_{1}A(b_{1}^{-1}b_{m_{1}}n(x_{1})a(t_{1}))+{is_{2}A(b_{2}^{-1}b_{m_{2}}n(x_{2})a(t_{2}))}\right)dt_{1}dt_{2}\end{split}

is
\ll_{A}s^{-A}
, provided
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
,
b_{1}\in\mathbf{I}_{m_{1}}
,
x_{1}\in\mathbf{J}_{n_{1}}
,
b_{2}\in\mathbf{I}_{m_{2}}
, and
x_{2}\in\mathbf{J}_{n_{2}}
.
Here
g_{1}=n(-x_{1})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{2})
, and
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
is the combination of
\chi(b_{m_{1}}n(x_{1})a(t_{1})\cdot o)\chi(b_{m_{2}}n(x_{2})a(t_{2})\cdot o)

with all the amplitude factors in the integral. (Here we omit the variables
b_{1},b_{2}
.)
In particular, we have
b_{1}^{-1}b_{m_{1}},b_{2}^{-1}b_{m_{2}}\in\mathbf{I}_{0}
, which is the interval in
B
centered at the identity with length
\ll\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}
.
Applying Corollary
6.5
, there is a constant
\delta>0
, and a uniformly nonvanishing function
\xi
on
(-\delta,\delta)\times(-2,2)^{2}

such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=1-\theta^{2}\xi(\theta,x,t),

for
(\theta,x,t)\in(-\delta,\delta)\times(-2,2)^{2}
. If
Z(\theta,x,t)
is an antiderivative of
\xi
with respect to
t
that is smooth as a function of
(\theta,x,t)
, we may integrate this to obtain

\displaystyle A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=t-\theta^{2}Z(\theta,x,t)+c(\theta,x)

for some function
c(\theta,x)
. In our case, we have

\displaystyle A(b_{j}^{-1}b_{m_{j}}n(x_{j})a(t_{j}))=t_{j}+\rho_{j}(t_{j}),\quad j=1,2,

where if we let
\theta_{j}\in(-\delta,\delta)
satisfying
b_{\theta_{j}}=b_{j}^{-1}b_{m_{j}}
, then

\displaystyle\rho_{j}(t_{j}):=-\theta_{j}^{2}Z(\theta_{j},x_{j},t_{j})+c(\theta_{j},x_{j}),\quad j=1,2,

satisfy the condition (
6.13
) in Proposition
6.11
(by taking
\epsilon_{0}=2\epsilon_{1}
):

\displaystyle\left|s_{j}\frac{\partial\rho_{j}}{\partial t_{j}}(t_{j})\right|\ll s\theta_{j}^{2}|\xi|\ll s\lambda^{-1+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta\ll s^{2\epsilon_{1}}\beta,

\displaystyle\left|s_{j}\frac{\partial^{n}\rho_{j}}{\partial t_{j}^{n}}(t_{j})\right|\ll s\theta_{j}^{2}\left|\frac{\partial^{n-1}\xi}{\partial t_{j}^{n-1}}\right|\ll_{n}s^{2\epsilon_{1}}\beta.

It follows by Proposition
6.11
that (
9.14
) is
\ll_{\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{-A}
if

(9.15)

\displaystyle d(g_{1},e)\text{ is bounded, }\;\;\text{ and }\;\;d(g_{1},MA)\geq\frac{1}{2}s^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}.

Recall that
g_{1}=n(-x_{1})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{2})
.
Thus, it suffices to show our assumptions (
9.12
) can imply (
9.15
).
Since

\displaystyle n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}})=n(-x_{n_{1}}+x_{1})g_{1}n(x_{n_{2}}-x_{2})

and
|x_{n_{i}}-x_{i}|\ll\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}
, we have

(9.16)

\displaystyle d(n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}}),g_{1})\ll\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}.

Then if
\lambda
is sufficiently large, (
9.12
), (
9.16
) and the triangle inequality imply (
9.15
).
∎

Given
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
, we define

(9.17)

\displaystyle\varphi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})=\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z})\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z}).

Note that
\varphi_{m,n}
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
but is not compactly supported. From (
9.1
), we have
\phi_{m,n}=\chi\varphi_{m,n}
, and this relation implies that

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\chi\varphi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\chi\varphi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=P(\phi_{m,n},\phi_{m,n},g;\lambda).

Here
I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)
is defined as in Proposition
4.3
. Corollary
9.8
directly implies the following.

Corollary 9.9
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
and let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
).
Suppose that
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and
d(g,e)\leq 1
. If

\displaystyle d\left(\left(b_{m}n(x_{n})\right)^{-1}g\left(b_{m}n(x_{n})\right),MA\right)\geq\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2},

then we have

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

We also want to bound
I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)
without conditions on
g
. Since the support of
\phi_{m,n}
is at a scale
\asymp\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}\times 1
, we have the following control of its
L^{1}
-norm.

Lemma 9.10
.

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\lambda^{-1/4}\beta^{1/4}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof.

Let
\chi^{\prime}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
satisfying

•

0\leq\chi^{\prime}\leq 1
,

•

\chi^{\prime}\equiv 1
on
\{(x,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{2}:x\in\mathbf{J}_{n},-C\leq t\leq C\}
,

•

\supp(\chi^{\prime})\subset\{(x,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{2}:x\in 2\mathbf{J}_{n},-2C\leq t\leq 2C\}
.

Here
C
is a sufficiently large constant so that
\supp(\chi(b\cdot))
is contained in
\{(x,e^{t})\in\mathbb{H}^{2}:-C\leq x,t\leq C\}
for any
b\in B
,
and
2\mathbf{J}_{n}
is the closed interval in
\mathbb{R}
centered at
x_{n}
with length
2|\mathbf{J}_{n}|
.

Then we have
\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})=\chi^{\prime}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})\right|d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\chi^{\prime}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})\right|d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{z})\right|^{2}d\mathbf{z}\right)^{1/2}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-1/4}\beta^{1/4}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

∎

Lemma 9.11
.

Fix a smooth cutoff function
\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
.
Suppose
|s-\lambda|\leq\beta
,
\beta\leq 3s^{1/4}

and
u\in K_{0}
satisfying

(9.18)

\displaystyle d(u,Mb_{m}^{-1})\geq s^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}.

We have

(9.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z})\overline{\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})}\exp\left(isA(u\mathbf{z})\right)d\mathbf{z}\ll s^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

The implied constant depends on
\epsilon_{1}
, and the size of the first
n
derivatives of
\chi_{2}
and
\chi
, where
n
depends on
\epsilon_{1}
and
A
.

Proof.

We apply the definition for
\phi_{m,n}
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z})\overline{\phi_{m,n}(\mathbf{z})}\exp\left(isA(u\mathbf{z})\right)d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z})\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\overline{\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widetilde{\phi}\tau_{m})(\mathbf{z})}\exp\left(isA(u\mathbf{z})\right)d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z})\chi(\mathbf{z})\eta_{n}(b_{m}^{-1}\mathbf{z})\exp\left((-ir+1/2)A(b^{-1}{\mathbf{z}})\right)\exp\left(isA(u\mathbf{z})\right)d\mathbf{z}\right)\overline{\widetilde{\phi}(r,b)}\tau_{m}(b)d\mu(r,b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times B}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{3}(x,t,b)\exp(isA(ub_{m}n(x)a(t))-irA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t)))dt\right)\eta_{n}(x)\overline{\widetilde{\phi}(r,b)}\tau_{m}(b)\,dx\,d\mu(r,b),

where

\displaystyle\chi_{3}(x,t,b)=\chi_{2}(b_{m}\mathbf{z})\chi(b_{m}\mathbf{z})e^{A(b^{-1}b_{m}\mathbf{z})/2-t},\quad\text{ for }\mathbf{z}=(x,e^{t}),

is a smooth and compactly supported function on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times B
. We may later omit the variables
x
and
b
for
\chi_{3}
. Because of the supports of
\widetilde{\phi}
and
\tau_{m}
, to prove the bound (
9.19
), it suffices to prove the following bound

(9.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi_{3}(t)\exp(isA(ub_{m}n(x)a(t))-irA(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t)))dt\ll s^{-A}

for
r\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
and
b\in\mathbf{I}_{m}
. Hence,
b^{-1}b_{m}\in\mathbf{I}_{0}
. Applying Corollary
6.5
, there is a constant
\delta>0
, and a uniformly nonvanishing function
\xi
on
(-\delta,\delta)\times(-2,2)^{2}

such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=1-\theta^{2}\xi(\theta,x,t),

for
(\theta,x,t)\in(-\delta,\delta)\times(-2,2)^{2}
.
We denote by
b_{\theta}=b^{-1}b_{m}
where
|\theta|\ll\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}
.
If
Z(\theta,x,t)
is an antiderivative of
\xi
with respect to
t
that is smooth as a function of
(\theta,x,t)
, we may integrate this to obtain

\displaystyle A(b_{\theta}n(x)a(t))=t-\theta^{2}Z(\theta,x,t)+c(\theta,x)

for some function
c(\theta,x)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle A(b^{-1}b_{m}n(x)a(t))=t+\rho(t),

where

\displaystyle\rho(t)=-\theta^{2}Z(\theta,x,t)+c(\theta,x)

satisfies the condition (
6.4
) in Proposition
6.6
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\left|r\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}(t)\right|\ll r\theta^{2}|\xi|\ll r\lambda^{-1+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta\ll r^{2\epsilon_{1}}\beta,

\displaystyle\left|r\frac{\partial^{n}\rho}{\partial t^{n}}(t)\right|\ll r\theta^{2}\left|\frac{\partial^{n-1}\xi}{\partial t^{n-1}}\right|\ll_{n}r^{2\epsilon_{1}}\beta.

We take
\epsilon_{0}=2\epsilon_{1}
.
The condition (
6.3
) will follow from (
9.18
). That is, there exists a constant
B>0
so that
d(ub_{m},M)\geq Bs^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
. Then we deduce (
9.20
) from Proposition
6.6
.
∎

Proposition 9.12
.

Suppose
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
. Let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
). Then we have

I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

for all
g\in G_{0}
with
d(g,e)\leq 1
.

This bound follows directly from a more general result as follows.

Proposition 9.13
.

Suppose
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
. Let
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
be the decomposition given by (
9.1
). Then we have

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\|\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

for all
g\in G_{0}
with
d(g,e)\leq 1
.

Proof.

We first unfold the integral
P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)
by the inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
k_{\lambda}
and denote by

\displaystyle Q(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;s)=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{s}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}.

Then

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}Q(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;s)h_{\lambda}(s)d\nu(s).

By applying Lemma
9.10
, it suffices to prove

(9.21)

\displaystyle Q(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;s)\ll s^{-1+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta\|\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\|\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}+O_{A}(s^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2})

for
s\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
. As before, we write
\mathbf{z}_{j}=n(x_{j})a(t_{j})\cdot o=(x_{j},e^{t_{j}})
.
If we apply the functional equation
\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}=\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
, write
\varphi_{-s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
as an integral over
K_{0}
by the integral formula (
3.2
), and apply Lemma
6.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle Q(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\varphi^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}_{-s}(a(-t_{1})n(-x_{1})gn(x_{2})a(t_{2}))d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\exp\left((1-is)A(ka(-t_{1})n(-x_{1})gn(x_{2})a(t_{2}))\right)dkd\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\exp\left((1-is)\left(A(ugn(x_{2})a(t_{2}))-A(un(x_{1})a(t_{1}))\right)\right)\left|\det\frac{\partial k}{\partial u}\right|dud\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}.

Here we use
\partial k/\partial u
to denote the Jacobian matrix of the diffeomorphism
u\mapstochar\rightarrow k
from
M\backslash K_{0}
onto itself, which is defined by the relation
ka(-t_{1})n(-x_{1})\in NAu
, or equivalently
un(x_{1})a(t_{1})\in NAk
. Let
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2})
be a fixed cutoff function so that
\phi_{m,n}\chi_{1}=\phi_{m,n}
for arbitrary
m,n
, and let

\displaystyle\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z}_{1},u)=\chi_{1}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\exp(-A(un(x_{1})a(t_{1})))\left|\det\frac{\partial k}{\partial u}\right|,

which lies in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{2}\times M\backslash K_{0})
.
We have

\displaystyle Q(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z}_{1},u)\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\exp\left(isA(un(x_{1})a(t_{1}))\right)d\mathbf{z}_{1}\right)\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})e^{(1-is)A(ugn(x_{2})a(t_{2}))}dud\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\int_{M\backslash K_{0}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\chi_{2}(\mathbf{z}_{1},u)\overline{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\exp\left(isA(un(x_{1})a(t_{1}))\right)d\mathbf{z}_{1}\right|du\cdot\int_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\left|\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\right|d\mathbf{z}_{2}.

We apply Lemma
9.11
to get the above integral inside the absolute value is
\ll_{A}s^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
if
d(u,Mb_{m_{1}}^{-1})\geq s^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}
.
This implies that we may restrict the integral over
M\backslash K_{0}
to the disc of radius
s^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}
centered at
Mb_{m_{1}}
, and this gives the main term in the estimate (
9.21
).
∎

To apply the amplification inequality from (
4.1
), we need first to construct an element
\mathcal{T}_{v}\in\mathcal{H}_{v}
for a finite place
v
of
F
that will form part of the amplifier
\mathcal{T}
. Recall that we have defined Hecke operators
T_{v}(1),T_{v}(2)
in Section
2.3
. In this case, we consider those
v\in\mathscr{Q}
, that is,
v
is inert.
Note that the Hecke operator
T_{v}(1)
(resp.
T_{v}(2)
) corresponds to the operator summing over the set of nodes at distance 2 (resp. 4) from the given node in the Bruhat-Tits tree for
\mathrm{PGL}(2,E_{v})
. Then an elementary computation, or applying the classical relation
[
12
, §1.4, (4.12)]
, gives the following Hecke relation:

(9.22)

\displaystyle T_{v}(1)*T_{v}(1)=q_{v}^{2}(q_{v}^{2}+1)+(q_{v}^{2}-1)T_{v}(1)+T_{v}(2).

If we define the real numbers
\tau_{v}(1)
and
\tau_{v}(2)
by

\displaystyle T_{v}(1)\psi=\tau_{v}(1)q_{v}^{2}\psi,\quad T_{v}(2)\psi=\tau_{v}(2)q_{v}^{4}\psi,

then (
9.22
) implies that we cannot have both
|\tau_{v}(1)|\leq 1/4
and
|\tau_{v}(2)|\leq 1/4
.
We define

(9.23)

\displaystyle T_{v}=\begin{cases}{T_{v}(1)}/\tau_{v}(1)q_{v}^{2}\quad\text{ if }|\tau_{v}(1)|>1/4,\\
{T_{v}(2)}/\tau_{v}(2)q_{v}^{4}\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

It follows that
T_{v}\psi=\psi
for all
v\in\mathscr{Q}
. Note that
|K_{v}(1)/K_{v}|\asymp q_{v}^{4}
and
|K_{v}(2)/K_{v}|\asymp q_{v}^{8}
, so
\|T_{v}(1)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\asymp q_{v}^{4}
and
\|T_{v}(2)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\asymp q_{v}^{8}
. Hence,

(9.24)

\displaystyle\|T_{v}\|_{L^{2}}\ll 1.

Proof of Proposition
9.6
.

Let
N\geq 1
be a parameter to be chosen later. We define
\mathscr{Q}_{N}=\{v\in\mathscr{Q}|N/2\leq q_{v}\leq N\}
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{N}=\sum_{v\in\mathscr{Q}_{N}}T_{v},

where
T_{v}
’s are defined by (
9.23
). This choice of
\mathcal{T}_{N}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{T}_{N}\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle|=\#\mathscr{Q}_{N}\cdot|\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle|\gg N^{1-\epsilon}|\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle|.

By Proposition
4.3
applied to
\varphi_{m,n}
, we have

(9.25)

\displaystyle|\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle|^{2}\ll N^{-2+\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Recall that we use
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset G_{0}
to denote the compact subset consisting of
g\in G_{0}
with
d(g,e)\leq 1
.
Corollary
9.9
implies that we only need to consider the terms in (
9.25
) with

\displaystyle d\left(\left(g_{0}b_{m}n(x_{n})\right)^{-1}\gamma\left(g_{0}b_{m}n(x_{n})\right),MA\right)<\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
g_{0}^{\prime}:=g_{0}b_{m}n(x_{n})\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
and
d(g_{0}^{\prime-1}\gamma g_{0}^{\prime},e)\leq 2
if
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}
.
Let
C>0
be the constant as in Proposition
8.2
.
We choose

\displaystyle X=(C^{-1}\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2})^{-1}=C\lambda^{1/2-2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2},

so then take
\delta=CX^{-1}=\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2}
.
We use the uniform bound

\displaystyle|I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})|\ll\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2})

from Proposition
9.12
to obtain

(9.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{\prime-1}\gamma g_{0}^{\prime},MA)\leq\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\\
\ll&\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{\prime-1}\gamma g_{0}^{\prime},MA)\leq\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)\right|+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}).\end{split}

We next expand
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
as a sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathfrak{n}}1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}

for some constants
a_{\mathfrak{n}}
.
If we now suppose
N^{4}\leq X
, that is

\displaystyle N\leq C^{1/4}\lambda^{1/8-\epsilon_{1}/2}\beta^{-1/8},

then our choice of
X
and
\delta
means that we may apply Proposition
8.2
to show that for any
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
appearing in the expansion of
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
,
the term
1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}
makes a contribution
\ll_{\epsilon}X^{\epsilon}\ll_{\epsilon}\lambda^{\epsilon}
to the sum in the right hand side of (
9.26
).
Moreover, the sum of
a_{\mathfrak{n}}
’s with
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
is

(9.27)

\displaystyle\ll\left(\sum_{v\in\mathscr{Q},N/2\leq q_{v}\leq N}({q_{v}^{-2}}+{q_{v}^{-4}})\right)^{2}+\sum_{v\in\mathscr{Q},N/2\leq q_{v}\leq N}{q_{v}^{-2}}\ll 1.

Hence, we only need to consider the term
\mathfrak{n}=\mathcal{O}
. By the bound (
9.24
), we have

(9.28)

\displaystyle a_{\mathcal{O}}=[\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}](e)=\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{v\in\mathscr{Q}_{N}}\|T_{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ll N.

By applying (
9.28
) in (
9.26
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{\prime-1}\gamma g_{0}^{\prime},MA)\leq\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\varphi_{m,n},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}(N+\lambda^{\epsilon})\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}).

We conclude that

\displaystyle|\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle|^{2}\ll\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}N^{-2+\epsilon}(N+\lambda^{\epsilon})\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}).

Choosing
N=C^{1/4}\lambda^{1/8-\epsilon_{1}/2}\beta^{-1/8}
gives (
9.9
).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Propositions
9.4
and
9.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\leq(\#\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi))^{1/2}\left(\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{\geq\delta}(\phi)}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\ll\delta^{-1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}

Then we combine (
9.9
) with the above inequality to get (
9.10
).
∎

9.3.
Estimates for the sums of
\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle

We first provide preliminary estimates for proving Proposition
9.7
.
Given
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
with the decomposition
\chi\phi=\sum_{m,n}\phi_{m,n}
given by (
9.1
), for any
0<\delta<1
, we define

\displaystyle\phi_{\delta}=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\phi_{m,n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\varphi_{\delta}=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\varphi_{m,n}.

Recall that here
\varphi_{m,n}
is defined by (
9.17
) and we have

\phi_{m,n}=\chi\varphi_{m,n}
, and so
\phi_{\delta}=\chi\varphi_{\delta}
.

Proposition 9.14
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
.
For any
0<\delta<1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\ll\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

Proof.

We have

(9.29)

\displaystyle\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}=\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\|\phi_{m,n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},{\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\\
(m_{1},n_{1})\neq(m_{2}.n_{2})\end{subarray}}\langle\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\rangle.

The first term in the right-hand side of (
9.29
) is
\ll\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}
by Propositions
9.4
.
The second term is also
\ll\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}
by Proposition
9.3
.
∎

Proposition 9.15
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
. For any
0<\delta<1
and
g\in H^{\prime}
, we have

I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)\ll\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\ll\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Proof.

Since
g\in H^{\prime}
,
g
is an isometry of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
. We can define the translation

\displaystyle\phi_{\delta}^{g}(\mathbf{z}):=\phi_{\delta}(g\mathbf{z}),

which is still a function on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
We may see that
\|\phi_{\delta}^{g}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}=\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}
.
Then

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{\delta}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{\delta}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{-1}g\mathbf{z}_{2})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{\delta}(\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{\delta}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{2}^{-1}g^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\overline{\phi_{\delta}(g\mathbf{z}_{1})}\phi_{\delta}(\mathbf{z}_{2})k_{\lambda}(\mathbf{z}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1})d\mathbf{z}_{1}d\mathbf{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{\delta}\times k_{\lambda},\phi^{g}_{\delta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{\phi_{\delta}}\widetilde{k_{\lambda}},\widetilde{\phi^{g}_{\delta}}\rangle

\displaystyle\ll\|\widetilde{k_{\lambda}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\phi_{\delta}^{g}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}\|\phi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}.

The proposition then follows from Proposition
5.4
and Proposition
9.14
.
∎

Proposition 9.16
.

Suppose that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap H_{\beta}
,
0<\delta<1
, and
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
.
Let
0<\omega<1
be a small parameter satisfying
\omega^{-1}=o(\lambda^{1/2-2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2})
.
Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
, such that the following holds.
If
d(g,e)\leq 1
and

d\left(g,H^{\prime}\right)\geq C\omega,

then we have

I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{1},A}\delta\omega^{-2}\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Proof.

We may write

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)=\sum_{\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)

By Corollary
9.8
, if

(9.30)

\displaystyle d(n(-x_{n_{1}})b_{m_{1}}^{-1}gb_{m_{2}}n(x_{n_{2}}),MA)\geq\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2},

then

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)\ll_{\epsilon_{1},A}\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

We then need to consider the quadruples
(b_{m_{1}},x_{n_{1}},b_{m_{2}},x_{n_{2}})
not satisfying (
9.30
).
By Proposition
7.8
, there exists a
C>0
, so that as long as
d(g,e)\leq 1
and

d\left(g,H^{\prime}\right)\geq C\omega
,
the number of quadruples not satisfying (
9.30
) is

\displaystyle\ll\omega^{-2}(\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2})^{4}N_{1}^{2}N_{2}^{2}\ll\omega^{-2}(\lambda^{-1/2+2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{1/2})^{4}(\lambda^{1/2-\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2})^{2}(\lambda^{1/2}\beta^{-1/2})^{2}\ll\omega^{-2}\lambda^{6\epsilon_{1}}.

For each pair
\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}
,
by Proposition
9.13
, we have

\displaystyle P(\phi_{m_{1},n_{1}},\phi_{m_{2},n_{2}},g;\lambda)\ll\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\delta\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g)\ll

\displaystyle\left(\omega^{-2}\lambda^{6\epsilon_{1}}\right)\cdot\left(\lambda^{1/2+4\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\delta\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\right)+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\omega^{-2}\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}+O_{A}(\lambda^{-A}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}).

∎

Let
v\in\mathscr{P}
be a finite place that is split in
E
.
Similar to (
9.22
), we have the Hecke relation in
\mathcal{H}_{v}
:

(9.31)

\displaystyle T_{v}(1)*T_{v}(1)=q_{v}(q_{v}+1)+(q_{v}-1)T_{v}(1)+T_{v}(2).

If we define the real numbers
\tau_{v}(1)
and
\tau_{v}(2)
by

\displaystyle T_{v}(1)\psi=\tau_{v}(1)q_{v}\psi,\quad T_{v}(2)\psi=\tau_{v}(2)q_{v}^{2}\psi,

then (
9.31
) implies that we cannot have both
|\tau_{v}(1)|\leq 1/4
and
|\tau_{v}(2)|\leq 1/4
.
We define

(9.32)

\displaystyle T_{v}=\begin{cases}{T_{v}(1)}/\tau_{v}(1)q_{v}\quad\text{ if }|\tau_{v}(1)|>1/4,\\
{T_{v}(2)}/\tau_{v}(2)q_{v}^{2}\quad\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

It follows that
T_{v}\psi=\psi
for all
v\in\mathscr{P}
. Note that
|K_{v}(1,0)/K_{v}|\asymp q_{v}^{2}
and
|K_{v}(2,0)/K_{v}|\asymp q_{v}^{4}
, so
\|T_{v}(1)\|_{L^{1}}=\|T_{v}(1)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\asymp q_{v}^{2}
and
\|T_{v}(2)\|_{L^{1}}=\|T_{v}(2)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\asymp q_{v}^{4}
. Hence,

(9.33)

\displaystyle\|T_{v}\|_{L^{1}}\ll q_{v}^{2},

and

(9.34)

\displaystyle\|T_{v}\|_{L^{2}}\ll 1.

Proof of Proposition
9.7
.

Let
N\geq 1
be a parameter to be chosen later and let
X=N^{4}
. Apply Proposition
8.3
to
g=g_{0}
to get a constant
C_{1}
(which is denoted by
C
in Proposition
8.3
) and the set of primes
\mathscr{P}_{0}
. We define
\mathscr{P}_{N}=\{v\in\mathscr{P}_{0}\,|\,N/2\leq q_{v}\leq N\}
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{N}=\sum_{v\in\mathscr{P}_{N}}T_{v},

where
T_{v}
’s are defined by (
9.32
). This choice of
\mathcal{T}_{N}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{T}_{N}\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle|=\#\mathscr{P}_{N}\cdot|\langle\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle|\gg N^{1-\epsilon}|\langle\psi,\phi_{\delta}\rangle|=N^{1-\epsilon}\left|\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\right|.

By Proposition
4.3
, it follows that

(9.35)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\right|^{2}\ll_{\epsilon}N^{-2+\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Here
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset G_{0}
is the compact subset consisting of
g\in G_{0}
with
d(g,e)\leq 1
.
Let
C>0
be the constant given by Proposition
9.16
.
We choose
\omega=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}C_{1}X^{-8}=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}C_{1}N^{-32}
, and break the sum in (
9.35
) into those terms with
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})<C\omega
and the complement. We denote the complement by
D
, that is

\displaystyle D=\{\gamma\in G(F)|g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}\text{ and }d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})\geq C\omega\}.

To estimate the sum over
D
,
we first use the bound

|I(\lambda,\phi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})|\ll\delta\omega^{-2}\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\ll N^{64}\delta\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}

from Proposition
9.16
.
Here, we need to assume
0<\omega<1
satisfies

(9.36)

\displaystyle\omega^{-1}=o(\lambda^{1/2-2\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{-1/2}).

This gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in D}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|

\displaystyle\ll N^{64}\delta\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\cap g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)\right|

\displaystyle\ll N^{64}\delta\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}\|\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}\|_{L^{1}}.

Note that (
9.33
) gives
\|T_{v}\|_{L^{1}}\ll N^{2}
so
\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{L^{1}}\ll N^{3}
, and therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in D}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\phi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll N^{70}\delta\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

We next estimate the sum over
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})<C\omega
.
We first expand
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
as a sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathfrak{n}}1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}

for some constants
a_{\mathfrak{n}}
.
Our choice of
X
,
\omega
and the constant
C_{1}
means that we may apply Proposition
8.3
to show that for any
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
appearing in the expansion of
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
,
the term
1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}
makes no contribution to the sum in the right-hand side of (
9.35
).
Hence, we only need to consider the term
\mathfrak{n}=\mathcal{O}
. By the bound (
9.34
), we have

(9.37)

\displaystyle a_{\mathcal{O}}=[\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}](e)=\|\mathcal{T}_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{v\in\mathscr{P}_{N}}\|T_{v}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\ll N.

Hence,

(9.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})<C\omega\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\phi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll N\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})<C\omega\end{subarray}}1_{K_{f}}(\gamma)\left|I(\lambda,\phi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Since
\gamma
varies in a finite set, we may assume that only the identity contributes to the right-hand side of (
9.38
).
We apply the bound

|I(\lambda,\varphi_{\delta},e)|\ll\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}
from Proposition
9.15
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},H^{\prime})<C\omega\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)I(\lambda,\phi_{\delta},g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll N\lambda^{1/2}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Adding our two estimates, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\phi_{m,n}\in\mathcal{S}_{<\delta}(\phi)}\langle\psi,\phi_{m,n}\rangle\right|^{2}\ll N^{-2+\epsilon}(N^{70}\delta\lambda^{1/2+10\epsilon_{1}}\beta^{3/2}+N\lambda^{1/2})\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{2})}^{2}.

Choosing
N=\delta^{-1/69}\beta^{-1/46}=(\delta^{1/3}\beta^{1/2})^{-1/23}

gives the desired bound.
Finally, we check that
N\geq 1
and

\omega=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}C_{1}N^{-32}=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}C_{1}\delta^{32/69}\beta^{48/69}=\frac{1}{2}C^{-1}C_{1}(\delta^{1/3}\beta^{1/2})^{32/23}

satisfies the assumption (
9.36
) provided
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and
\lambda^{-1/2}\beta^{1/2}\leq\delta\leq\beta^{-3/2}
with
\epsilon_{1}
sufficiently small.
∎

10.
Kakeya-Nikodym norms

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.2
.

10.1.
Notation and sketch of proof

We first recall the Helgason transform on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
,
the Helgason transform of
f
is defined by

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(s,kM):=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}f(x)e^{(1-is)A(k^{-1}x)}dx,

where
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
kM\in K_{0}/M
.
The boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
is naturally identified with
K_{0}/M
.
For
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
x\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we denote by

\varphi_{s}(x)=\varphi_{s}^{\mathbb{H}^{3}}(x)

the spherical function on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with spectral parameter
s
.
We shall omit the superscript for the spherical functions in this section.
If
f
is left
K_{0}
-invariant, then its Helgason transform agrees with the Harish-Chandra transform on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, that is

\displaystyle\widehat{f}(s,kM)=\widehat{f}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}f(x)\varphi_{-s}(x)dx

for any
s\in\mathbb{C}
and
kM\in K_{0}/M
. For
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
, their convolution, which we denote by
f\times g
, is defined as the convolution on the group
G_{0}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle(f\times g)(x):=\int_{G_{0}}f(y\cdot o)g(y^{-1}\cdot x)dy.

If moreover
g
is assumed to be
K_{0}
-invariant, then

\displaystyle\widehat{f\times g}(s,kM)=\widehat{f}(s,kM)\widehat{g}(s).

We recall the Plancherel formula on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
For
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{K_{0}/M}\widehat{f}(s,kM)\overline{\widehat{g}(s,kM)}d\nu(s)dk.

Here we normalize the Haar measure on
K_{0}/M
so that the total volume is 1.

Let
l_{[-1/2,1/2]}
be the oriented vertical geodesic centered at
o
of unit length, i.e.,

\displaystyle l_{[-1/2,1/2]}=\{a(t)\cdot o:t\in[-1/2,1/2]\},

and the positive orientation is upward.
Any oriented geodesic of unit length in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
is equal to
g\cdot l_{[-1/2,1/2]}

for some
g\in G_{0}
.
We define the
\lambda^{-1/2}
-tube of
l_{[-1/2,1/2]}
to be

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{T}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}=\{(z,e^{t}):|z|\leq\lambda^{-1/2},|t|\leq 1/2\}.

The
\lambda^{-1/2}
-tube of
g\cdot l_{[-1/2,1/2]}
is defined to be
g\cdot\mathscr{T}
.
Fix
g_{0}\in\Omega_{v_{0}}
.
We shall consider the
L^{2}
-norm problem for
\psi
restricted to the tube
g_{0}\cdot\mathscr{T}
.

Let
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}
be the open disc of radius
\lambda^{-1/2}
centered at
0
in
\mathbb{C}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{D}_{\lambda^{-1/2}}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<\lambda^{-1/2}\},

and denote by
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
its closure.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
the space of Schwartz functions on the Euclidean space
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
and let
\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
be the subspace consisting of Schwartz functions supported in
\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\phi(z,t)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
.
We shall consider the Fourier transform in the
t
-variable, i.e.,

\displaystyle(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)(z,s):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(z,t)e^{-ist}dt.

We will denote the Fourier inversion by
\mathscr{F}_{t}^{-1}
.
For
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
, we have the integral formula for the Fourier inversion

\displaystyle\phi(z,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)(z,s)e^{ist}ds.

If
b\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times(-1,1))
is a non-negative cutoff function and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
,
we define the inner product on
g_{0}\cdot\mathscr{T}
by

\displaystyle\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{D}}\psi(g_{0}n(z)a(t)\cdot o)\overline{b(z,t)\phi(z,t)}e^{-2t}dtdz.

Here the
e^{-2t}
factor comes from the hyperbolic metric.

The same as the previous sections, we let
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
be a small constant satisfying
\epsilon^{\prime}\leq 10^{-6}
.
Let
\beta
be a parameter satisfying
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
.
We shall bound the part of
b\psi
with Fourier transform away from the spectrum
\lambda
using a purely analytic argument,
and bound the part with Fourier transform near the spectrum via the method of arithmetic amplification.
Theorem
1.2
follows from combining the following propositions with the choice
\beta=\lambda^{1/5}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

If
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\|\phi\|_{2}=1
and

\supp(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times(\mathbb{R}\backslash\pm[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]),

we have
\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\beta^{-1/4+\epsilon}
.

Proposition 10.2
.

If
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\|\phi\|_{2}=1
and

\supp(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times(\pm[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]),

we have
\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon}\lambda^{-1/16+\epsilon}\beta^{1/16}
.

We construct the same test function as in Section
4.2
.
We fix a real-valued function
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of Paley-Wiener type that is nonnegative and satisfies
h(0)=1
. Define
h_{\lambda}^{0}(s)=h(s-\lambda)+h(-s-\lambda)
, and let
k_{\lambda}^{0}
be the
K_{0}
-bi-invariant function on
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with Harish-Chandra transform
h^{0}_{\lambda}
.
The Paley-Wiener theorem implies that
k_{\lambda}^{0}
is of compact support that may be chosen arbitrarily small. Define
k_{\lambda}=k_{\lambda}^{0}*k_{\lambda}^{0}
, which has Harish-Chandra transform
h_{\lambda}=(h_{\lambda}^{0})^{2}
.

Let
b\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times(-1,1))
be a non-negative cutoff function.
If
g\in G_{0}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
, we define

\displaystyle J(\lambda,\phi,g)=\iint_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\overline{b\phi(z_{1},t_{1}})b\phi(z_{2},t_{2})k_{\lambda}(a(-t_{1})n(-z_{1})gn(z_{2})a(t_{2}))e^{-2t_{1}-2t_{2}}dt_{1}dz_{1}dt_{2}dz_{2}.

We prove a similar inequality as in Proposition
4.3
for geodesic tubes.

Proposition 10.3
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{H}^{S}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R})
. We have

(10.1)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi,b\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\ll\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}\left|(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(\gamma)J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Proof.

We consider the same kernel function as in Proposition
4.3
with the spectral expansion

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}k_{\infty}(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(x^{-1}\gamma y)=\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\mathcal{T}\psi_{i}(x)\overline{\mathcal{T}\psi_{i}(y)}

on
\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathbf{G}(F)\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A})
.
If we integrate it against
\overline{b\phi}\times b\phi
on
g_{0}\mathscr{T}\times g_{0}\mathscr{T}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i}h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\left|\langle\mathcal{T}\psi,{b}\phi\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)}(\mathcal{T}*\mathcal{T}^{*})(\gamma)J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0}).

Since we have
h_{\lambda}(\lambda_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
, dropping all terms but
\psi
completes the proof.
∎

10.2.
Bounds away from the spectrum

We start the proof for Proposition
10.1
.
In Proposition
10.3
, we can choose
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{f}
to be the characteristic function of a
sufficiently small open subgroup of
\mathbf{G}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, then only the identity element
\gamma=e
will make a nonzero contribution to the sum in (
10.1
). This gives

(10.2)

\displaystyle\left|\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle\right|^{2}\ll\left|J(\lambda,\phi,e)\right|.

We first decompose
k_{\lambda}
along the radial direction.
Without loss of generality, we assume the support of
k_{\lambda}(a(t))
is contained in
[-1,1]
.
Let
b_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a cutoff function that is equal to
1
on
[-1,1]
and zero outside
[-2,2]
.
Moreover, we suppose that
b_{0}
is even and
0\leq b_{0}\leq 1
.
Then
k_{\lambda}(a(t))=b_{0}(t)k_{\lambda}(a(t))
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Define
b_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to be a function satisfying

•

0\leq b_{1}\leq 1
and
b_{1}
is even,

•

b_{1}(t)=1
for
|t|\leq\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
,

•

b_{1}(t)=0
for
|t|\geq 2\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
.

Let
b_{2}=b_{0}-b_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Since
b_{i}
are constant in some neighborhood of
0
,
we can extend the domains of
b_{i}
to
\mathbb{H}^{3}
by the polar coordinates, i.e.,

(10.3)

\displaystyle b_{i}(n(z)a(t)):=b_{i}(t_{0}),\text{ where }t_{0}\text{ satisfies the relation }n(z)a(t)\in K_{0}a(t_{0})K_{0},

so that
b_{i}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
are bi-
K_{0}
-invariant, and the restrictions
b_{i}\circ a(t)
are the original functions,
i=0,1,2
.
Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
be bi-
K_{0}
-invariant functions so that

k_{i}=b_{i}k_{\lambda}
.
Then we decompose the integral
J(\lambda,\phi,e)=J_{1}(\phi)+J_{2}(\phi)

where

\displaystyle J_{i}(\phi)=\iint_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\overline{b\phi(z_{1},t_{1}})b\phi(z_{2},t_{2})k_{i}(n(e^{-t_{1}}(z_{2}-z_{1}))a(t_{2}-t_{1}))e^{-2t_{1}-2t_{2}}dt_{1}dz_{1}dt_{2}dz_{2},\quad i=1,2.

By the choice of the width of
\supp(b_{1})
,
we will show that
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfying
\|\phi\|_{2}=1
and
\supp(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)\subset\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times(\mathbb{R}\backslash\pm[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta])
, we have

(10.4)

\displaystyle J_{1}(\phi)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}},

and

(10.5)

\displaystyle J_{2}(\phi)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{0},A}\lambda^{-A}.

Hence, Proposition
10.1
will follow from (
10.2
), (
10.4
) and (
10.5
).

10.2.1.
Proof of (
10.4
)

We define
\Phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
by
\Phi(n(z)a(t)\cdot o):=b\phi(z,t)
.
It may be seen that
J_{1}(\phi)=\langle\Phi\times k_{1},\Phi\rangle_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
.
Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
is the inner product on
L^{2}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
with respect to the hyperbolic metric.
By the Plancherel formula,

\displaystyle J_{1}(\phi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{K_{0}/M}\widehat{\Phi}(s,k)\widehat{k_{1}}(s)\overline{\widehat{\Phi}(s,k)}d\nu(s)dk\ll\|\widehat{k_{1}}\|_{\infty}\|\Phi\|_{2}^{2}\ll\|\widehat{k_{1}}\|_{\infty}.

We will prove the following pointwise bound for
\widehat{k_{1}}
,
and the bound (
10.4
) will follow from this immediately.

Proposition 10.4
.

If
|s|\leq\lambda/2
, then
\widehat{k_{1}}(s)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},A}\lambda^{-A}
.

If
|s|\geq\lambda/2
, then
\widehat{k_{1}}(s)\ll\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
.

We first prove the case
|s|\geq\lambda/2
