. We write the integral via the polar coordinates. There exists a constant
c>0
so that

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{1}}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}b_{1}k_{\lambda}(x)\varphi_{-s}(x)dx

\displaystyle=c\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)k_{\lambda}(a(t))\varphi_{-s}(a(t))\sinh(t)^{2}dt.

The bounds
k_{\lambda}(a(t))\ll\lambda^{2}(1+\lambda|t|)^{-1}
from
[
30
, Lemma 2.8]
, and
\varphi_{-s}(a(t))\ll(1+|st|)^{-1}
from
[
30
, Theorem 1.3]
with
|s|\geq\lambda/2
imply that

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{1}}(s)\ll\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{1}(t)(\lambda t^{-1})(\lambda t)^{-1}\sinh(t)^{2}dt\ll\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}.

Now we consider the case
|s|\leq\lambda/2
. We apply the inverse Harish-Chandra transform to
k_{\lambda}
, and apply the integral expansion of the spherical function.
We obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{k_{1}}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}b_{1}(x)\varphi_{r}(x)\varphi_{-s}(x)dx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\int_{K_{0}}b_{1}(x)\varphi_{r}(x)\exp((1-is)A(kx))dkdx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}b_{1}(x)\varphi_{r}(x)\exp((1-is)A(x))dx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)d\nu(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{K_{0}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}b_{1}(x)\exp((1+ir)A(kx))\exp((1-is)A(x))dx\right)h_{\lambda}(r)dkd\nu(r).

It suffices to show for
|r-\lambda|\leq\beta/4
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}b_{1}(x)\exp((1+ir)A(kx))\exp((1-is)A(x))dx\ll_{A}r^{-A}.

Combining all amplitude factors and applying the Iwasawa coordinates for
\mathbb{H}^{3}
,
it suffices to show

(10.6)

\displaystyle J(k):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\chi_{1}(x,y,t)\exp(ir(A(kn(x+iy)a(t))-(s/r)t))dxdydt\ll_{A}r^{-A}.

Here
\chi_{1}(x,y,t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is a cutoff function at scale
\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
, and the
k
-variable is ignored for notational simplicity.
We denote by
\rho=s/r
and we can assume it to satisfy
|\rho|\leq 2/3
by our assumptions on
s
and
r
.
The phase function with parameters
k\in K_{0}
and
\rho
is

\displaystyle\phi(x,y,t;k,\rho)=A(kn(x+iy)a(t))-\rho t.

Lemma 10.5
.

If
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is a cutoff function at scale
\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
, and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is real-valued and satisfies

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi(x)|\geq c>0,\;\;\phi^{(n)}(x)\ll_{n}1

for any
x\in\supp(\chi)
and
n\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle\int\chi(x)e^{ir\phi(x)}dx\ll_{A}r^{-A}.

Here
c>0
is a fixed constant.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int\chi(x)e^{ir\phi(x)}dx=\beta^{-3/2+3\epsilon_{0}}\int\chi(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}x)e^{i(r\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}})(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}}\phi(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}x))}dx.

The cutoff function
\chi(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}x)
is now at scale
1
.
The lemma follows by integration by parts.
∎

Hence, to apply the above lemma to
J(k)
for showing (
10.6
), it suffices to give a nonzero lower bound for

\nabla\phi=(\partial\phi/\partial x,\partial\phi/\partial y,\partial\phi/\partial t).

We first calculate the gradient of
A(kn(x+iy)a(t))
.
Let
u(x,y,t;k)=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(x,y,t;k)&\beta(x,y,t;k)\\
-\bar{\beta}(x,y,t;k)&\bar{\alpha}(x,y,t;k)\end{pmatrix}\in K_{0}
such that

\displaystyle kn(x+iy)a(t)\in NAu(x,y,t;k).

For simplicity, we will write
\alpha=\alpha(x,y,t;k)
and
\beta=\beta(x,y,t;k)
.
Then a straight calculation from (
2.1
) shows that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(kn(x+iy)a(t))

\displaystyle=e^{-t}(\alpha\bar{\beta}+\bar{\alpha}\beta),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y}A(kn(x+iy)a(t))

\displaystyle=e^{-t}(\alpha\bar{\beta}-\bar{\alpha}\beta)i,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}A(kn(x+iy)a(t))

\displaystyle=|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}.

We have the following lower bound for
|\nabla\phi|
.

Lemma 10.6
.

There exists a constant
c>0
.
For any
(x,y,t)\in\supp(\chi_{1})
, we have
|\nabla\phi(x,y,t)|\geq c
.

Proof.

By the compactness, it suffices to show that there do not exist
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
so that
|\alpha|^{2}+|\beta|^{2}=1
and

(10.7)

\displaystyle\alpha\bar{\beta}+\bar{\alpha}\beta=\alpha\bar{\beta}-\bar{\alpha}\beta=0,

(10.8)

\displaystyle|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}=\rho.

Suppose otherwise and let
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
be such pair.
(
10.7
) implies that
\alpha_{0}=0
or
\beta_{0}=0
.
Then (
10.8
) shows
\rho=|\alpha_{0}|^{2}-|\beta_{0}|^{2}=\pm 1
,
which gives the contradiction.
∎

10.2.2.
Derivatives of the distance function

The distance function will appear as a part of the phase function in the oscillatory integral we shall study for proving (
10.5
). We will estimate its derivatives.
We consider the projection
G_{0}=K_{0}\overline{A^{+}}K_{0}\rightarrow\overline{A^{+}}
by the Cartan decomposition.
We let

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,\infty)

be the function defined by

\displaystyle n(z)a(t)\in K_{0}a(\mathcal{A}(z,t))K_{0}.

The function
\mathcal{A}(z,t)
is equal to the distance from the origin
o
to
n(z)a(t)\cdot o
under the hyperbolic metric, and is given by

(10.9)

\displaystyle\cosh(\mathcal{A}(z,t))=\frac{1}{2}e^{t}+\frac{1}{2}(|z|^{2}+1)e^{-t}=\cosh(t)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-t}.

Lemma 10.7
.

Suppose
|z|\ll\lambda^{-1/2}
and
\mathcal{A}(z,t)\gg\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
.
Then we have
|t|\gg\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
.

Proof.

Since
|z|\ll\lambda^{-1/2}
, by (
10.9
),

\displaystyle\cosh(\mathcal{A}(z,t))=\cosh(t)+O(\lambda^{-1}).

The lemma follows from the above with the Taylor approximation for
\cosh
.
∎

Proposition 10.8
.

Suppose
|z|\ll\lambda^{-1/2}
,
t\ll 1
, and
\mathcal{A}(z,t)\gg\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
. Then we have

(10.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{A}(z,t)=\operatorname{sgn}t+O(\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1-2\epsilon_{0}}),

(10.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}\mathcal{A}(z,t)\ll_{n}\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n+1},\qquad\text{ for any integer }n\geq 2.

Proof.

By applying
\partial/\partial t
to (
10.9
), we get

\displaystyle\sinh(\mathcal{A}(z,t))\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{A}(z,t)=\sinh(t)-\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-t}.

Note that

\displaystyle\sinh(\mathcal{A}(z,t))=\sqrt{\left(\cosh(t)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-t}\right)^{2}-1}=\sqrt{\sinh^{2}(t)+O(\lambda^{-1})}=|\sinh(t)|(1+O(\lambda^{-1}\sinh^{-2}(t))),

so

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{A}(z,t)=\operatorname{sgn}\left((\sinh(t)\right)+O(\lambda^{-1}\sinh^{-2}(t))=\operatorname{sgn}t+O(\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1-2\epsilon_{0}}),

where the last step is due to Lemma
10.7
.

To bound the higher derivatives, we note that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{A}(z,t)=\frac{\sinh(t)-\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-t}}{\sqrt{\left(\cosh(t)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-t}\right)^{2}-1}}

and for any
w\in\mathbb{C}
with
|w|=O(1)
the real part inside the square root is

\operatorname{Re}\left(\left(\cosh(w)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-w}\right)^{2}-1\right)=\operatorname{Re}\left(\sinh^{2}(w)+O(\lambda^{-1})\right)=\frac{\cosh(2\operatorname{Re}(w))\cos(2\operatorname{Im}(w))-1}{2}+O(\lambda^{-1})\\
=\sinh^{2}(\operatorname{Re}(w))+O(\operatorname{Im}(w)^{2})+O(\lambda^{-1}).

For
t\ll 1
given in the proposition, by Lemma
10.7
, we have
|t|\gg\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
. Therefore, for
C>0
and for any
w\in\mathbb{C}
with
|w-t|\leq C\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
, we have

\operatorname{Re}\left(\left(\cosh(w)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-w}\right)^{2}-1\right)=\sinh^{2}(\operatorname{Re}(w))+O((C\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}})^{2})+O(\lambda^{-1})\gg\beta^{-1+2\epsilon_{0}},

which holds when
C>0
is chosen to be sufficiently small. Thus,

\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\mathcal{A}(z,w)=\frac{\sinh(w)-\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-w}}{\sqrt{\left(\cosh(w)+\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}e^{-w}\right)^{2}-1}}

can be extended to be holomorphic (in
w
) on the ball of radius
C\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
centered at
t
. By applying Cauchy’s integral formula, with the bound

\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\mathcal{A}(z,w)-\operatorname{sgn}t=O(\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1-2\epsilon_{0}})
for
|w-t|\leq C\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
, we obtain, for any
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n+1}}{\partial t^{n+1}}\mathcal{A}(z,t)=\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathcal{A}(z,t)-\operatorname{sgn}t\right)\ll_{n}\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1-2\epsilon_{0}}(C\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}})^{-n},

which proves (
10.11
).
∎

10.2.3.
Proof of (
10.5
)

We first unfold
J_{2}(\phi)
by the inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
h_{\lambda}
to get

\displaystyle J_{2}(\phi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{2}(\phi,s)h_{\lambda}(s)d\nu(s),

with

\displaystyle J_{2}(\phi,s)=\iint_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}}\overline{b\phi(z_{1},t_{1})}b\phi(z_{2},t_{2})((b_{2}\varphi_{s})\circ a)(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}(z_{2}-z_{1}),t_{2}-t_{1})e^{-2t_{1}-2t_{2}}dz_{1}dz_{2}dt_{1}dt_{2}.

Since
\beta\geq\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}
and
h_{\lambda}(s)
rapidly decays away from
\lambda
, it suffices to prove the bound

(10.12)

\displaystyle J_{2}(\phi,s)\ll_{A,\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{-A}

for
|s-\lambda|\leq\beta/2
.
We apply asymptotics for the spherical function from
[
30
, Theorem 1.5]
.
There are functions
f_{\pm}\in C^{\infty}((0,3)\times\mathbb{R})
such that

(10.13)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}f_{\pm}(t,s)\ll_{n}t^{-n}(st)^{-1}

and

(10.14)

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}(a(t))=f_{+}(t,s)e^{ist}+f_{-}(t,s)e^{-ist}+O_{A}((st)^{-A})

for
t\in(0,3)
. Plug the formula (
10.14
) into the integral
J_{2}(\phi,s)
. Because of the support of
b_{2}
, the error term in (
10.14
) contributes
O_{A}(s^{-A})
, which may be ignored. We shall only consider the integral involving
f_{+}
, as the integral involving
f_{-}
is similar.
By expanding one
\phi
via the Fourier inversion and the changes of variables
z_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow z_{2}+z_{1},t_{2}\mapstochar\rightarrow t_{2}+t_{1}
, the integral we are considering is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\overline{b\phi(z_{1},t_{1})}\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi(z_{2}+z_{1},s_{2})e^{is_{2}t_{1}-4t_{1}}

(10.15)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(t_{2})b_{2}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2}))f_{+}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2}),s)\exp(is\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2})+is_{2}t_{2})dt_{2}\right.

(10.16)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\int_{-\infty}^{0}\chi(t_{2})b_{2}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2}))f_{+}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2}),s)\exp(is\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},t_{2})+is_{2}t_{2})dt_{2}\right)dz_{1}dz_{2}dt_{1}ds_{2}.

Here we denote by
\chi(t_{2})=b(z_{2}+z_{1},t_{2}+t_{1})e^{-2t_{2}}
and omit the variables
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1}
as the estimates for the derivatives of
\chi
with respect to
t_{2}
are uniform on
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1}
. If we replace
t_{2}
with
\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2}
, then we write the inner integral (
10.15
) in the form
\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha(t_{2})e^{i|s+s_{2}|^{1/2+\epsilon_{0}}f(t_{2})}dt_{2}
with

\displaystyle\alpha(t_{2})=\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\chi(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2})b_{2}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2}))f_{+}(\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2}),s),

\displaystyle f(t_{2})=\frac{s}{|s+s_{2}|^{1/2+\epsilon_{0}}}\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2})+\frac{s_{2}}{|s+s_{2}|^{1/2+\epsilon_{0}}}\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2}.

By (
10.10
) and the Fourier support assumption on
\phi
:
|s_{2}\pm\lambda|\geq\beta
, we have the following estimate for the derivative of the phase function
f(t_{2})
:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial t_{2}}\right|=|s+s_{2}|^{-1/2-\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\left|s(1+O(\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1-2\epsilon_{0}}))+s_{2}\right|=\left(\frac{|s+s_{2}|}{\beta}\right)^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}}+O(\beta^{-2\epsilon_{0}})\gg 1.

For any
n\geq 2
, (
10.11
) implies that,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}f}{\partial t_{2}^{n}}

\displaystyle\ll_{n}\frac{s}{|s+s_{2}|^{1/2+\epsilon_{0}}}\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n+1}(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}})^{n}\ll\beta^{-2\epsilon_{0}}\ll 1.

By (
10.13
), if
t\gg\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}
and
n\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t^{n}}b_{2}(t)f_{+}(t,s)\ll_{n}\lambda^{-1}\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n}=\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n+1}.

Moreover, by (
10.10
) and (
10.11
), for any
n\geq 1
, it may be seen that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t_{2}^{n}}\mathcal{A}(e^{-t_{1}}z_{2},\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}t_{2})\ll(1+\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n+1})(\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}})^{n}\ll\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\ll 1.

Therefore, by the chain rule to higher derivatives, for any
n\geq 0
we obtain that the
n
-th derivative of the amplitude function
\alpha(t_{2})
is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}\alpha}{\partial t_{2}^{n}}\ll_{n}\beta^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n+1}=\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n}.

Integrating by parts
n
times, or applying
[
34
, (4) p.331]
, gives that the inner integral (
10.15
), which can be written as the form of the oscillatory integral
\int_{0}^{\infty}\alpha(t_{2})e^{i|s+s_{2}|^{1/2+\epsilon_{0}}f(t_{2})}dt_{2}
, is bounded by

\displaystyle|s+s_{2}|^{-n(1/2+\epsilon_{0})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{2}}\frac{1}{\partial f/\partial t_{2}}\right)^{n}\alpha\right|dt_{2}\ll_{n}|s+s_{2}|^{-n(1/2+\epsilon_{0})}\lambda^{-1}(\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}})^{n}\beta^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}}\ll|s+s_{2}|^{-n\epsilon_{0}}.

In summary, the inner integral (
10.15
) is
\ll_{A,\epsilon_{0}}|s+s_{2}|^{-A}
. The second inner integral (
10.16
) can be bounded in the same way by replacing
s+s_{2}
with
s-s_{2}
and is
\ll_{A,\epsilon_{0}}|s-s_{2}|^{-A}
. Hence, with these estimates and the fact
|s\pm s_{2}|\gg\beta
, by applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the Plancherel theorem, we obtain (
10.12
).

10.3.
Bounds near the spectrum

We want to prove Proposition
10.2
via the method of arithmetic amplification.
As
\psi
and
b
are real-valued,
we can assume
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\supp(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)\subset\mathbb{C}\times[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
.
We shall need the following bounds for
J(\lambda,\phi,g)
.

Proposition 10.9
.

We suppose that
\lambda^{\epsilon^{\prime}}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{1/4}
,
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\|\phi\|_{2}=1
and
\supp(\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi)\subset\mathbb{C}\times[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
.
Let
g\in G_{0}
satisfying
d(g,e)\leq 1
.
If
1/8>\epsilon_{0}>0
and
d(g,MA)\geq\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
, then

J(\lambda,\phi,g)\ll_{\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon_{0},A}\lambda^{-A}.

Proof.

We can unfold the integral
J(\lambda,\phi,g)
by the Fourier inversion for
\phi
and inverse Harish-Chandra transform for
h_{\lambda}
, that is,

\displaystyle J(\lambda,\phi,g)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi(z_{1},s_{1}})\mathscr{F}_{t}\phi(z_{2},s_{2})J(s,s_{1},s_{2},z_{1},z_{2})h_{\lambda}(s)dz_{1}dz_{2}ds_{1}ds_{2}d\nu(s).

The integral
J(s,s_{1},s_{2},z_{1},z_{2})
is defined by

\displaystyle J(s,s_{1},s_{2},z_{1},z_{2})=\iint_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi(z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2})\exp(-is_{1}t_{1}+is_{2}t_{2})\varphi_{s}(a(-t_{1})n(-z_{1})gn(z_{2})a(t_{2}))dt_{1}dt_{2},

with
s_{1},s_{2}\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
,
and
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
defined by

\displaystyle\chi(z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2})=(2\pi)^{-2}b(z_{1},t_{1})b(z_{2},t_{2})e^{-2t_{1}-2t_{2}}.

By the rapid decay of
h_{\lambda}
away from
\lambda
, we may also assume
s\in[\lambda-\beta,\lambda+\beta]
.
From the fact that
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq\lambda^{-1/2}
, it may be seen that

\displaystyle d(n(-z_{1})gn(z_{2}),MA)\gg\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}.

Then the proposition follows from Proposition
6.11
applied to
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=0
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
10.2
.

We choose our amplifier
T_{v}
for
v\in\mathscr{Q}
to be the same as (
9.23
).
Let
N\geq 1
be a parameter to be chosen later. We define
\mathscr{Q}_{N}=\{v\in\mathscr{Q}|N/2\leq q_{v}\leq N\}
, and define

\mathcal{T}_{N}=\sum_{v\in\mathscr{Q}_{N}}T_{v}.

Then we have

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{T}_{N}\psi,b\phi\rangle|=\#\mathscr{Q}_{N}\cdot|\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle|\gg N^{1-\epsilon}|\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle|.

By Proposition
10.3
, it follows that

(10.17)

\displaystyle|\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle|^{2}\ll N^{-2+\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|.

Recall that we use
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subset G_{0}
to denote the compact subset consisting of
g\in G_{0}
with
d(g,e)\leq 1
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
.
Proposition
10.9
implies that we only need to consider the terms in (
10.17
) with

\displaystyle d\left(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},MA\right)<\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}.

Let
C>0
be the constant as in Proposition
8.2
.
We choose

\displaystyle X=(C^{-1}\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2})^{-1}=C\lambda^{1/2-\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{-1/2},

so then take
\delta=CX^{-1}=\lambda^{-1/2+\epsilon_{0}}\beta^{1/2}
.
We use the trivial bound

J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\ll 1
to obtain

(10.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},MA)<\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},MA)<\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)\right|.

We next expand
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
as a sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\subset\mathcal{O}}a_{\mathfrak{n}}1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}

for some constants
a_{\mathfrak{n}}
.
If we now suppose
N^{4}\leq X
, that is

\displaystyle N\leq C^{1/4}\lambda^{1/8-\epsilon_{0}/4}\beta^{-1/8},

then our choice of
X
and
\delta
means that we may apply Proposition
8.2
to show that for any
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
appearing in the expansion of
\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*}
, the term
1_{K(\mathfrak{n})}
makes a contribution
\ll_{\epsilon}X^{\epsilon}\ll\lambda^{\epsilon}
to the sum in the right hand side of (
10.18
),
and the sum of
a_{\mathfrak{n}}
’s with
\mathfrak{n}\neq\mathcal{O}
is
\ll 1
by (
9.27
).
Therefore, we only need to consider the term
\mathfrak{n}=\mathcal{O}
. Since
a_{\mathcal{O}}\ll N
by (
9.37
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbf{G}(F)\\
\gamma\in g_{0}\mathcal{D}_{0}g_{0}^{-1}\\
d(g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0},MA)<\delta\end{subarray}}\left|(\mathcal{T}_{N}*\mathcal{T}_{N}^{*})(\gamma)J(\lambda,\phi,g_{0}^{-1}\gamma g_{0})\right|\ll N+\lambda^{\epsilon}.

We obtain

\displaystyle|\langle\psi,b\phi\rangle|^{2}\ll N^{-2+\epsilon}(N+\lambda^{\epsilon}).

Choosing
N=C^{1/4}\lambda^{1/8-\epsilon_{0}/4}\beta^{-1/8}
gives the result.
∎
